# Consideration of funding proposals - Addendum VI

## Funding proposal package for FP226

### Summary

This addendum contains the following seven parts:

a) A funding proposal titled "Resilient Puna: Ecosystem based Adaptation for sustainable livelihoods and climate resilient communities in Peru";

b) No-objection letter issued by the national designated authority(ies) or focal point(s);

c) Environmental and social report(s) disclosure;

d) Secretary's assessment;

e) Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment;

f) Report of the accredited entity to the Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment; and

g) Gender documentation.

## Note to Accredited Entities on the use of the funding proposal template

• Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-reference to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc.

• Accreditations are valid for 5 years. Any changes to the project description or any other material provided in the funding proposal. Updates to the funding proposal and/or annexes must be reflected within this timeframe.

• The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not exceed 20 pages. Materials exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual service standard time frame.

• The recommended font is Arial, size 11.

• Under the GCF’s Information Disclosure Policy, project and programme funding proposals will be disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneously with the submission to the Board, subject to any confidentiality requirements. Please refer to Section G of the IIP for further details. The IDP Accredited Entities are asked to fill out information on disclosure in section G.

A.20. Executing Entity information  
The Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ) GmbH, besides being the Accredited Entity (AE) of the project, will also act as an Executing Entity (EE). GIZ is registered in Germany and is a joint venture between the German Federal Ministry for Economic Cooperation and Development (BMZ) and the Federal Ministry of Finance. It has offices in Argentina, Brazil, Chile, Colombia, Ecuador, Peru, Uruguay and Venezuela. The project will be implemented by GIZ in partnership with the National Service of Natural Protected Areas by the State of Amazonas (SINAP), a public institution of the state government of Amazonas, Brazil.

A.21. Executive summary (max. 750 words, approximately 1.3 pages)  
Climate change is leading to increased temperatures and more frequent extreme weather events in Peru. These changes are degrading the Puna ecosystems, (peatlands, wetlands, and grasslands), and the services they provide (provisioning and regulating). The Andean region of Peru (SHAP) has experienced a 1°C increase in temperature over the last century. Increased temperatures have contributed to glacier melting while fewer rain and lower snowfall have led to reduced water availability for agriculture. In addition, the Puna is heavily reliant on rainfall. These climate factors are lowering the livelihoods of approx. half a million people in SHAP. The Andean region has experienced an increase in temperature by 1°C over the last century, which has decreased 51 percent of Peru's glaciers. In addition, huge stocks of carbon stored in the Puna ecosystems could be released into the atmosphere.

The Andean Indigenous Peoples and Local Communities (PLCs) in the SHAP are characterized by their high vulnerability to climate change due to their dependence on natural resources for their livelihoods. Over many years, they have passed down from one generation to others their agricultural, water and ecosystem management practices. These include crop varieties such as potatoes and Andean grains, water management technologies, systems of terraces to use solar energy, and the use of local seeds. They also bring important food crops such as potatoes, quinoa and other highly nutritious high altitude crops. However, these communities are increasingly facing climate stress; nowadays Andean population find the increasing severity and unpredictability of extreme weather events a major challenge. The PLCs in SHAP have limited access to information on their vulnerabilities such as low levels of development, dependence on subsistence agriculture and insufficient practices for adaptation to climate change.

The GCF financial contribution means the project will be implemented under Public Policy or Private Finance; in addition to the GCF contribution (i.e., GCF financial resources requested by the Accredited Entity) to implement the project the programme described in the Action Plan. Direct beneficiaries include individuals who will receive a targeted support from the GCF-funded intervention and is a measurable reduction of vulnerability to climate change. Indirect beneficiaries are the communities that will benefit from the implementation of the project, however not in the region. It includes those that will be supported by MIGA/CGS for financing projects, mobilized by the average number members of the PLCs. The GCF-funded intervention will support the development and implementation of a climate change adaptation plan to develop site-specific climate diagnostics and preparation of participatory intervention plans; and it DII officials that will receive capacity building in order to implement the project. It is expected that the project will be implemented by 2035.

The project will be measured against the baseline set with the GCF-funded interventions but will benefit from new generation of the implementation of the project, which will be implemented by SINAP and GIZ. For more information see Annex 23.

knowledgeably, currently young people are abandoning ancient practices, resulting in insufficient means and capacity to develop alternatives or adopt climate resilient livelihoods. The Peruvian Ministry of Agricultural Development (MIDAGRI) has identified the need for a participatory approach that involves the direct participation of Andean Indigenous Peoples and Local Communities to build their own resilience to climate change impacts. This project aims at strengthening the capacities of Andean communities in Peru to improve the Puna ecosystems management and ensure the continuous provision of their services such as water, pasture, timber, and other non-timber forest products throughout the year-round dry season. Therefore, the project participatory approach consists in providing technical assistance and setting up a participatory process that will allow Andean communities to design and implement an Ecosystems Services Approach (ESA) for climate change adaptation.

The project is being implemented by the Peruvian Ministry of Agricultural Development (MIDAGRI), promoting Climate Resilient Value Chains (CRVC) led by IRLCs at local scale via the alignment and leveraging of existing public and private sector initiatives. The project will also provide technical assistance to MIDAGRI programs through technical assistance and set the financing structure to sustain microfinancing.

At the local level, experiences with EIA implementation and the facilitation of community participation in decision-making processes have been identified as key factors for successful projects. Local communities will receive direct financial and technical support to design, implement and monitor EIA measures that are aligned with their own development plans. The project will also contribute to boost the local economy for the continued protection of the Puna ecosystems. In doing so, at the national level the project will work in close collaboration with MIDAGRI and other relevant actors, including those that boast the local economy for the continued protection of the Puna ecosystems.

The project recognizes the importance of a participatory approach to address gender inequality from its inception into all its programs (Agrupas: Senu Azul, INIA, Agrodes and SERFOR), and improve the coordination between sectoral M&E systems. The project will also work in close collaboration with MIDAGRI with its mandate for actions within Natural Protected Areas (NPAs). To assess progress on agricultural and water management systems and promote coordination between sectoral M&E systems.

The project will support MIDAGRI in strengthening the capacities of Andean communities to design, implement and monitor their NGOs into the System for Monitoring Adaptation and Mitigation Measures (SMAM) managed by Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources (MARN). The SMAM is a tool developed by the Directorate of Climate Change and Desalinization (DCCCD) of MARN and in coordination with the General Directorate of Forests (DGFOREST) of MIDAGRI. The project will also work closely with DGFOREST.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 4

## By implementing investments on the ground (through component 1), the aim is to maintain or improve the provision of pure ecosystem services for climate resilience of Andean Indigenous Peoples and Local Communities, while also ensuring that these communities are not further dependent on and impacting those ecosystems. This component will provide financial and technical support to indigenous peoples and local communities in order to implement their own projects on the ground. To ensure IPCs investment ownership and enhance replication potential, the component will have a strong focus on capacity building and knowledge sharing.

### 4. By implementing investments on the ground (through component 1), the aim is to maintain or improve the provision of pure ecosystem services for climate resilience of Andean Indigenous Peoples and Local Communities, while also ensuring that these communities are not further dependent on and impacting those ecosystems. This component will provide financial and technical support to indigenous peoples and local communities in order to implement their own projects on the ground.

#### 4.1. The project will be implemented through a combination of Ecosystem Services (ES) and Climate Resilient Value Chains (CRVC). ES are pre-identified and validated with IPCs during stakeholder consultations phase. EPA approach draws on community knowledge, local practices and traditional ecological knowledge to identify the most appropriate measures for each landscape. The project will support communities in deciding which combination of ESA measures and Climate Resilient Value Chains will be planned, implemented and monitored. This will be done through a participatory process involving all stakeholders facilitated by the EES, and in some cases by local partners (Civil Society Organizations (CSOs), IPCs in the SADGIP, indigenous peoples and local communities). The project will also support the development of other EbK-related measures, namely improved livelihoods in ecosystems, maintained carbon stocks and sustainable land use. In addition, a knowledge exchange platform will be established to facilitate exchanges between communities' exchanges to nurture dialogue will be implemented to co-produce knowledge and foster local innovation. This will be done through a participatory process involving all stakeholders facilitated by the EES.

Furthermore through the last component 3 the project will promote integrated gender responsive landscape planning, governance platforms and policy improvement and coordination, fostering dialogue and partnerships between governments, rural communities, producer organizations, watershed committees, and MIDAGRI extension personnel. These platforms for knowledge exchange, dialogue, coordination and consensus-building will be fostered according to local needs and priorities. ### 7. Due to climate change, sustainable management of 23.9 ha of Puna ecosystems, benefiting directly 60,715 people and indirectly another 2,011,856 people. The mitigation co-benefit is estimated at US$45,000 per year (US$2.00 per person). The water resources in the Puna are not affected by climate change (conservation) will not recomplement the water from rainfall or glacial melting in the same magnitude but they will capture and store more water in the soil and aquifers, which will be available for agriculture, livestock and drinking water.

### 8. A cooperation agreement between the Government of Chile (Ministry of Environment and Forests - MEF) with MIDAGRI as the leading political partner. Profonrural, the NGO Instituto de Montaña and GIZ Peru will act as Technical Partners. The National Fund for the Conservation of Protected Areas by the State (SEINANP) will act as Executive Entity that will contribute with financing to achieve the objectives of the project. The project will be implemented in 10 municipalities of different forms of organization (i.e., communities, producer associations and cooperatives) in the process of formulation and implementation of climate change adaptation strategies, including planning, presenting and implementing of climate-resilient Local initiatives financed through the Puna Facility.

*Please note that the GIZ working definition for gender response follows the OECD Gender Equality Continuum (2005). A gender responsive approach is one which takes into account the different needs and requirements of women and men in project activities but goes a step further in responding to gender specific needs and/or requirements, or by considering gender dimensions in all aspects of project design, implementation and evaluation. This includes ensuring that all project activities are designed with measures regardless of their gender (i.e., training or strategies developed that consider gendered needs, preferences, and constraints).*

B. PROJECT-PROGRAMME INFORMATION

### B.1 Context (max. 1000 words, approximately two pages)

#### B.1.1 Socio-economic context:

* Peru is considered an upper-middle-income economy, but inequality is high, and poverty is concentrated among rural populations.
* More than 80% of farmers practice subsistence, rainfed agriculture, and changes in precipitation are increasing competition over water resources.
* Peru has a large number of small-scale producers who are vulnerable to climate change impacts on their livelihoods due to increasing temperatures. Peru is susceptible to natural disasters including floods, droughts, and landslides.
* The effects of climate change will be amplified by increased climate change variability and weather. Combined with ongoing problems such as agricultural overexploitation, this could have a significant impact on the food security of small-scale farmers in Peru's development.

#### B.1.2 Population:

* Peru has officially recorded more than 33 million people in 2021, of whom 50.5% are women.
* The country’s annual population growth decreased to 1.2%, in comparison to 1.9% (World Bank, 2022).
* Most of the population is concentrated in the central and southern regions of Peru, with Lima accounting for about 43% of the total population; Cusco with 58% of the Peruvian population, the Arequipa with 28% of the population, and the Amazon with 14% of the population.
* The Quechua people are the largest ethnic group in Peru. They represent about 60% of the total population in the Andes Mountain range (about 60% of the total population).
* The indigenous peoples of Peru represent about 25.7% of the total population, with the majority living in the Amazon region.
* The Quechua, followed by the Aymara, are a large proportion of the indigenous people who live in the Andean highlands and lowlands.
* The Afro-Peruvian population is concentrated on the coast and in some areas of the central highlands. These communities will be focused on the Andes region where most of the population belongs to the Quechua Indigenous Peoples Group.

#### B.1.3 Agriculture:

* The agricultural sector employs 29% of the total population in Peru (CEPAL, 2022), and 56% of the rural population.
* Women are responsible for more than half of all agricultural activities in Peru, especially in small-scale farming.
* Besides the economic benefits, the agricultural sector also contributes to the country’s food security, as well as its cultural identity. However, the sector is still underdeveloped and requires significant investment and expenditure and in the sector is too low, mainly due to a lack of interest from policy makers (World Bank, 2019).
* The Andes region has been identified by FAO as one of the most important areas for climate change adaptation.
* The target region of the project intervention lies in the Southern High Andes area (SHAIP): Puno, Cusco, Arequipa, Ayacucho, and Huancavelica. This area is characterized by high altitudes, low temperatures, and a dry climate, which makes it an important hot spot for conservation priorities worldwide (Myers et al., 2000). This area is located above 3500 m.a.s.l. and the ecosystem is dominated by Andean grasslands and shrublands. The region has been described as one of the last relics of high Andean forests also known as “grasslands hold significant importance as they play a vital role in safeguarding biodiversity, water resources, and soil conservation” (Myers et al., 2000). These areas are rich in wetlands and grasslands capture rainwater and retain it for some period. Local communities rely on Puno lakes for their livelihoods.
* The main economic activities in the SHAIP are mixed husbandry and agriculture for local subsistence on both commercial and small-scale farms, with a focus on livestock (sheep, alpaca, llama) and crops (potatoes, quinoa, maize, barley, lama, and corn), typically raised in mixed systems with sheep, while the vicuña is a wild species. In the Andean region, there are also cattle farming activities that are present.
* The Feasibility Study for more details.

#### B.1.4 Agricultural production:

* Peru is generally characterized by small-scale farms focused on subsistence production and low-complexity value chains tied to local markets. These farming systems combine crops with livestock (sheep, alpaca, llama) and sometimes other animals such as cattle or poultry.
* Puna ecosystems contribute with a substantial amount of meat, fiber, and wool for the country’s economy. The most important species are sheep, alpaca, llama, vicuña, and cattle.
* Peru is also one of the largest producers of Andean grain, especially quinoa. Peru is also the largest producer of native potato in Latin America and exported

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE B

## 15. From the total agricultural units in Peru, 82 percent correspond to farms with equal or less than five (5) hectares, of which 64 percent are located in the highlands and 36 percent in the lowlands. The majority of these smallholder farmers live in the departments of Cajamarca, Puno and Cusco (INEI, 2012). Thus, the majority of farms in the SHAP are less than five hectares.

## 14. Forests in Peru cover approximately 35% of its territory, with a great diversity of species and ecosystems. These environments differ in terms of altitude, soil quality, water availability, and climate.

### Forestry

In the Andes, there is a wide variety of trees and shrubs, which are used by households, leading many farmers to seek additional income through off-farm activities. Smallholder farmers in the highlands region make use of the forest for firewood, timber, and other non-timber forest products (NTFPs). These NTFPs include traditional tubers like native potatoes, maniocs, dulceco, oca, and maca; are grown using traditional agricultural practices. In the lowland areas, they grow a variety of crops such as maize, beans, yuca, rice, and maize along with guinea pigs, llamas, turkeys, and berries. In the inter Andean valleys, various types of trees are used for firewood.

### Land tenure

In the high Andes generally appears to be dominated by private regime, in average 48% of the agricultural land is owned by individuals or families (INEI, 2012). However, in the lowlands and inter-Andean valleys, it is predominantly communal regime. In the Amazon region, it is a mixed regime (private and communal), followed by communal land regime; for 48% of agricultural units in average (variation from 1 to 70 %) according to the data collected during the project (INEI, 2012). Traditional Andean communities living in the highlands tend to combine private and collective regimes as an alternative strategy to secure their land tenure. In the lowlands and inter-Andean valleys, they use a mixed regime of private and communal land tenure. The Amazon region is characterized by a limited access to land, which is conditioned upon their membership in a community and their relationship with a territory.

### Population

In SHAP there are mainly Andean Indigenous Peoples and Local Communities (ILCs). In the target area of the project, 60% of the population living within the project target area have Quechua as their mother tongue while 39.5% speak Aymara. The majority of these communities are located in the highlands region; however, also in the inter-Andean valleys and Amazon regions there are communities that speak Quechua or Aymara. Nonetheless, they have other forms of organization and live in other places such as villages, hamlets, among others. In addition to the Quechua and Aymara languages, there is a wide variety of indigenous languages spoken by these communities. They also form associations, cooperatives, community enterprises and organizations by the need to manage local development projects.

### Andean Indigenous Peoples

Andean Indigenous Peoples can be identified as Indigenous or Original Peoples according to their historical links with the territory they traditionally use or occupy; c) Their own social institutions and customs; d) Collective ownership of land and natural resources; e) The right to self-determination, which includes the right to decide indigenous languages. These rights do not alter after their nature or their collective rights.

### Andean Indigenous Peoples’ Knowledge

Andean Indigenous Peoples have a vast knowledge about the environment in which they live and how they learned from their ancestors. For instance, they still use ancient techniques to storage water into micro water reservoirs named olleras, which are used for irrigation of crops; or they use traditional methods such as terraces and canals to divert rivers descending from highlands area into irrigate footpaths etc. This ancestral knowledge is accumulated, and transmitted through generations. The Andean Indigenous Peoples’ Knowledge (AIK) is a key element that is central to climate change adaptation. During their agricultural and livestock activities, they also combine collective conservation practices with individual ones; for instance, they use the same techniques of management of the livestock. They perform animal fertility rites involving singing, dancing and festivity. Also, they have a series of festivals such as the “chakana” (the Andean calendar), the “killa” (a ritual dance) or the “pachamama ditches”. Native fibers (alpacas, llama, vicuña wool) and ancestral weaving traditions continue to occupy

A central concept not only in the Andean culture but also in the social and economic aspects since many communities depend on camellia wool for their livelihoods. In the pre-colonial times, Andean worldview was represented by the Ayllu concept, which is a system of social organization based on kinship ties. The members of an ayllu share some reciprocal norms and rituals with respect to humans, animals, rocks, spirits, mountains, lakes, rivers, pastures, crops. The main objective of ayllus was to give in guide to the people in their daily life, work, and social values, and to ensure that the natural resources are used sustainably. The concept of the Ayllu concept can still be found in some communities of Cusco, Apurimac and Puno. These cultural and spiritual values of Andean people are still very strong today. In addition to these cultural values, Andean people also have a deep understanding of social values, and social cohesion at landscape level (I). IPCCs play vital role in the management of their ecosystems (IFAD, 2015). The Andes region is characterized by high biodiversity, but it is also vulnerable to desertification, erosion, high rates of urban migration among others. Additionally, gender traditional norms in the Andes often prevent women from participating in decision-making processes and they are not always recognized from the same opportunities and recognition, despite their deep knowledge of agriculture, water and ecosystems.

## 8.1.2 Climate Change context (historical climate trends and future scenarios)

Climate change and unsustainable management of productive practices are degrading the Puna ecosystems, particularly in the southern part of the region. The main drivers of degradation include: over-provision and regulation of water provision; fodder, food, and fiber; nutrient and carbon regulation. Fewer rains and longer drought periods have contributed to a decrease in the availability of water resources for livestock grazing and crop production. In addition, millions of downwinders. B.1.3 Climate risks and vulnerability of the SGRP ecosystems

31. Glacier retreat. Increasing temperatures lead to the shrinking of glaciers. Peru is home to 71% of the world's tropical glaciers; however, due to higher temperatures and the increased absorption of solar radiation, the country has already lost 51% of its glaciers in the last 70 years. Accelerated glacier melting is possibly the strongest evidence of climate change in the Andes. The National Research Institute on Glaciers and Mountain Ecosystems (INAGEM) reports that the Peruvian Andes have lost more than half of their ice volume since the mid-1980s, with some glaciers already crossing peak flow. Once the glaciers feeding these watersheds disappear, dry season average discharge may decrease by 50% or more. The disappearance of glaciers in the Cordillera Blanca and Huayhuash has already affected local livelihoods due to the scarcity of water resources. This is also threatening sacred festivals and religious ceremonies that are held at altitudes where these glaciers once existed, as well as the spiritual beliefs of individuals who make a pilgrimage to the sacred Colca Puncu Pukara.

32. Frequent extreme weather events. The frequency of hot days trends return a general decrease over the last three decades, especially at higher altitude in A and SE (Figure 31). Trends for several extreme weather indices are also negative. For example, the number of days with maximum temperature above 30°C has decreased by more than half since 2005. The frequency of hot days in frontiers corresponds to trends of minimum temperature with low magnitudes and few significant signals. Some studies have shown that the occurrence of extreme weather events is related to changes in the amplitude of minimum temperature cycle (García, Rass, Jacobson, & Michel, 2007). Regionally, differences in the occurrence of hot days are more evident at higher altitudes where the frequency has considerably reduced due to the boost

The impact of higher temperatures and less precipitation will have an important effect on species distribution and abundance. The study found that the mean temperature increase for tropical Andes located between 900m-3400m above sea-level, the authors concluded that due to the increase in temperature, there is a decrease in the number of suitable habitats for some species (e.g., Eriocaulaceae). but with significant agricultural significance. Wheat yields in the SW and A subregions are also likely to significantly decrease by 2040 (from 1285 to 935 kg/ha) with statistical confidence in the RCP4.8 scenario using the CSM1 model. Likewise, the crop yield of maize is expected to decrease from 670 to 450 kg/ha and that of soybeans from 2000 to 1500 kg/ha.

The projected changes in precipitation are likely to have a significant impact on agricultural production systems, particularly for smallholders who own livestock and use traditional farming practices, relying on rainfed crops and limited irrigation (Angulo et al., 2013). The reduction of the water availability is expected to be more severe during the dry season when drought was 69% and 65%, on most. Most of the products from this region are used for self-consumption (Lusa, 2008).

In the case of livestock, the reduction in the carrying capacity of ecosystems in the high Andes territories into which the project area is inserted will be significant. The estimated decrease in the number of cattle per hectare was estimated in the context of the Ministry of Agriculture Development and Irrigation's Climate Risk Analysis and Adaptation Strategy (MADIRIG, 2012). The scarcity of water and limited grazing areas are further reducing livestock productivity. Extreme weather events such as droughts and floods will also exacerbate the vulnerability of subsistence farmers who heavily rely on these resources. A detailed climate change impact study was carried out in the project area (see Annex 3).

Impact on population: In principle, climate change affects all people – but to varying degrees in addition to resource availability, it is important to consider how the system will be affected by the changes and what the system will also depend to a relevant extent on gender-conforming behaviour and gender-inequitable distribution of resources. This means that the impact of climate change can vary significantly depending on the context.

The study carried out in the project area (see Annex 3) shows that the vulnerability of specific groups is applicable to specific highland communities use migrate to anticipate or react to hazards. In terms of temperature-related risks, the most vulnerable are children and young people who are more exposed than adults to heat waves, especially young people, mostly men due to frost and droughts. Crespinaga et al., 2009) findings show that after an average of 15 years of migration, women and girls are still behind in terms of education and social inclusion. Women look for work as vendors, weavers and shop owners to smooth out income losses in places like Lima and other cities, while men continue to be the main breadwinners. The study also shows that young people, especially women and girls and the elderly stay behind. According to a survey carried out in the Apurimac region, in the last five years, 10% of the population has migrated from the project area (see Annex 3). This is higher than the proportion of Peru (7%) with the highest immigration according to the proportion of cases (Lusa, 2008).

The CI will be used as an indicator for the evaluation of the potential to generate positive impacts on the water availability at the district level. For example, the EEA Water Management System in the project area has been designed with the objective of increasing the spring flow due to infiltration produced by the wetland system movement and decrease the base flows. ### B.1.5 Priorities for climate change adaptation in ecosystems and agriculture

The most relevant impacts of climate variability and change on the Puna ecosystems and agricultural value chains are: (i) increased temperatures, which can lead to changes in phenology, reduced yields, a decrease in crop quality, increased incidence of pests and diseases; and loss in resilience to external shocks; (ii) reduced water availability; (iii) increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events.

At present, the priority value chains suffer significant impacts due to climate change, which translate into the following sectoral priorities:

#### Andean crops

The main effects of climate change on potato production are complex, as plant growth and yield are related to temperature. Potato cultivation, in order to maintain its nutritional properties, requires cold temperatures. Many of the Puna areas where potatoes are cultivated have experienced a significant increase in mean annual air temperature for their crops (Gambino et al., 2014; Shaw and Kingdonson (2013) found that potato farmers in the region have raised their crop altitudes by an average of about 50 m since the mid-1980s. The altitude at which potatoes are grown has risen almost 500 m above those reported 38 years ago. In the agro-ecological zones of the Puna, in addition to the increase in temperature and decrease in precipitation, there is also an increase in extreme weather risk, such as frosts, droughts and hailstorms that generate risks of production losses and loss of genetic diversity of potatoes. The average yield per hectare has decreased by 10% since 2003/2004 (the last year with data) compared to potato sowings (2002/2003 season) with respect to the average accumulated sowings up to October of the last five seasons. The decrease in yield is due, among other factors, to the increase in temperature and the change by increasing areas of potato cultivation in natural grassland areas in Puna area, due to the increased population pressure on land resources. This has led to a reduction in the quality of potatoes produced, which are now similar to tinto dark and seca seeds.

Other crops that make it more resilient to climate change (Balaskian et al., 2022; Hinojosa, et al., 2018) if they other crops, nevertheless quinoa is not immune to the negative impacts of climate change. The main effects are: (i) increased temperatures and reduced precipitation, which make it no longer have the necessary conditions for sustained production. As temperatures rise and exceed the crop's optimal temperature range, yields decrease; (ii) increase in extreme weather events such as frosts, hailstorms or a reduction of 112°C in maturation and 189 kJ per kg in yield. Additionally, increased rainfall by 300 mm leads to an increase in pests and diseases that affect the quality of the crop; (iii) water scarcity is another challenge that quinoa producers face, causing water scarcity for irrigation. In Ayacu, Puno, the entire quinoa harvest was lost due to lack of water.

#### South American Camellias

Alpaca and Vicuña: Increasing temperatures and reduced rainfall have resulted in diminished pastures and a higher incidence of diseases that affect these animals and contribute to higher mortality rates among herds. This situation is likely to negatively impact viable alpaca populations (Kastner and Lichtenstein, 2018). Furthermore, climate change has affected the distribution of vicuñas, which are more sensitive than alpacas to changes in temperature. Vicuñas are forced to cover larger areas with lower quality pastures and migrate to higher elevations where better pasture conditions exist (Kastner et al., 2019). This migration can lead to increased competition for resources among both alpaca communities and the communities that rear them. A primary challenge currently faced by South American camellias is the lack of infrastructure, such as water supply systems, which limits production for feeding the herd. Consequently, they are incurring additional expenses that were not present before.

Sustainable management of South American camellias is therefore an ancestral activity and in several cases are still practiced with traditional techniques. However, it has been observed that the productivity levels achieved by South American camellias are more efficient grain. There is a need to ensure long-term sustainability of this livestock production system through the development of sustainable production systems based on agroecological models for camel breeding and fiber production. This includes improving the management and conservation of water resources, developing new technologies that improve productivity in the context of climate change, adapting to manufacturing, developing a stronger quality control protocol, promoting the sale of the direct commercialization on national and international market. Other productive practices are e.g., crop rotation, selection of adapted crop varieties, use of organic fertilizers, and the conservation and restoration of key ecosystem services (food production, soil fertility, water regulation).

Adaptation: The project will work on complementary value chain initiatives traditional Andean crops, breeding of small animal (guinea pigs), community based tourism and traditional handicrafts. Traditional crops include: maize, potatoes, quinoa, amaranth, beans, wheat, barley, sorghum, rice, sweet potato, yuca, banana and (v) Maphua. Their vulnerabilities are related to those explained for the main crops (quinoa and potato), as well as to the following:

* **Increased frequency of extreme weather events:** This is a general vulnerability in the region, but most farm rural people in the Peruvian Andes hold diversified, low input cropping systems and intercropping as an adaptation strategy.

* **Ecosystems based Adaptation (EBA) supports the sustainable use of resources and diversification of land and forest uses.** EBA is a key component of the project and will be implemented at both community level on a local scale but ideally at landscape scale[1]. ## B

### Many core elements and principles of the EBA approach coincide with traditional knowledge, management practices, technologies, and values of High Andean communities.

There are traditional communal systems for water management in the region.

The management of nucal reservoirs, known as qochas, to store rainwater for human use and animal consumption and prevent soil loss through erosion by constructing trenches following the contour of the land, thus capturing runoff water and allowing it to infiltrate into the ground. Such infrastructure is complemented with ancient diversion canals conducting streamflow to irrigate terraces from which the water flows down.

Moreover, the Southern Andes are well known for their terraced landscapes, dating back to pre-Inca periods. Terraces were built on steep slopes in order to increase the amount of land available for agriculture and grazing by the surface of land that can be used for agriculture or animal grazing.

Terraces are also used as a means of soil conservation through the establishment of common exclusion areas, rotational grazing, and restoration allows ecosystems to recover and continue supplying services such as water regulation and flood control.

These ancient technologies have been an integral part of traditional livelihoods in the SHARP for centuries, shaping agriculture and land use practices. They are not only important for their ecological functions but also for their social infrastructure themselves but on the social network behind them, which demands a high degree of social cohesion to maintain these systems over time.

Proven solutions with potential to be up-scaled:

The protection of grasslands and wetlands through fencing and the establishment of exclusion areas is an effective way to protect water resources. (2022) demonstrated the contribution of ecosystems (peatlands, grasslands, and Polypepis forests to water regulation services.

### B.1 Prioritized Project Areas
Due to the limited available resources and limited project funds, areas where interventions could have the greatest potential were prioritized in a two-stage process. In the first stage, the scope of analysis was defined according to a set of criteria that included vulnerability (as measured by the number of inhabitants per km2), land use/land cover, area between 2,800 and 3,500 m.a.s.l., presence of Key Pure Ecosystems (peatlands, grasslands and wetlands), distance to designated protected areas, and proximity to urban centers. The second stage was based on the vulnerability index with high or very high risks of droughts and high climate vulnerability. In the second stage, priority was given to districts where the population is greater than 10,000 inhabitants per km2 and where there are no other important social factors. As a result of the first stage, 91 districts in the regions of Arequipa, Cusco, Ayacucho, and Puno were prioritized for interventions with capacity building activities (45 districts) or both capacity building and project areas (46 districts). The total area covered by these priority districts is 4,116,475 hectares and capacity building activities, while 33 districts will only benefit of capacity building activities. Four additional districts were prioritized for interventions in the regions of Cusco, Ayacucho, Arequipa, and Puno. These areas are the Cochas in Lima region, the Landscape Reserve of Cochas, the National Reserve of Salinas y Aguada Blanca in Arequipa Region, and the National Reserve of Salkantay in Cusco Region. The total area covered by these districts is 105,472 hectares.

### Explanation of the prioritization methodology
A detailed explanation of the prioritization methodology and process is included in Annex 18b "Explanation of the prioritization methodology and process". It should be noted that 63% of the population are Quechua speakers and the other 31% Spanish speakers (National Census, 2017). As depicted in the Map of Indigenous Peoples in Peru (Quechua in light pink), the majority of these districts are located in the Andean region.

### Note
In 2012, 85% were Quechua speakers and 17% Spanish speaker within the project area of the project (CENKRO, 2012).

### B.1.7 Direct beneficiaries of the project

61. The direct beneficiaries are primarily Andean Indigenous Peoples and Local Communities from vulnerable rural regions in the high Andean regions (for detailed beneficiary numbers, refer to section 6.1.3). In the region, these communities are organized into various types of organizations such as cooperatives, associations, and enterprises. These organizations are regarded as potential recipients of funding from the Green Climate Fund.

62. Local communities are defined in most cases as peasant communities which are the most widespread and important category in the region, as they represent a strong part of the Andean culture as they are descendants of Quechua and Aymara peoples. These communities have been historically recognized by the government and protected by Peru's Political Constitution, with rights over natural resources and the land.

63. The term "peasant community" is used in this project to include a wider range of activities in which they engage, as peasant communities are predominantly focused on agricultural activities.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 22

B

## Community Enterprises: These are Local communities themselves that, using their legal status, organise and manage resources for the benefit of all members of the community. They can be either producer or service enterprises, to ensure the well-being of their members and contribute to the development of the community as a whole.

### Producers' Associations: Represent a stable organisation of natural or legal persons, both local through a cooperative association or a producer association, or national, such as an agricultural producers' association. The production of producers associations according to a specific economic activity, for example, farmers associations, apiece exporters' association, etc., is the main objective of these associations.

### Irrigation: Irrigation has promoted producer association, which comprises a process of voluntary grouping of individuals, organized in order to improve their living conditions and to ensure the development of their territory. The aim is to optimize the utilization of production resources, enhance earnings through associative marketing efforts, and establish an efficient organization for the management of irrigation.

### Cooperatives: An agricultural cooperative is a society of persons that can also be the same members of a producer association. It is a legal entity with its own assets and liabilities, which is a jointly owned and democratically controlled company that complies with cooperative principles. The agricultural cooperative provides services to its members in accordance with their needs and the objectives of the cooperative activity that they carry out. Among the services that the cooperative can provide to its members are the supply of inputs, credit, marketing, technical assistance, training, etc., as well as any other related or complementary service that contributes to the realization of its objective. The agricultural cooperative association is a legal entity with its own assets and liabilities, which is a jointly owned and democratically controlled company that complies with cooperative principles.

### Micro and Small Enterprises (MSEs): Are an economic unit constituted by a natural or legal person, under any form of organization, who carries out production activities in order to supply the market with goods or services. According to the Legislative Degree 1086, micro enterprises have from 1 to 10 employees and annual sales below 5 million pesos; small enterprises have from 1 to 100 employees and annual sales up to 17 million taxable tax units.

### B.1 Related ongoing and forthcoming projects with synergies

The table below lists the projects that are currently being implemented, ongoing and forthcoming interventions in the SGP. It is more extended list and can be found in the Feasibility Study (Annex 2) section 7.1.6 Gaps and opportunities.

### Among the projects described below, it is worth noting some of them which will have some synergies:

For example, the project "Promoting Climate Change Adaptation through an Ecosystem-based Adaptation programme" implemented by UNDP and IUCN from 2011-2015 in Uganda, Nepal and Peru. This project has developed a number of tools for planning and monitoring, as well as decision-making tools for EPA planning and monitoring; implement pilot measures focused on water resources management, land use planning, and forest conservation.

The project "Promoting Climate Change Adaptation through an Ecosystem-based Adaptation programme" implemented by UNDP and IUCN from 2011-2015 in Uganda, Nepal and Peru. This project has developed a number of tools for planning and monitoring, as well as decision-making tools for EPA planning and monitoring; implement pilot measures focused on water resources management, land use planning, and forest conservation.

The Puna Facility will also complement and benefit from the experience of the Six Phase of SGP's Small Grants Programme (SGP) in the Andes region. The SGP has been implemented by UNDP and IUCN since 2013-2018 in the regions of Puno, Tacna and Cusco. The Puna Facility will fund EMA measures and Climate Resilient Value Chain projects that are complementary to the SGP's Small Grants Programme (SGP) in the Andes region. For example, development one such as the GEF's SGP. The Puna Facility will start its call for proposals at the beginning of 2019.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 23

## Project characteristics

- **Title:** Seventh Operational Round: Phase II of the GEF Small Grants Programme in Peru
- **Budget:** USD 1,375,817 (co-financing required: USD 469,943)
- **Project period:** 2018–2023
- **Executing Agency:** IUCN, UNDP

### Objectives and results

- Strengthen landscape, regional, and national level governance for the implementation of EEA measures to better land use planning and management of the region's wetlands.
- Enhance ecosystem services with climate mitigation and adaptation results in the Amazon with the Inter-American Development Bank as an additional partner.
- Promote sustainable forest management, as well as the revitalization of degraded landscapes through a landscape approach.
- Strengthen community-based EEA measures at the local level.

### Project synergies

- Both programmes fund GEF investments to enhance ecosystem services with agriculture and forestry in the Southern Andes in Peru through small grants for local communities and private sector tenants and sustainable development (LBS).

### Project principles

This project serves as a "gap" for the GEF project portfolio, which is currently focused on the implementation of EEA measures for better land use planning and management. The project will be implemented under Activity 2.1 of the UNDP/UNCCD Facility, similar to the manner in which the UNDP/UNCCD Facility has been used as a "gap" for other GEF projects such as strengthening community-based EEA measures and monitoring impacts.

### Project partners

- **Title:** Seventh Operational Round: Phase II of the GEF Small Grants Programme in Peru
- **Budget:** USD 1,375,817 (co-financing required: USD 469,943)
- **Project period:** 2018–2023
- **Executing Agency:** IUCN, UNDP

### Project synergies

Both programmes fund GEF investments to enhance ecosystem services with agriculture and forestry in the Southern Andes in Peru through small grants for local communities and private sector tenants and sustainable development (LBS).

### Project principles

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 24

## B

### GEF Green Climate Fund
**Implementation period: 2021-2025**

#### Donor: GEF Agency UNDP

#### Title: Amazon Bio Eco-Bio Facility for the conservation and sustainable use of biodiversity and carbon mitigation outcomes by investing in eco bio-based businesses in Peru
**Budget:** $10,000,000 USD
**GEF contribution period:** 2023-2030
**Date:** 2020
#### Donor: GEF Agency PROFOINAPA

### Results:
The programme is in its Second Operational phase. The mid-term review for the project will be conducted by the UNDP and the World Bank, with the participation of relevant stakeholders called Strategic Landscape Partners (SLP). SLPs are groups that have a common interest in landscape adaptive management of landscapes, and are composed of government authorities, private sector (CPND, 2019).

### Objectives:
The Amazon Bio Eco-Bio Facility for the conservation and sustainable use of biodiversity and carbon mitigation outcomes by investing in eco bio-based businesses in Peru aims to conserve Peruvian forests in the Amazon, and reduce the negative impacts on the local economy. The project will support REDD+ infrastructure in Peru.

### Results:
This project is still under implementation.
The project has already achieved 10 million tons of CO2 with an estimated cost of $63 million USD. It also aims to provide technical and financial support for climate adaptation activities in Peru.

Both facilities are managed by Proforest, which provides technical assistance on best practices and management through engineering services and training. The project will adapt to climate change impacts in the intervention area.

## B

### Goal:
The aim of the project is to increase the resilience of communities, ecosystems and livelihood in the High Andean region of Peru through the implementation of Ecosystem-Based Adaptation (EBA) measures, including CIVC, the establishment of public and private financing for investing in these measures and the support from multilateral climate funds.

### Goal Stakeholders:
- Public and private financing for investing in EBA measures.
- Communities and local governments.
- Local communities.
- Government agencies.
- Financial institutions.
- Multilateral climate funds.
- Private sector.
- Civil society organizations.
- Research and academic institutions.
- International organizations.
- National and international financial institutions.

### Objectives:
1. The use of ecosystem services to enhance the resilience of communities, ecosystems and livelihood in the High Andean region of Peru through the implementation of EBA measures.
2. Financial resources for the establishment of public and private financing for investing in these measures.
3. Multilateral climate funds support for the implementation of EBA measures.

### Outputs:
1.1 Strengthen the capacities for territorial management of ecosystems and ecosystem services through CIVC.
1.2 Support the processing of information to improve decision-making on the use of ecosystems and ecosystem services.
1.3 Develop the capacities for territorial management of ecosystems and ecosystem services through CIVC.
1.4 Support the processing of information to improve decision-making on the use of ecosystems and ecosystem services.

### Results:
- Institutional framework for territorial management of ecosystems and ecosystem services through CIVC.
- Improved decision-making processes based on knowledge and practices related to the use of ecosystems and ecosystem services.
- Enhanced capacity building in local communities, government agencies, financial institutions, civil society organizations, research and academic institutions, international organizations, national and international financial institutions.

### 79. Goal:
The aim of the project is to increase the resilience of communities, ecosystems and livelihood in the High Andean region of Peru through the implementation of Ecosystem-Based Adaptation (EBA) measures, including CIVC, the establishment of public and private financing for investing in these measures and the support from multilateral climate funds.

- Public and private financing for investing

B 2.a.3. Economic barriers

I. Barriers that limit access to information and knowledge are:

* Lack of market information and limited access to markets.
* Limited commercial activity: The small part of their production that they manage to sell is sold through informal channels, generally resulting in very low prices.

II. Barriers that limit access to finance include:

* Limited financial resources: Producers do not have access to formal credit facilities because they don't meet the legal and commercial requirements to apply for a credit line.
* Lack of market information and limited access to markets.
* Limited commercial activity: The small part of their production that they manage to sell is sold through informal channels, generally resulting in very low prices.

III. Barriers that limit access to technology include:

* Lack of market information and limited access to markets.
* Limited commercial activity: The small part of their production that they manage to sell is sold through informal channels, generally resulting in very low prices.

### iv. Organizations with commercial capacities and clear target markets

Organizations with commercial capacities and clear target markets. These organizations are usually small- to medium-sized enterprises or cooperatives with commercial capacities and clear target markets and formalized producers' organization.

#### v. Limited capacity to integrate EDA and climate resilience into investments

Barrier i: Limited capacity to integrate EDA and climate resilience into investments. The government processes and systems for disbursing public funds are not designed to include EDA or climate resilient practices into policies, plans, budgets and investments. MIGADIR extension services do not regularly provide information on the impacts of climate change on agricultural production in Ethiopia; so there is no way to monitor and evaluate progress in adaptive capacity neither at the Ministry nor at local level. The lack of a monitoring system for EDA and climate resilience has also led to the loss of knowledge being lost. On top, in some instances, they are no longer relevant in the new climate context and are often not being used by regional or national governments. According to Gertner et al (2002), one of the challenges that need to be overcome to optimize EDA investments is the lack of information on the budget allocation process from public funds to EDA investments. Sometimes, it depends on the political decision of both regional and national governments. The lack of a monitoring system for EDA and climate resilience includes a monitoring system, for example hydrology, what makes more complex to verify the impacts that the interventions have had in terms of adaptive capacity. In addition, the inclusion of gender considerations into the gender approach is a pending and necessary task in the investment projects; it is necessary to include consideration on how the investments will be implemented and incorporated the needs and women's visions from the formulation, implementation and evaluation of projects.

#### Knowledge and information about EDA

Barriers ii: The practices and ancestral knowledge linked to EDA are not sufficiently disseminated and scaled-up. In this field, a very important barrier refers to the fact that the best practices and ancestral knowledge related to EDA are not being used because they are not known by farmers. Practices related to EDA are being lost due to (i) the processes of emigration of young people and (ii) the lack of appreciation and recognition of these practices in the rural areas. The study carried out by the Ethiopian Climate Change project carried out a survey to identify the existence of a common practice among the farmers, which is the use of traditional knowledge and ancestral knowledge. This knowledge was obtained through observation of the sky. This usage is mainly spread among old farmers (older than 70 years), for whom it has higher credibility. The younger generation does not have this knowledge because they do not know about ancestral knowledge, refusing non-modern and then these unreliable skills. The rural areas of SHAP have experienced a significant loss in the use of traditional practices related to EDA due to the migration of young people in anticipation or in reaction to hazards; others stay in spite of the challenges changes. The vulnerability of highland farmers is increasing because they are not able to adapt their agricultural production systems, land and livestock, and demographic factors such as family size, age, and health. Outmigration has been identified as a major factor that contributes to the loss of traditional knowledge related to EDA; this migration may weaken the adaptive capacity as traditional knowledge and labour resources are being lost.

## Institutional barriers

### Barrier 1: Limited alignment and coordination among stakeholders to reinforce positive impact on the territory.

The weak intersectoral and intergovernmental articulation and coordination is one more reason for policy failure in the implementation of the MEFERE. The lack of a clear institutional framework, with hierarchical structural organizations that limits the channels of coordination between ministries, levels of government and other actors involved in the management of ecosystems, generates an environment of critical knots that make the correct execution of the policy impossible. The implementation of public policies in this area is complicated by the fact that there are no mechanisms for articulation or coordination among the platforms, instances or spaces of articulation and coordination where the government authorities of the three levels of government (national, regional and local) meet to discuss issues related to ecosystems.

Despite the existence of a territorial development plan, the lack of institutional alignment and the community's involvement is limited due to the lack of awareness, weak capacities to dialogue, negotiate and put their proposals into practice.

### Barrier 2: Limited institutional knowledge hinders the implementation of PES schemes. 

The coordination between the different actors involved in the management of ecosystems is a key factor for the success of the MEFERE. The limited institutional knowledge of the actors involved in the design and execution of the policy, as well as their lack of experience in the field of ecosystem services, hinders the implementation of PES schemes. This situation is due to the fact that there are no mechanisms or instruments available to address the challenges present in the design of the MEFERE and the execution of the funds raised, as well as its incorporation into the institutional framework of the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources (MENRE). Technical barriers are mainly related to the limited knowledge to understand the functioning and management of ecosystems. On the other hand, limited capacities and methodologies to monitor and evaluate the effectiveness of PES schemes in terms of their contribution to the recovery and conservation of ecosystems which activities are effective for the recovery, conservation and sustainable use of HES source ecosystems; and monitoring and evaluation of the impact on the ecosystem services provided by these ecosystems.

### Barrier 3: Limited integration of EIA into regulatory frameworks. 

Although there is a State Policy No. 54 "For the Protection of Water Resources", it does not establish the necessary instruments to channel efforts toward respecting the occupation and appropriate use of the territory, embankments, waterways and other areas that are subject to the protection of water resources (Olea, E., 2015). Another aspect is the absence of policies for the recognition and valuation of ecosystem services provided by HES source ecosystems. This lack of regulation in this area has a negative impact on the way the reward of the restoration and conservation of ecosystems; the same an advance in this sense as the MEFERE, which seeks to establish a framework that allows the achievement of the objectives fulfilled through the companies that provide water and sanitation services and must be used for the restoration and conservation of HES source ecosystems. This new framework opens a range of technical and social needs and limitations that must be overcome. Since MERERE is a policy that seeks to integrate the ecosystem approach into the regulatory frameworks, it is necessary to design regulatory frameworks that facilitate other public actors could be engaged and committed.

## B.3 Project/programme description (max. 2900 words, approximately 5 pages)

### Overall
The project will support MECAGEN in promoting a paradigm shift in the management of Pyura chilensis and other invasive species in the country. This will increase climate resilience of some of the most vulnerable groups in the country which include rural farming and fishing communities on these islands. The project will also contribute to the achievement of national measures to reduce greenhouse gas emissions, including through reducing deforestation, precipitation variability, and ocean acidification. It will support the identification of financial, economic, social, and regulatory barriers (as described in section B2 (a)) to the implementation of ecosystem-based adaptation strategies that are aligned with the needs of ecosystems in a more sustainable and climate-smart way, offering alternatives to reduce unsustainable practices contributing to deforestation and ocean acidification. The project will also contribute towards a comprehensive approach on Ecosystem-Based Solutions and a focus on the implementation and upscaling of these solutions.

### In order to achieve this, the project consists of three components:
1. Resilient ecosystems and communities,
2. Public-private partnerships for the development, financing, and upscaling, and
3. Governance for the incorporation of EBA measures which are aligned with the three outcomes as depicted below.
The overall project design is presented in detail below[24].

### 24. Ecosystem-based Adaptation, Nature-based Solutions, and Natural Climate Solutions are similar concepts describing slightly different approaches to climate change adaptation that focus on the role of ecosystems in reducing vulnerability and increasing resilience. The terms have been used interchangeably throughout this document based on experience gathered in the country and alignment with current national policies. The terms, however, may be used microscopically to describe an approach or macroscopically to describe a strategy.

#### Outcome 1: Ecosystems are restored, conserved, and better managed through the implementation of EBA measures complemented by CRVC:

95. The project aims at supporting investments led by IPCLs for resilient ecosystems at local landscape level and strengthening their capacity to implement EBA measures and CRVC. This will be achieved by reducing direct financial pressure on IPCLs while providing them with technical support, training, and access to markets. At the same time it addresses weak associativity, limited economic opportunities, and low access to finance and markets for Climate Resilience Value Chains (CRVC). The project will also contribute to the implementation of EBA measures by supporting the development of CRVC through two main outputs:

### Output 1.1: Investments for EBA measures and Climate Resilient Value Chains are implemented at the local landscape level

a. The relevant knowledge is recovered and disseminated, and local monitoring committees and observation systems are implemented.

b. Activities to create a conducive environment of limited economic opportunities by supporting the development of Climate Resilience Value Chains and enabling entrepreneurship in the SHARP region.

### Activity 1.1.1: Setting up the basis for financing and implementing EBA measures and climate resilient value chains (CERVIC)

This activity will focus on creating a communication strategy to develop awareness among the local stakeholders regarding the importance of EBA measures and CRVC in landscape and/or micro water basin levels that will be financed by the Puna Facility. In this regard, the Local initiatives will organize workshops with the local stakeholders to identify the potential beneficiaries for EBA measures and CRVC in landscape and/or micro water basin levels (e.g., producers' cooperatives) who will be able to receive technical support in order to participate in the call for proposals. The process of identification of potential beneficiaries will be carried out by the Local initiatives, which will be responsible for the prioritized project area (38 districts) and be visited to identify potential beneficiaries interested in participating in the Call for Proposals. The identified beneficiaries will be invited to participate in a workshop organized by the Puna Facility. These workshops will be held at least once per district and will include representatives of the EBA measures and CRVC that contribute to their resilience. In sub-activity 1.1.2, the potential beneficiaries will apply their Local Similarities (LS) and/or Micro Water Basin (MWB) in order to identify the most appropriate EBA measure and CRVC that can be implemented within their LS or MWB. The results of this activity will be established in the Puna Facility Operations Manual. This activity includes the following sub-activities:

### Sub-activity 1.1.1.1: Awareness raising among local stakeholders regarding the importance of EBA measures and CRVC

This sub-activity aims to inform local stakeholders about the importance of EBA measures and CRVC in landscape and/or micro water basin levels that will be financed by the Puna Facility.

### Sub-activity 1.1.1.2: Identification of potential beneficiaries for EBA measures and CRVC

# Activity 1.1.2: Financing and Implementing of EBA measures and Climate Resilient Value Chains

## Sub-activity 1.1.2.1: Implementation of Local Initiatives Financed by the Puna Facility (EE)

### Objectives:
The objective is to promote climate-resilient livelihoods and adaptation through the implementation of climate-focused Local Initiatives at local landscape level.

### Types of financial support:
Two types of financial support are complementary and contribute to a general objective of increasing resilience in the landscape. These include:

- **Non-Regulated Grand Sub-Windows:** For ILCIs with their different forms of organization (local communities, cooperatives, associations, etc.) and for projects that do not require specific environmental conditions.
- **Agricola Matchmaking Sub-Windows:** For farmers' production organizations (small and/or medium-sized producers) and for projects related to the development of new markets.

### Selection process:
The selection process for the Local Initiatives from preparation and approval call for proposals to the signing of the Grant Agreement is described in detail. The local initiative selection criteria and scorecard will include a gender component, with at least 50% of the points allocated to women's organizations, cooperatives, associations or women-led enterprises.

### Grants signed:
As of January 2017, 127 Local Initiatives Grant Agreements have been signed (with a total value of up to EUR 14.4 million) for the implementation of projects in the different sub-windos (including requirement conditions and sustainability can be found in B.4.5 and B.6.1 respectively).

## Sub-activity 1.1.2.2: Financial support through MEGADE (MEGADICR: In this sub-activity, MEGADEI, MEGADEII, and MEGADEIII will finance projects that contribute to the implementation of climate-resilient value chains including guris, pineapples, and high-value crops. These investments are implemented directly by MEGADEI (in the case of the Agricola Matchmaking Sub-Windows) in the same sector.

### Types of interventions:
MEGADE will finance three types of public intervention projects:

- **Public investment projects:** On the one hand, include afforestation and reforestation activities to improve soil quality and water and thermal irrigation to improve the efficiency of water resource application. These interventions are implemented directly by MEGADEI.
- **SEIPR will implement public investment projects that will contribute to research and technology transfer in high-value crops (pineapples, guris, etc.).**
- **Agricola Program Non-refundable funds for boosting the competitiveness of small producers:** These investment projects are implemented directly by MEGADEII.

### Objectives:
These investments contribute to the implementation of climate-resilient value chains including guris, pineapples, and high-value crops. They also aim at reducing costs (through the use of local tools) in order to reduce costs and improve agricultural productivity. These projects contribute to increasing the resilience of value chains.

For more information on the establishment of the Puna Facility please see sub-activity 2.1.1.1 for the operationalization Annex 21.

## Activity 1.1.3. Technical Assistance for implementing EBA measures and Climate Resilient Value Chains at National Level

### Sub-activity 1.1.3.1. Technical Assistance for implementing EBA measures (TA: EBA)

The activity will be three-fold:

* **Sub-activity 1.1.3.1.1. Technical Assistance for the implementation of EBA measures (TA: EBA)**: This sub-activity will provide TA to implement EBA measures, including extension services and commercial coordination, development of courses and events, among others.
* **Sub-activity 1.1.3.1.2. Technical Assistance for the improvement in value chains (TA: GIZ):** The project will aim at improving the value chain by increasing market access and developing opportunities for EBA including through a legal platform for sale of surplus products.

### Sub-activity 1.1.3.2. Technical assistance for the refinement of proposals and Local initiatives implementation (TA: Proframone)

The objective of this sub-activity is to provide TA that will assist in the development of proposals and local initiatives, including through a series of workshops held at regular intervals before the final proposal submission.

### Sub-activity 1.1.3.3. Technical Assistance for the implementation of Local initiatives (TA: Proframone)

This sub-activity aims to provide TA that will assist in the implementation of Local initiatives provided by the TA provider and optionally in conjunction with the CSD in the case of the Non-repayable Grant Sub-Win dow.

### Sub-activity 1.1.3.4. Technical assistance for the improvement of sustainable cattle breeding (TA: SEFOR)

This sub-activity aims to improve the sustainability of cattle breeding, including participatory approaches for planning, implementing, monitoring and reporting using the community resource management approach, as well as through the development of courses and events.

### Sub-activity 1.1.3.5. Technical assistance in value chains (TA: GIZ)

The project will aim at improving the value chain by increasing market access and developing opportunities for EBA including through a legal platform for sale of surplus products.

## Sub-activity 1.2.1: Strengthening knowledge and capacity building for a person-centered approach (PUC) (Instituto de Montaña): This sub-activity aims to support the recognition and appreciation of IPLCs ancestral knowledge and experiences, as well as their role in the conservation and sustainable use of natural resources. It will focus on participation and leadership of Indigenous Peoples and Local Communities in recovering ancestral knowledge and experiences, and will be implemented through a participatory process that includes training, capacity building, learning and to share innovations and practices developed between communities. Particular attention will be placed on reinforcing the role of women as key actors in the conservation and sustainable use of natural resources and their resilience within communities. At least one exchange among PLCs women from different communities will also be promoted during this sub-activity (1.2.1.1). The intergenerational dialogue plan will define the implementation strategy, both intercultural and intergenerational exchanges, and the methodology for building capacity in ancestral knowledge and practices. This sub-activity will be implemented in coordination with MIDAGRI (through its programs), SERNAMP, Profonrane, Local Government.

### 1.2.1.1: Facilitating Intergenerational dialogue on ancestral practices and innovation in a wide range of contexts (PUC): The intergenerational dialogue plan will define the implementation strategy for women (youth) and adults) on ancestral practices and innovation in a context of change (climate, markets, and others) in selected communities. This sub-activity will be implemented through workshops, training sessions, and other innovative strategy and implementation plan will be prepared, containing the methodology, means and instruments (video, photos, etc.) to facilitate intergenerational dialogue on ancestral practices and innovation. ### 1.2.1.2: Implementing community monitoring and observation systems to measure the impact of EBA recommendations feedback for regional and national policies:

This activity will be implemented in alliance with the IPLCs implementing Local Initiatives and supporting local CBOs, as well as with MIDAGRI (through its programs), SERNAMP, Profonrane, and other relevant beneficiary communities that were selected in Puna Facility No.1 non-repayable Grant Sub-Win and will provide technical assistance to these communities for the implementation of the Community Monitoring System (CMS) for monitoring this activity. This sub-activity will build upon Community Monitoring Committees (CMC), which applies to the Puna Facility No. 1 non-repayable Grant Sub-Win, and will be implemented in coordination with MIDAGRI (through its programs). The CMCs are composed of local representatives (composed by women and men) (designated previously in sub-activity 1.1.2) who will lead the implementation of this sub-activity. The CMCs will monitor the progress of the Local Initiatives, providing feedback on the progress of the Local Initiative to the community members. Within this activity the capacities of local representatives will be strengthened through training and capacity building sessions in monitoring systems and measures. This will enable knowledge weaving and co-production in understanding how traditional practices and
