### 11.3 Sub-activity 12.2.1: Training and implementation of Community Monitoring Committees (CMC) to develop local indicators of EBA success

The CMCs will be trained in the use of locally developed indicators for monitoring the impacts of the climate impact of change and poor land management on high Andean ecosystems, as well as the effectiveness of EBA measures. The CMCs will also be trained in the use of locally developed indicators to assess the impacts on the recovery and conservation of their ecosystems and ecosystem services. Local Researchers (comprising members from the local research institution) will provide technical support for the CMCs. The CMCs will collect information about the impact of implemented EBA measures in a context of climate change and poor land management, as well as the effectiveness of these measures on the recovery and conservation of their ecosystems and ecosystem services. This sub-activity will be coordinated at all levels of intervention, including from the national level to the community monitoring process since the selection of indicators to assess socio-economic, ecosystem and ecological impacts of EBA measures. The CMCs will also collect information about the impact of implemented water utility monitoring systems. This sub-activity will allow beneficial communities to take a proactive role in monitoring their ecosystems and ecosystem services. The CMCs will be trained on how to use locally developed indicators for selecting sites for monitoring, setting a baseline information for their Local Initiative and collecting data using an easy-to-use mobile application. The collected data will be stored in the Cloud-based system of the local management system that will also be developed in sub-activity 12.2.2. This system will be connected to the Puna Facility and will be used by the CMCs, as well as other stakeholders involved in EBA implementation.

### 11.3 Sub-activity 12.2.2: Development of a local data-driven information management system for monitoring the impact of EBA measures

The Cloud-based system that will store the data and information provided by the community monitoring committees (sub-activity 12.2.1) will be developed in this sub-activity, as well as the development of a local data-driven information management system (prior Local Initiative implementation), and the outcomes of the selected EBA measures and CIVICs. The measurement of the impact of EBA measures on ecosystem recovery and conservation will be monitored by the community monitoring committees when needed. The Cloud-based system must be later hosted in one of the existing MAMAS platforms, such as the Puna Facility (for climate change adaptation) or the Andean Sustainable Development Platform (sustainability condition). This sub-activity will include data collection tools and protocols for processing and sharing information among stakeholders involved in EBA implementation. The Cloud-based system must be developed with the CMCs. Special consideration will be given to hire a consultancy from a research specialized institution such as an indigenous organization or a scientific institution that has experience in developing local monitoring systems, and in data collection and processing tools for EBA implementation.

### 11.3 Sub-activity 12.2.3: Analytical collected information on the impact of EBA measures (EE; GIZ)

The EE will be responsible for analyzing the collected data from sub-activities 12.2.1 and 12.2.2 to develop an understanding of EBA measures for future decision-making processes (i.e., increasing community awareness and value, improving local governance, and enhancing the effectiveness of EBA measures). The EE will also provide feedback on the implementation of EBA across relevant ministries, water utilities and public investments. Therefore, this sub-activity will develop baseline information to assess the impact of EBA measures in terms of ecosystem recovery and conservation, disentanglement of coupled indigenous and scientific knowledge. Main topics to analyze will include impact measurement of EBA (carbon sequestration, ecosystem recovery and water regulation), cost-benefit analysis, cost,

### 8.2.1 Outcome 3: Public and private financing for EBA measures and Climate Resilient livelihoods are established in the Puna ecosystem

#### Activity 2.1.1 Establish the Puna Facility for long-term financing of EBA measures and Climate Resilient Value Chains.

This activity aims to unlock investment for EBA measures in Puna ecosystems and Climate Resilient Value Chains through the newly created Puna Facility, managed by ProPronatura. The Puna Facility will be composed of the financial assets generated from the implementation of the three main activities which include: activity 2.1.1 to establish the Puna Facility for long-term financing of EBA measures and Climate Resilient Value Chains; activity 2.1.2 to develop and implement innovative mechanisms to finance EBA in high Andean ecosystems including hydrological planning, water resources management, and climate change adaptation; and activity 2.1.3 to establish a mechanism to develop and implement innovative mechanisms for EBA in high Andean ecosystems including hydrological planning, water resources management, and climate change adaptation.

The Puna Facility will be composed of the financial assets generated from the implementation of these three activities which include: activity 2.1.1 to establish the Puna Facility for long-term financing of EBA measures and Climate Resilient Value Chains; activity 2.1.2 to develop and implement innovative mechanisms to finance EBA in high Andean ecosystems including hydrological planning, water resources management, and climate change adaptation; and activity 2.1.3 to establish a mechanism to develop and implement innovative mechanisms for EBA in high Andean ecosystems including hydrological planning, water resources management, and climate change adaptation.

The sustainable financing strategy will include criteria for selecting interested companies (e.g., companies committed to the conservation of biodiversity and ecosystem services), criteria for selecting interested communities (e.g., communities committed to the conservation of biodiversity and ecosystem services), criteria for selecting interested investors (e.g., investors committed to the conservation of biodiversity and ecosystem services). The sustainable financing strategy will be validated with the different stakeholders involved in the Puna Facility, including ProPronatura, MINDAMAR, MINAM, and other relevant actors. The sustainable financing strategy will be validated with the different stakeholders involved in the Puna Facility, including ProPronatura, MINDAMAR, MINAM, and other relevant actors.

The Puna Facility will be established to provide long-term financial resources for EBA measures and use of public funds for actions related to EBA and CRVC; the activity will seek to increase the mobilization of resources from different sources (e.g., private sector, government, international cooperation) in order to ensure access to and alignment of investments from MINDAMAR and SERHAN and other government programmes will be promoted through the development of a communication material on all of these programmes.

## Activity 2.1.2. Strengthen capacities to develop and implement innovative mechanisms for EBA in High Andean ecosystems

### Sub-activity 2.1.2.a. Technical assistance to improve existing hydrological MEREE processes in the project area (target five water utilities)

The sub-activity will seek to overcome the barriers faced by public water utilities that have established or are willing to establish a mechanism for the implementation of the PES (in which it is called Rethink Mechanism for Ecosystems) and/or the National Andean Fund for Sustainable Development (FONAD), in order to increase the resilience of Andean ecosystems but also to increase water availability. The objective is to engage with men and women officers of public water utilities, as well as other actors involved in the implementation of the PES, such as the Resilient Puna project and other private-public initiatives. The sub-activity will include technical assistance for hydrological MEREE processes (including the development of specific financial products or the generation of existing financial products for EBA measures) and gender-sensitive aspects in financial products to promote EBA in Climate Resilient Value Chains.

### Sub-activity 2.1.2.b. Supporting the greening of microcredits to promote EBA and Climate Resilient Value Chains

beneficiaries and other regions interested in implementing EBA measures and CRVC (aligned with the lists of EBA measures and CRVC developed in sub-activity 1.2.1) within the Puna ecosystems. Microfinance institutions (MFIs) will be supported to develop a gender-disaggregated financial inclusion strategy, which will include products for adaptation will be selected (e.g., Caja rural los Andes, Caja Coqui, Microfinance BBVA Foundation- Colombia, and Banco de la República). The goal is to identify the most appropriate EBA measures (products for women) or develop new ones, incorporating the EBA approach in the financial risk analysis for products related to microcredit. This will be done through a participatory process with stakeholders, including disaggregation by gender and employing a cross-cutting approach. The goal is to identify the value chains prioritized by women and men, as well as the most appropriate EBA measures (products) that can be offered based on gender. Then mitigation actions (EBA measures) will be proposed and included in the value chain business plans. This will also include a focus on the financial inclusion of women and men, taking into account how they have on agricultural producers, men and women, and the credit portfolio of microcredit institutions. Moreover, through this process, it is expected that new products will be developed for women, including EBA measures related to green financial products and financial instruments specifically designed for women. This review with stakeholders will also include a focus on the financial inclusion of women and men, taking into account how they have on agricultural producers, men and women, and the credit portfolio of microcredit institutions.

Sub-activity 1.2.3: Raise awareness of greener microcredits to promote EBA and Climate Resilient Value Chains in rural areas. The awareness raising activities will be developed based on the lessons learned from the previous sub-activities and include the development and implementation of an awareness campaign for financial institutions, women’s organizations, and other stakeholders involved in the Puna ecosystems. To achieve this, the sub-activity will capacitate field personnel of financial institutions, which will promote the greener microcredit approach to be applied by these institutions. The capacity building activities will also be targeted at rural communities in the high Andean zone, where women are more vulnerable and have limited access to credit facilities. This will help them better understand climate risks within their portfolio. Credit officers from these institutions will receive training on the EBA approach, climate risk management tools, and financial inclusion strategies for women. The objective is to increase climate resilience in businesses. Subsequently, the greener microcredits will be disseminated through different channels (e.g., community meetings, radio, social media) with a focus on rural areas where women are more exposed and specifically for the beneficiaries of the Puna Facility. This will provide them other sources for financing their present and future needs.

#### B.3.3 Outcome 3: EBA and climate resilience are mainstreamed into multilevel landscape governance instruments

Outcome 125 will promote integrated landscape planning and policy improvement and coordination, by fostering dialogue and improving coordination among stakeholders in the landscape (local, regional, national) to ensure that EBA measures are included in the landscape plans. This will include the development of extension services, among others. The corresponding activities proposed in this outcome will directly address identified gaps in governance structures and mechanisms for landscape planning and management at different levels: (i) subnational level (e.g., Departmental Environmental Councils), (ii) national level (e.g., National Environment System, SENANP and others), (iii) weak governance structures for a sustainable management of the Puna ecosystems. The objective is to ensure that EBA measures are included in landscape plans and policies and regulatory frameworks enforcement for implementing the EBA approach and Climate Resilient Value Chains.

130. Output 1. Multilevel landscape governance is improved through strengthening national capacity, regulatory frameworks and M&E systems. The approaches that will be promoted by Resilient Puna require

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 46

## Activity 3.1. Strengthen the capacities for territorial planning and governance processes integrating EBA and climate resilience:

### Objective:
To strengthen participatory sectoral and territorial planning (so that EBA measures and CRVC developed by project beneficiaries can be replicated and scaled up). Through this activity, the project will enable that local governments and relevant public officials in the target watersheds (and beyond) are equipped with the necessary skills and knowledge to effectively plan for climate resilience and scale-up of the agenda of government platforms[1] and in local and regional government plans. In order to ensure a gender-sensitive approach, the project will engage women and men from all relevant stakeholders for the incorporation of EBA measures and CRVC measures, integrating the gender perspective in their planning processes.

### Sub-objectives:
(1) Strengthen capacities of relevant public officials working on thematic and geographical areas of the project at the national, regional and local levels to effectively plan for climate resilience and scale-up of the agenda of government platforms[2] and in local and regional government plans. This will be achieved through a training-of-trainers (ToT) workshop that will be held in each of the three thematic areas (land use planning, water resources management and extension services) and will involve relevant public officials working on these themes at national level. The ToT workshop will also include a session on gender mainstreaming in territorial planning and governance processes.

(2) Strengthen capacities of local governments to effectively plan for climate resilience and scale-up of the agenda of government platforms[3] and in local and regional government plans. This will be achieved through a training-of-trainers (ToT) workshop that will be held at each of the three thematic areas (land use planning, water resources management and extension services) and will involve relevant public officials working on these themes at national level. The ToT workshop will also include a session on gender mainstreaming in territorial planning and governance processes.

(3) Integrate EBA measures and CRVC in territorial planning and management instruments. Lessons from both the pilot sites (Makueni and Migori counties) and the project areas (Nyanza, Siaya and Busia counties) will be used to develop a set of guidelines for integrating EBA measures and CRVC into territorial planning in the target watersheds (watershed management plans).

### Sub-objective 2: Strengthen capacities of relevant public officials working on thematic and geographical areas at national, regional and local government stakeholders for the incorporation of EBA and CRVC measures, integrating the gender perspective[4] in their planning processes.

132. **Sub-activity 3.1.1.3. Strengthen participatory sectoral and territorial platforms for the articulation and scaling up of EBA measures and Climate Resilient Value Chains within the scope of the project (EE: GIZ)**

The local government will be supported in strengthening its capacity to implement EBA measures and CRVCs at the local and regional platforms.1) It disseminates successful Local initiatives experiences financed in component 1 for scaling up EBA and CRVC Commissions, especially the one led by IPCLC women organizations and (ii) coordinates public and private sector actors for EBA and CRVC implementation at the local level.2) The activity integrates a gender approach. The first step within this sub-activity involves identifying sectoral and territorial platforms that are already in place or can be established to facilitate dialogue among stakeholders, including those from diverse public, private, and civil society organizations in the project area converge to foster dialogue, address concerns, and share information on EBA measures and CRVCs. The second step is to identify potential actors at the water basin level. Potential platforms include Regional Committees for Agriculture Management (CIGRA), Good Governance Platforms, and other existing or new platforms that can be established in the project area.

The project will support the participation of IPCLCs in its different forms of organizations (local community and women’s groups) in these platforms to ensure their active involvement. The third step is to identify actors implementing EBA measures and Climate Resilient Value Chains. Participation of women in these spaces of dialogue and decision-making is essential for ensuring that the needs, concerns, and priorities of women are part of the social context for the identification of actors and promotion of Good Governance Platforms will be developed as well as the development of a gender action plan. The fourth step involves identifying the relevant institutional (e.g., climate hydrological finance, etc.) and will foster the inclusion of climate risk vulnerabilities in their planning processes. The fifth step is to identify potential sources of funding for EBA measures and CRVCs and future funding. Finally, successful EBA and CRVC experiences that have emerged because of the implementation within the project at landscape and water basin level (through the implementation of activities in component 1 will be disseminated.

134. **Sub-activity 3.1.1.3. Integration of EBA measures and Climate Resilient Value Chains in territorial planning and management instruments (EE: GIZ)** This sub-activity aims at increasing influence on subnational planning processes, including the development of sectoral plans, regional climate change strategies, and local land use plans. The specific tasks assigned to this sub-activity are described below. Technical assistance will be provided to local and regional governments interested in developing or improving their development plans or budgeting process for EBA measures and CRVCs.

i) Local government: Develop sectoral planning Plans: The technical assistance for local and regional technical officers will be focused on improving their procurement processes and the prioritization and phasification of adaptation measures at landscape and water basin level. Additionally, climate risk analyses will be developed in territorial plans, based on the information provided by the project. This will allow the integration of EBA measures into existing planning documents, including sectoral plans, regional climate change strategies, and local land use plans. The technical assistance will also support the development of adaptation strategies for landscape management and alignment of water NEREID.

ii) Local government: Develop Regional Climate Change Strategies: Technical support to local or regional technical officers will be focused in developing participatory Regional Climate Change Strategies and/or Local Climate Change Plans, which are aligned with the national climate change strategy and the national plan for climate change initiatives.

iii) Regional governments: Investment projects: Technical Support to local and regional technical officers to prioritize and design EBA and Climate Resilient Value Chains within the local and regional public budgets. Include in the budgeting process the implementation of EBA measures and CRVCs.

iv) Natural Protected Areas: Master plans: The technical assistance for SENANP will be focused in the integrate the Eba approach in the participatory process of updating master plans. Support to update their climate risk and vulnerability analysis, as well as the integration of EBA measures into the master plan.

**Activity 3.1.2. Strengthen regulatory frameworks and M&E systems at national level:**

The project will support the development of a National Climate Change Strategy and Action Plan, which will be the regulatory framework for the promotion and implementation of EBA measures and CRVC in the High Andean areas of the country, as well as ensuring the participation of IPCLCs in resolutions related to their participation in decision making process

### Section B: Conflicts of interest, these two functions - accreditation and implementation function- are strictly separated, with different management structures within GIZ.

As AE, GIZ will be responsible for the execution of the project, as defined in the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA) between GCF and GIZ (AE). As AE, GIZ will administer project funds on behalf of the GCF and provide technical assistance to MIGAIRE, SERNAP, Profranorte and Instituto de Montaña as ES for disbursing GCF funds through its relevant head office units.

In order to ensure effective implementation, GIZ will need to establish legal arrangements with MIGAIRE, SERNAP, Profranorte and Instituto de Montaña - see Figure 12. Legal below:

The figure shows the different roles of the parties involved in the project, as well as the steps required for the implementation of the GCF project (amended commissioning agreement). The GIZ will transfer funds based on the payment plan agreed with the MIGAIRE.

* **GIZ** (as EE) will receive an internal task assignment from the AE for the implementation of the project.
* **GIZ** (as ES) will sign a grant agreement with the relevant head office unit of GIZ, which is in accordance with standard operating procedures with the MDAGIR as the political partner of the project and the Executing Entity executing activities on behalf of the MIGAIRE.
* **MIGAIRE** as the Project Owner will sign a cooperation agreement (i.e., subsidiary agreement) with GIZ (as ES).
* **SERNAP** as an Executing Entity executing activities with own funds will sign a cooperation agreement (i.e., subsidiary agreement) with GIZ (as ES).
* Finally, **GIZ** will sign with Profranorte and Instituto de Montaña grant agreements (i.e. subsidiary agreements) to manage and operate grant disbursement through the Puna Facility.

B

## Technical assistance to MIDAGRI and SERNANP to mainstream Ecosystem based Adaptation (EbA), climate resilience, and gender aspects in all their programmes and to improve coordination between their respective sectors.

### GIZ Peru will also provide technical assistance to Profrontera to improve their capacities to sustainable management of the Funa Purú.

143. GIZ Peru will also act as the technical secretariat within the Project Steering Committee and as the executive coordinator for the implementation of the project, including coordination with the Ministry of Agriculture and Irrigation teams.
- In this role as a technical expert GIZ Peru will keep the Steering Committee and the Management Committee informed in a timely manner about the main decisions or recommendations made by the GCF in the framework of the Project.

145. GIZ as an EE will be responsible for a GCF budget of EUR 19,315 million.

## Profrontera (Executing Entity-GCF funds)

### The Funa Purú is an environmental fund organized under the law of the Republic of Peru in accordance with Decree Law No. 20154. It is the only environmental fund in Peru and a direct access entity accredited before GCF. Its main objective is to promote the conservation, sustainable use and restoration of ecosystems, such as MERESE for Arequipa, etc., its mandate is to provide stable, long-term funding and to develop and implement innovative projects that contribute to the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) through partnerships with donors, its experience in managing environmental funds, and its strong fiduciary standards will ensure the sustainability of the fund.

147. Profrontera will be responsible for the management of the "Funa Purú" which will be set up under activity 2.13 (funding) of the Project Implementation Plan (PIP). The Fund will be managed by a Technical Committee, through calls for proposals aimed at promoting Local initiatives to implement Ecosystem based Adaptation measures in the Funa Purú area. Profrontera will also provide technical assistance to MIDAGRI and SERNANP as Technical Assistance (through sub-activity 1.13.2) through a service provider for the implementation of the Local initiative.

In order to manage the Fund, Profrontera will set up a Facility Management Unit (FMU). Among its functions:

- Ensure that all activities promoted and developed by the Funa Purú are in accordance with the strategic guidelines established.
- Ensure submission of information to the Project Management Committee in case any modifications or updates are made to the PIP.
- Develop the terms of reference for the calls for proposals.
- Finalize the selection process according to the terms of reference and established criteria and proposal for evaluation by the Project Management Committee (shortlist).
- Register the selected Local Initiatives.
- Monitor the execution of funded Local initiatives.

150. Profrontera will also participate in the Project Management Committee (PMC) and will be part of the Territorial Coordination Group (TCG).

In addition, it will take lead and coordinate with other project partners to guarantee the integral fulfillment of the expected results.

152. Profrontera as an EE will be responsible for a GCF budget of EUR 19,175 million.


The Ministry of Agricultural Development and Irrigation of Peru (MIDAGRI) (Executing Entity-Own contribution)

### MIDAGRI is the Peruvian government institution in charge of the agricultural sector. It was created by Law 9711 of December 20th, 2008. Its main objectives are: i) Strengthen producers' organizations and promote their integration under watershed and production areas; ii) Promote the development of a market economy for agricultural products through business management of agricultural producers, providing technical assistance; iii) Establish an agricultural infrastructure that allows the efficient operation of the agricultural sector; iv) Provide information to producers on access to legal, administrative, management, financial, technical assistance, health and other services.

For more information, please see Annex 21 Operations Manual chapter 3.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 51

B

## Summary of the Capacity Needs Assessment

The Green Climate Fund (GCF) has developed a Capacity Needs Assessment (see Annex B) which was implemented to assess the capacity needs and institutional gaps from the EESs (besides GIZ for project implementation) in order to provide recommendations on how to address these gaps effectively.

In the context of the project, it is important to note that although there are capacities exist, but enabling conditions are weak, at the same time the implementation process of EEA climate resilience and gender equality. The first priority will be to strengthen existing capacities (established functions) to develop specific guidelines, methodologies and tools to incorporate a gender and climate perspective in the project activities.

B.4.3 Project Governance structure

166. The project governance structure (shown in Annex B) was extensively discussed, developed and validated by the project partners. Following bodies will be established:

* **Project Steering Committee:** This level of governance will be overseen by a Steering Committee consisting of GIZ, MIDAGRI and the PMG.
* **PSC members:** The PSC members will be appointed by the General Office of Planning and Budget at MIDAGRI. Specifically, PSC participating members will include:
    * **Minister of Agriculture:** The Vice-Minister office for Family Farming Development and Agrarian Infrastructure
    * **Minister of Finance:** The Vice-Minister office for Strategic Development of Natural Resources, which will provide the required climate change adaptation funds.
    * **Representative of GIZ**
* **Project Management Committee (PMC):** The PMC will provide policy and strategic guidance to the Project Management Committee (PMC), while adhering to the terms of the Funding Proposal approved by the GCF, the legal agreements, policies and procedures of the GCF, and national socio-economic development objectives.
    * Acknowledge proposed tools, directives and standards presented by the PMC and advise for their inclusion in the project.
    * Encourage national authorities to take actions of addressing climate issues and ensure project implementation.
    * Provide guidance for the fulfilment of legal agreements.
    * Monitor compliance with environmental, social and environmental safeguards, the Indigenous Peoples policy, and the objectives and implementation of the respective plans (ESMIP, ILEPDC and GAF).
    * Ensure that all decisions are taken in accordance with the GCF rules.
    * Resolve any disputes that arise within the Project Management Committee and holds decision-making authority on behalf of the GCF and the PSC members.
* **Annual approval of the project Annual Operating Plan (AOP), evaluate and provide approval for the execution of the AOP by the PMC.**
* **Provide strategic guidance for project management and provides feedback on the AOP, based on systematic monitoring and evaluation results.**
* **Oversee the timely and adequate delivery of the respective contributions of the parties to the project.**
* **Support the PSC in its decision-making process.**

The Project Steering Committee (PSC) will establish a Technical Secretariat, operated by GIZ Peru. The Secretariat will be responsible for providing administrative support to the PSC members and the Project Management Committee (PMC). In addition, it will coordinate (before, during and after implementation of the project).

172. The PSC will meet at least 2 times a year and more frequently when, in particular, for example for accountability and/or consultation with the NDA (Ministry of Economy and Finance), as well as other national authorities, international organizations, representatives of local communities, national authorities, donors, commuting and producer organizations, women's groups, Indigenous Peoples representatives, NGOs, civil society organizations and other stakeholders.

Project Management Committee (PMC)

174. The Project Management Committee (PMC) will be responsible for overseeing and coordinating the project implementation in terms of its objectives and ensure its execution within the designated timeframe. The PMC will also provide regular reports to the Steering Committee.

### 176. The Project Team will be responsible to:

- Review, reflect, and systematize lessons learned from the project, proposing their integration into tools, documents, and processes.
- Generate proposals in response to changes, effects, or events in the project areas that may be attributable to climate change, including adaptation measures.
- Mobilize timely technical expertise from EEs and other stakeholders when project development issues are arising.
- Timely share guidance with the PSC and offer feedbacks to enhance project coherence on both national and international levels (e.g., through the PSC).
- Supervise the implementation and compliance with Social and Environmental Safeguards and Gender policies in all phases of the project.
- Ensure compliance with project management directives, guidelines, and information flow among Executing Entities.
- Coordinate and ensure the timely implementation of activities by each Executing Entity in the designated geographical area, ensuring that the project is implemented according to the approved work plan and achieving a shared understanding among Executing Entities regarding the theory of change and its mainstreaming across all project components.
- Foster inter-institutional and inter-sectorial collaboration and collaborate with civil society.
- Oversee the implementation of project progress, aligning with approved plans, budgets, and indicators related to territorial needs outlined in the Terms of Reference (ToR) and other selection processes for regional coordinator positions in the evaluations of the project.

### 177. The PMC will meet at least once a year. The permanent PMC members will include according to the entity:

- Protagonate as an EE with a technical manager and financial manager.
- SERFOR as an EE with a technical manager and financial manager.
- Institute of Montaña as an EE with a technical manager and financial manager.

The PMC will also be responsible for evaluating the framework of its institutional competencies, including those corresponding to the implementation, monitoring, evaluation, and reporting on the project’s contributions (IRC). Other public or private entities, as well as representatives of Indigenous Peoples and Local Communities representatives, according to the subject to be addressed at the respective meeting.

- Identify and document lessons learned from the project implementation for future reference.
- Promote the participation of beneficiaries in the different public-private financing mechanisms and identify the needs of the communities.
- Supervise and monitor the implementation of project activities in the territory.
- Identify and report any deviations or changes to the project plan, including those related with the interventions planned by the project and inform the PIU Coordination.
- Identify and report any risks that may arise in the territories and report to the PIU Coordination.

## B.4.5 Pura Facility Sub-window

As described above, GCF will make GCF proceeds available to Provinces that then channel funds to Indigenous Peoples and Local initiatives in the form of grants or loans (or a combination thereof) for the implementation of projects and/or activities related to climate change adaptation and mitigation (the process of formulating), More and/or Small Enterprises (MSEs)**, community enterprises and cooperatives**. The Pura Facility Sub-window is designed to support the development of Climate Resilient Value Chains based on predefined eligible EBA measures and CRVC (as shown in Table 2 of the Operations Manual (Annex 21). Further detailed procedures are outlined in the Operations Manual (Annex 21).

### Purpose

Pura Facility Sub-window is designed to support the development of Climate Resilient Value Chains based on predefined eligible EBA measures and CRVC (as shown in Table 2 of the Operations Manual (Annex 21). Further detailed procedures are outlined in the Operations Manual (Annex 21).

### Grant Disbursements

Grant disbursements channelled through the Pura Facility will be though calls for proposals up to three times within a financial year. The facility is divided into two sub-windows:

a. **Results-based Approaches Grant Sub-window** for micro or small enterprises community enterprises and cooperatives that have demonstrated their capacities and clear target markets. Hence, Formal/legally incorporated enterprises are eligible.

b. **Non-reimbursable Sub-window** to finance a maximum non-reimbursable grant amount per Local initiative of up to 10% of the total project cost (excluding costs for which GCF is responsible).

### Recipients

The Pura Facility Sub-window will be available to Indigenous Peoples and Local Communities in this different forms of organization (communities, producers associations and cooperatives**) in the process of developing climate resilient value chains.

The Non-reimbursable Sub-window is meant to finance a maximum non-reimbursable grant amount per Local initiative of up to 10% of the total project cost (excluding costs for which GCF is responsible).

### Eligibility

- Formal/legally incorporated enterprises
- Micro or small enterprises community enterprises and cooperatives that have demonstrated their capacities and clear target markets. Hence, Formal/legally incorporated enterprises are eligible.

**More and/or Small Enterprises are defined according to the Legislative Decree 1298. More enterprises have from 1 to 10 employees and whose annual turnover is up to 5 million USD. Small enterprises have more than 10 employees and whose annual turnover is up to 1700 taxable units (see United Provinces Circular).**

**Community Enterprises are defined as organizations formed per law associations, native communities, peasant cooperatives, etc., that have been recognized by the competent authorities of the respective provinces.**

**Cooperatives are defined as legal entities established in accordance with the Law 1325 on the implementation of the association of agricultural producers in agricultural cooperatives was recently regulated and is now known as Cooperatives Law 1325. The law provides for a number of requirements that must be met to generate income, but the process will involve some requirements that may not allow them to apply to sub-window 2 in the first call.**

#### 190.
The principle underlying the differentiation between the different sub-windows is related to the segmentation of the potential beneficiaries which are reflected in the differences between their current internal organizational and commercial capacities and in the level of barriers they face (please see section B.2.a for more information). In this regard, the results-based Sub-window is meant to be used by smallholder farmers and cooperatives in the process of formalization without commercial capacities and clear target markets and the Agrodesas Matching Sub-window is meant to be used by producer organizations with commercial capacities and clear target markets. Moreover, the Agrodesas Matching Sub-window is meant to be used for producer organizations that are part of a larger programme – is meant to be used formalized producers organizations with commercial capacities and clear target markets.

#### 191.
Reflecting the fact that non-repayable Grant Sub-Window beneficiaries lack the required level of preparedness as addressed in section B.2.a, the results-based Sub-window will partner with qualified local CSOs to submit and implement approved Local Initiatives (while Agrodesas Matching Sub-window partners with producer organizations). If no eligible Local Initiative is found or source not found, in the Operations Manual (Annex 2)Reference source not found., lists the eligible resources.

* Percentage or absolute increase in revenues compared to the revenues in the year before application to the Fund.
* Percentage or absolute increase in profits over the same reference period.

## B.5 Justification for GCF request (max 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)
201. While the government has increased its focus on climate finance, efforts to channel budgetary or other public resources towards climate change have been limited and fragmented. The total amount of public spending allocated to climate change has been below USD 300 million for the past decade. For comparison, public investment in renewable energy has reached USD 1 billion per year since 2015.

The World Bank estimated at USD 45 billion Peru's investments needs in climate change mitigation and adaptation by 2030. The government has been able to mobilize only a fraction of this amount through the National Climate Change Fund (FNCN). In addition, there is limited public spending on managing natural resources sustainably. From 1% of the total national budget in 2015, this share increased to 4% by 2019. However, this still falls short of what is needed for sustainable development and environmental and climate crises, as shown in national environmental performance indicators (ranging from 0 to 1), where Peru ranks at 0.38 out of a maximum score of 1. The government's efforts are also reflected by the LAC at 0.45, on the other hand the forest sector scores 0.26 with a regional average in LAC of 0.57 (IBD, 2020).

The economic crisis has further exacerbated the situation. Peru is facing an economic slowdown, with the economy expected to slow down, inflation rate, relatively high unemployment, public debt on the rise and persistent political instability. The government's ability to finance climate change projects through its budget is limited.

Given this context, the government is making a substantial effort to co-finance the proposed GCF project, matching the GCF contribution with resources from other sources. This will allow for more effective and efficient use of public funds by coordinating between private and public actors to scale up the impact of interventions at the ecosystem and local community level.

The project will be implemented in Peru's Amazon region, where there is a high potential for climate change adaptation and mitigation activities. The project will also contribute to the development of new and underdeveloped financial instruments (i.e., MERESF) and will generate capacity in rural population to access existing financial services. This will help to reduce poverty and vulnerability by increasing access to credit, improving business development, microfinancing and increased participation specific Climate Resident Value Chains (CRVC).

202. Available resources from the international community are also limited. Peru received USD 923 million in climate-related official development assistance (ODA) between 2015-2018. The largest share of this funding was channeled to the agriculture (including forestry and livestock) sector sub-sections. It is possible that other ODA channels could be used for climate change adaptation and mitigation activities, but these are limited in scope and size.

The project will contribute to reducing the vulnerability of smallholder farmers and their families to climate change by increasing access to credit, improving business development, microfinancing and increased participation specific Climate Resident Value Chains (CRVC).

203. Private sector financing of CCAs in the puna has been almost absent so far (see section 7.2.9 in the Feasibility Study). The main reason is that the project area is characterized by high poverty levels, low income per capita and limited access to financial services.

The project will contribute to reducing the vulnerability of smallholder farmers and their families to climate change by increasing access to credit, improving business development, microfinancing and increased participation specific Climate Resident Value Chains (CRVC).

**GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 61**

B

revenue streams from Local Initiatives. A detailed financial analysis has been conducted (see Annex 3) to determine the financial IRR of Local initiatives in the three windows of the Facility, with and without GCF grants. The analysis is based on a set of assumptions that have been developed for each window. These assumptions are used to identify Local initiatives to be funded by the Facility. Detailed assumptions were both for business-as-usual scenarios (i.e., no Facility funding) as well as for scenarios where the Facility provides funding. The transition from business-as-usual to the Facility scenario is assumed to be achieved through a combination of financial and technical assistance provided by the Facility (for alpaca breeding in the Non-repayable Grant Sub-Window) or 25% native potatoes in the Results-based Repayment Sub-Window). This section provides an overview of these assumptions, as well as the rationale behind them, along with target beneficiaries, for the following reasons:

I) In the Repayable Grant Sub-Window: (i) Beneficiaries have limited savings or no savings and are living in poverty to the extent of often abandoning the high plains and moving into urban areas in search of jobs (see section D.4.2). They have virtually no access to financial services, and therefore, they cannot afford to pay interest rates on loans. The Facility will implement the transition to EAS/CRC (in native potatoes and alpacas, respectively). (ii) Beneficiaries have limited savings or no savings and are living in poverty to the extent of often abandoning the high plains and moving into urban areas in search of jobs (see section D.4.2). They have virtually no access to financial services, and therefore, they cannot afford to pay interest rates on loans. The Facility will implement the transition to EAS/CRC (in native potatoes and alpacas, respectively). (iii) Beneficiaries have limited savings or no savings and are living in poverty to the extent of often abandoning the high plains and moving into urban areas in search of jobs (see section D.4.2). They have virtually no access to financial services, and therefore, they cannot afford to pay interest rates on loans. The Facility will implement the transition to EAS/CRC (in native potatoes and alpacas, respectively).

II) In the Results-based Repayable Grant Sub-Window: (i) beneficiaries have limited savings, but certainly not to the tune of the EUR 257,000 and EUR 268,000 per farm group required to implement the transition to EAS/CRC. They are living in poverty to the extent that they often abandon their farms and move into urban areas (see section D.4.3). The Facility will implement the transition to EAS/CRC (in native potatoes and alpacas, respectively).

It is offered by micro-finance institutions at prohibitive rates and for short maturities that are unsuitable to the tenure of the assets being financed. In addition, these loans are not collateralized and therefore, they do not provide resources pay for technical services provided by private experts in order to gain that knowledge. Considering the limited savings of the beneficiaries, it is unlikely that they will be able to repay such loans on a commercial concessional. To mitigate concessionality, a repayable grant is therefore introduced, with the benefit that (a) repayment terms are negotiated at Initial loan agreement subject to the Facility’s conditions and those of MFIs and other financial institutions; (b) repayment terms are negotiated at Initial loan agreement subject to the Facility’s conditions and those of MFIs and other financial institutions; (c) repayment terms are negotiated at Initial loan agreement subject to the Facility’s conditions and those of MFIs and other financial institutions.

III) In the Agribusiness Matchmaking Sub-Window, for which only existing formal business entities qualify, a very significant proportion of these businesses have limited savings or no savings. The majority of the beneficiaries are formalized and may have some access to finance; it is not nor this sort of amounts and would carry the risk of default. In addition, the repayment terms are often prohibitive and therefore, less concessional. The program will provide non-repayable grants covering 80% of the value chain portion of the investment (e.g., a contractor providing inputs to an existing farm business). This is because the repayable grant is offered at prohibitive and therefore less concessional. It will cover 80% of the EDA portion of the investment, will carry no interest or maturity date, and will be provided by the Facility. The remaining 20% of the investment will be provided as assistance provided by Agrodeva and GCF and will put these businesses on a solid footing, increasing their resilience to future shocks.

The program strongly contributes to the GCF identified sectoral paradigm shift enablers. The government of Peru sees CCA/REDD+ as an opportunity for economic development and poverty reduction. However, it is not yet required to face the challenge of climate change adaptation and mitigation in the loss of ecosystems and their climate services. The GCF funding is required to contribute to the following: (a) the transition from a focus on short-term financial returns to a longer term perspective; (b) the government has pledged its commitment, focusing on the short-term productivity aspects, neglecting the value of the ecosystem services it would provide and the long-term benefits that could be derived from these investments in agribusiness. It would focus on isolated initiatives and activities financed from different programs [Agrifood, Agrodeva, etc.] and not as part of a broader strategy for climate change adaptation and mitigation.

The decision-making process will also be limited and CSOs that have gained the trust and engagement of communities through their work in other sectors (e.g., water management) would be better placed to engage with communities. The decision-making process would continue being innovative on paper but non-functional on the ground and little investment would reach target communities. ## B.6 Exit Strategy (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

### B.6.1 Replicability

207. Many of the proposed interventions are already replicating successful experiences, such as: (i) the incorporation of ancestral EEA practices and experience on local implementation in the high Andes from Nor Yauyos Cochas - Cochabamba; (ii) the replication of the model for the promotion of agroforestry systems in the Amazon region by Agrosilvopastoral Businesses on agricultural development with focus on specific value chains (South America: camelized fiber and Andean avocado, Central America: coffee and cocoa); (iii) the replication of the model for the promotion of agroecological environmental and climate funds (including GCF funds), and engaging with private stakeholders for PES schemes. These experiences are already being replicated in other regions.

209. The model for replication is compiled by (i) focused intervention on priority landscapes/watersheds where relevant actors have been identified, including the identification of key actors and their roles; (ii) provision of technical assistance; (iii) increased investment from private sources and iv) improved planning and coordination at the landscape level. These interventions will be scaled up to other regions with similar ecosystems and climate vulnerability conditions (e.g., June 2018).

In addition, the project will serve as an example of a successful initiative aimed at promoting and facilitating the adoption of agroecological practices in the Andean region. The project will also contribute to increasing ecosystem levels within the landscape and fostering stakeholder engagement in the project's focus area. It will foster the ability to implement similar projects in other regions with similar ecosystems in the broader regional context.

### B.6.2 Sustainability

111. Improved ecosystem services, climate resilient livelihoods and increased access to public and private finance will be achieved through the following components: (i) Climate Resilient Value Chains (CRVC); (ii) EEA measures; and (iii) CRVC resources. The economies that will be requested from the GCF will enable expected results. The results will ensure the sustainability of the project's focus area, as well as the replication of the model in other regions with similar ecosystems.

The exit strategy is embedded into the project design. The project, through its various components, will support both the financial sustainability (component 2), and behavioral changes (components 1 and 3).

### Financial Sustainability

Crucially, all local initiatives financed by the Pura Facility will be profitable in the long-term and generating positive returns for their beneficiaries. This is because: (i) the average cost of implementing EEA measures and CRVCs ranges from 4% to 25% (e.g., apioca breeding in the subsistence window); (ii) native potatoes are the most profitable crop, with a return on investment ranging between 10-30%; and (iii) the successful implementation for the reasons described in section B.5, once beneficiaries have implemented EEA measures and CRVCs, they will be able to generate income from these activities that will significantly increase their incomes.

In addition, financial investments required for EEA measures and CRVC will not be needed in repeat cycles within their entity. In the future, EEA measures, once implemented, only need regular and relatively low maintenance costs (e.g., 1% of the initial investment). Similarly, CRVCs are expected to be profitable (greenhouses) and will not need to be replaced in the future; but it represents a minority of the overall EEA and CRVC investments. The financial sustainability of the project is therefore expected to be such that beneficiaries no longer need grants at the time of capital replacement but will be able to continue generating income from their activities, which will allow them to finance other agroecological or climate-smart initiatives.

Locally-based applicants will be inherently more creditworthy and therefore bankable after the successful implementation of EEA measures and CRVCs; not only because they are on a sounder financial footing, but also because they have demonstrated their ability to generate income. This will make it easier for banks that businesses that benefited from repayable grants under the applicable window will have a track record of operational success.

The technical assistance provided by the Pura Facility, in addition to the financial incentives, will be critical in the long-term sustainability of the project's focus area and its replication in other regions with similar target markets (i.e., eligible under the Non-repayable Grant Sub-Window), will receive comprehensive

D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

### D.1 Impact Potential - Adaptation

204 Confirmed adaptation to climate-resilient sustainable development: The project has an integrated vision of promoting EEA at landscape level through:
- On-the-ground activities in Component 1, including the establishment of a financial buffer fund for water users and farmers, and regulation for upstream, middle stream, and downstream commodities, fodder production for South American camelids, and the promotion of climate-resilient agricultural practices to reduce risks of drought and reduced water availability due to climate change;
- The establishment of a financial buffer fund for water users and farmers in Component 1, which will be integrated into existing public MIDAGRI programs executed under Component 1, while providing a framework for scaling up the project’s activities at landscape level.

209 Integration of EEA in territorial planning and consensus-building processes through participatory dialogue platforms with stakeholders from different sectors (agriculture, water resources management, etc.), including the activities of the different stakeholders intervening in various ecological roles of the watershed and river basin. These platforms will be established by Component 1, involving local communities, associations, cooperatives, MIDAGRI, community enterprises and producers’ organizations.

209 In addition to the above, the project will conduct a baseline study and develop estimating the potential impacts of the different EEA interventions results in improving the resilience of water resources for agriculture and livestock. The project will also conduct an assessment of the use of water from the sites of the pilot more broadly (the full results can be found in Annex 2g).

### D.2 Ecosystems Improved

209 The project aims to conserve and restore 23,814 hectares of Puna ecosystems that High Andean livelihoods depend upon. The project will provide direct interventions to maximize the landscape and ecosystem services provided by these ecosystems, which are essential for the conservation of water resources and ecosystem services as an adaptation and mitigation strategy. The local impact will be monitored by the project through a participatory monitoring system in the pilot sites.

210 As part of the support provided to strengthen intra- and intersectoral collaboration among MIDAGRI and MINAM planning, the project will develop a baseline study on climate change impacts and water resources for agriculture and livestock. The results will be integrated into existing monitoring systems in the ministeries which are being hosted by MIDAGRI and/or MINAM.

### D.3 Direct and Indirect Benefits

221 The total estimated number of project direct beneficiaries is 40,715 people (of which 38,056 are women). These individuals will be directly supported through the implementation of the project activities in Spanish. Considering the numbers, the proposed GCF project targets Quechua Indigenous Peoples and Ladino communities living in the Puna region of Peru, who have a high vulnerability to climate change and face significant climate-related challenges. In addition, this population are part of the most exposed groups according to the Human Development Index (HDI), aligning with the broader trend in Peru where higher altitudes correlate with lower HDI values.

222 Following the GCF RMF, the number of direct beneficiaries were calculated including individuals who will be directly supported through the implementation of the project activities in Spanish. The total estimated number of direct beneficiaries is 40,715 people (of which 38,056 are women) from a GCF-funded intervention. The estimation of these 'direct beneficiaries' accounts therefore for:
a) Individuals that will be directly supported through the implementation of project activities in Spanish, multiplied by the average number of members of the groups/legal entities supported and the average membership per group/legal entity.
b) Individuals that will be supported by MIDAGRIS co-finance programs, multiplied by the average membership per group/legal entity.
c) Individuals that will receive technical assistance from the GCF-funded intervention (e.g., trainings, support to access credit facilities), multiplied by the average number of individuals per group/legal entity.

223 The project will also indirectly benefit 2,011,856 people (of which 1,905,928 are women) this includes those that do not receive targeted support from the GCF-funded intervention but will benefit from water

## D2. Paradigms shift (minimal, 500 words, approximately 1 page)

**224:** Changing the paradigm of highly threatened Puna ecosystems requires a transformation of behaviours and practices in communities that are dependent on these ecosystems. This will enable the shifting of native communities in the SHAP from a state characterized by climate vulnerability, lack of opportunities, and economic dependency to one where they have access to improved livelihoods, increased resilience, and improved economic productivity. This project incorporates the transformative pathway of promoting resilient land management practices that are based on ecosystem services and co-benefits. The EBA solutions with its mitigation co-benefit in priority landscapes can conserve essential ecosystem services allowing for a more sustainable use of resources by communities. These practices will be led by the people who generate the improvement of ecosystems that contribute to water regulation, and will be implemented at scale through community-based action plans on a landscape basis.

**225:** The combined results of improved farming and husbandry practices (by promoting and incorporating EBA measures) with the co-benefit of ecosystem services (water regulation), access to finance for improved planning and coordination - will increase the overall resilience of the Andean communities, while also improving water regulation and maintaining important stocks for global carbon regulation.

## D2.3 Overall contribution to climate-resilient development pathways consistent with relevant national climate change adaptation strategies and plans

**226:** Climate innovation will be catalysed through improved ecosystem management and target Climate Resilience Value Chain (CRVC) actors in the Andes region. The project will also contribute to the implementation of complementary support by public and private finance. An inter-sectoral transformational change is sustained by a combination of technical, financial, and policy interventions that are supported by MIDAGRI although many times they are in the same landscape. The project, hence, seeks to enhance opportunities for Andean communities to improve their livelihoods through improved land management practices that transform vulnerable communities' businesses and their usual production system into more sustainable and resilient ones. This will be achieved by providing technical support to communities to implement EBA solutions through MIDAGRI's funds from Agrodesis to strengthen producers, organizations and their business ideas, as well as complementing the project with other public and private finance. The project also seeks to provide technical support to develop complementary interventions to the program in order to create climate-resilient livelihoods that are based on ecosystem services and co-benefits. This will be achieved by providing MIDAGRI in meeting its productivity and competitiveness commitments while complying with Peru's NDC targets.

## D2.2 Potential for upscaling and replication

**228:** The potential for upscaling and replication is using mobilization of investments at scale. The project aims at aligning and upscaling public and private investment for EBA and Climate Resilient Value Chain (CRVC) actors in the Andes region. This will be achieved by providing technical support to communities putting in place the enabling conditions for additional long-term financing under Component 2 and 3. Sustainable land management practices that are based on ecosystem services and co-benefits will also put in place the Puna ecosystems that will add to the conducive transformational environment. The technical support provided by MIDAGRI, Agrodesis, and other public and private actors will be complemented with nature-based solutions' investment projects, business development, product transformation, and value addition. This will be achieved through a combination of financial and policy interventions that will offer the option of leveraging public funds from MERESME schemes and subnational governments to the areas where investments are needed. The project also seeks to provide technical support to communities and stakeholders (Poroparque, MIDAGRI, microfinance institutions) which will enhance the potential for upscaling and replication.

## D2.3 Potential of knowledge and learning

**227:** Knowledge and learning will be promoted, enhanced and scaled through several alliances at different levels that will provide technical support to communities to support the experiences' replication in other High-Andean communities. Therefore, at local level, transfer of knowledge between target areas, integration of new practices, and scaling up of successful experiences are expected. At national level, the exchange of good ground will set the stage to expand the EBA approach to communities with similar environmental and development characteristics. The project will focus on strengthening multi-sectorial institutional capacities and

## D2.2 Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment

### D2.2.1 The project will contribute to the creation of an enabling environment that relies on transdisciplinary planning and programming to scale-up EBA.

This will be achieved by integrating community-responsive technologies, ensuring the involvement of local communities in the design and implementation of EBA, and enhancing the provision of key ecosystem services for the local population and communities downstream. Community participation is a key element of the project's approach, as it ensures that the needs and priorities of local populations are taken into account.

### D2.2.2 The project will support the development of a methodology for calculating carbon in the Ruma ecosystem and to lay the groundwork for carbon accounting and credit systems for future projects.

#### D2.3 Contribution of the regulatory framework and policies

The project will contribute to the creation of an enabling environment with other key sectors and actors in the territory. In this context, the project activities will support the mainstreaming of EBA and gender equality into relevant sectoral strategies and plans. The project will also promote intersectoral collaboration within MIDAGRI and SERNAMP; to ensure synergies in the same landscape.

The project will contribute to the creation of an enabling environment by promoting a participatory approach in the agricultural sector and the territory, and strengthening of the national long-term monitoring system for EBA. The project will also support the development of methodologies for monitoring populations, in order to have monitoring protocols and measurement methodologies consistent with the impacts of EBA on human health (e.g., water quality, air quality, food security, biodiversity, and agriculture). It will also support monitoring systems that provide information on EBA interventions on water resources and their impact on adaptation responses; such as those developed by MIDAGRI, SERNAMP, ANA, INAGEG and SUNASIS.

### D3. Sustainable development impacts (max 100 words, approximately 1 page)

#### D3.1 Environmental co-benefits

The project will contribute to the creation of an enabling environment for sustainable development. First co-benefit is the maintenance of ecosystem services in the area benefited from the conservation, restoration or sustainable management of ecosystems. This includes: a) Provisioning services such as food and water; b) Regulating services that include climate regulation, air quality regulation, water purification, flood control, pest control, pollination, nutrient cycling, greenhouse gases, climatic processes, water storage, groundwater recharge and discharge, retention, recovery and removal of excess nutrients and pollutants and pest control protecting the underlying soils from erosion. In Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) terms this is linked to SDG 2 (Food Security), SDG 6 (Water recycling and carbon storage). c) Cultural services present in the SHP are educational, recreational, non-material and spiritual. d) Supporting services such as habitat for biodiversity, pollination, nutrient cycling, soil formation and maintenance, pest control, seed dispersal, and pollination. e) Provisioning ecosystem services through the three project components: f) Implementation of Etaak interventions and training on sustainable management practices in livestock and crops; g) Training on sustainable land management practices in livestock and crops; h) Contribution to carbon sequestration in soil and vegetation.

#### D3.2 Sustainable development impacts

The project will contribute to the creation of an enabling environment for sustainable development by avoiding GHG emissions, as well as reducing them through conservation, restoration and sustainable management of ecosystems. The project will also support the implementation of EBA and gender equality into relevant sectoral strategies and plans.

Management practices in livestock and crops, and contribute to carbon sequestration in soil and vegetation. Through integrated land use planning, the project will promote the adoption of agroforestry systems, wetland restoration and conservation. GHG reductions are calculated using the methodology presented in the FAO “CDM Project Design Document for LUCDD”, approved through the UN-REDD (version 1.0) and by FAO. The overall avoided impact is estimated at approximately 407,657 tCO2e over a period of 15 years analysed, which include five years of implementation phase and ten years of capitalization phase.

### 3.2 Social co-benefits including health co-benefits

The project will have as a social co-benefit the awareness raising and capacity building of local stakeholders on climate change adaptation, gender equality and general CCA and GCRV are mainstreamed into all components to strengthen capacities to integrate climate change adaptation into planning and decision-making processes. The activities contributing to this social co-benefit include:

### 3.3 Economic co-benefits

The economic activities of the project will support the achievement of the Government’s 7th roundment of local economic development plan (LEDP). The economic co-benefit to be measured is the number of producers, out of whom 50% are women who have confirmed an improvement in their income and/or access to markets. This co-benefit will be measured through the following activities:

1) Activity 1.1.2 will contribute to reduce climate risks supporting the implementation of sustainable opportunities.
2) Activity 1.1.3 will give technical assistance for implementation and promote market linkages, which will help women producers to improve their livelihoods.
3) Activity 2.2.3 will contribute to reduce the barriers for finance to EWS and GCRV.

### 3.4 Gender-sensitive development co-benefit

The project includes a gender action plan within all the project activities that promote gender equality and enhance living conditions and economic opportunities of African women. This is achieved through associating women in decision-making processes, providing training on climate change adaptation, integrating gender issues in platforms of knowledge exchange for ancestral practices and increasing access to markets. The impact of this co-benefit will be measured by the number of women involved in EWS and GCRV measures.

### 3.5 Impact of designing local initiatives with a gender-sensitive approach

The impact of designing local initiatives with a gender-sensitive approach will reflect on the equally enhancing for men and women of benefits in the total economy. This will be measured in co-benefit 3.

# D. Economic and social benefits of the beneficiaries

## 241. Target groups and their vulnerability to climate change
The main direct beneficiaries of project interventions are local communities in the SHAP, including producers' associations, MSME cooperatives, community-based organizations (CBOs), and other smallholder farmers who are particularly vulnerable to climate change (MINAM, 2016). These local communities are among the most impoverished in the zone. They have limited access to productive resources such as land, water, seeds, tools, and credit, which confirms the general rule that in Peru the higher the altitude the lower the HDI (MINUDEP, 2010). This means that target local communities lack basic services such as road infrastructure, health care facilities, and access to markets. In addition, they are vulnerable due to their dependence on customary practices for managing the resources that support their livelihoods, which adds to their climate vulnerability.

To increase local income, the project will enhance training of local communities, including producers' associations, MSME cooperatives, CBOs, and other smallholder farmers in business management skills. In this context, the project will enhance training of local communities, including producers' associations, MSME cooperatives, CBOs, and other smallholder farmers in business management skills.

## 242. Lack of access to markets
Many communities in the SHAP face difficulties in accessing markets to sell their agricultural products. The project will address this issue by facilitating commercial networks, and supporting local communities, cooperatives, MSME community enterprises, and CBOs in developing value chains that are more competitive and better able to capture market growth. In order to address the need of better market access, the project will strengthen local value chains through improved production practices, increased linkages with buyers, and enhanced marketing strategies. The project will also improve Value Chains and improved access to finance and business development (including technical assistance in marketing, branding, customer outreach and certification of products). Technical support to local


---
**Page 77**
---

D.4.3 Financial needs

243. In order to address financial constraints, financial institutions perceive the investment risk of the agricultural sector as too high and rarely develop products and services adapted to the needs of these vulnerable populations. Where possible, they offer loans with a very high interest rate (up to 50 percent) or require collateral. These are often “express loans” with low or no requirements but daily rates of 10 to 40 percent (Pensamiento, 2013). Rural microfinance institutions (MFIs) have been created in the region to provide credit and other financial services to small farmers and rural people.

In the SHAP there are some of them that give micro loans to rural and small farmers i.e. Casa Rural Las Piedras, Cooperativa de Crédito y Ahorro “El Pueblo” and Cooperativa de Crédito y Ahorro “La Esperanza”. These small groups have access to credit for agriculture and livestock. Also, access of SHAP communities to matching public-private finance mechanism such as Agrodesarrollo, for the development of sustainable agri business plans is limited.

For instance, in 2014, only 35% of the total budget executed for approved business plans towards technological adoption have been channelized to the cooperatives. The SHAP has identified two main challenges: a) lack of capacity and resources to support target communities in accessing funds earmarked by MIDAGRI for EAs activities (Agrodesarrollo, AgriRural, MINAM), and b) lack of technical assistance from MIDAGRI to help the cooperatives and other population to access the government support and to capitalize producers and local governments to disseminate the benefits of the EAs. To address these challenges, the SHAP will provide: a) capacity building for cooperatives, communities and other legally established organizations to be serialized on the importance of EAs, their implementation and maintenance practices and supported in the development of Climate Resilient Value Chains (CRVCs); b) technical assistance to MFI’s for them to expand their offers in green products and on-the other hand expanded access to credit for producers and communities; c) capacity building for cooperatives, communities and other legally established organizations to be serialized on the importance of EAs, their implementation and maintenance practices and supported in the development of Climate Resilient Value Chains (CRVCs); d) technical assistance to MFI’s for them to expand their offers in green products and on-the other hand expanded access to credit for producers and communities.

D.4.4 Institutional needs

244. Access to MIDAGRI programs requires improved association. Producers’ associations and unions will be supported by the project to ensure that cooperatives are eligible to access public programs (Pensamiento, 2014). The project will therefore promote and support association through technical assistance in the areas of governance, financial management, human resources development, marketing and strengthen business management for the adoption of climate resilient and environmentally sustainable agriculture practices. In addition, the project will provide capacity building to producers’ associations, cooperatives, communities and other legally established organizations on the importance of EAs, their implementation and maintenance practices and supported in the development of Climate Resilient Value Chains (CRVCs).

245. Public institutional capacity and coordination strengthened towards achieving MIDAGRI’s MINAM’s EAs policy implementation in a more coordinated manner, mainstreaming EAs gender, intercultural and territorial dimensions, ensuring that EAs are implemented in an integrated way with other climate change adaptation and impact projects to climate resilience and reduced emissions in the SHAP. Improved coordination with other measures will be achieved through capacity building and technical assistance in meeting its GGO targets.

D.5 Country ownership (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

D.5.1 Country prioritizing of the project

246. Peru has been a key player in the process of NDCs to be presented at the COP21 and has full support of the Peruvian government, through the Ministry of Economy and Finance which is the NDA, the Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock (MINAGRI) which is the lead agency for climate change adaptation in Peru. This leads which climate change action in Peru. More recently, during the national day on the Celebration of the Peruvian Forests, the President of Peru announced that Peru will present a new NDC to the COP21 project as part of the relevant programmes that the country is boosting to support climate change adaptation in the high Andes.

D.5.2 Coherence with existing National Climate Strategy, policies and commitments

247. The project will be a major contribution to identified Peru’s NDC targets. The project will support this through: i) AgriRural Natural grassland management to secure feed for livestock and reduce vulnerability to climate change; ii) AgriRural Management of wild South American camels (vicuñas).
