# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 75

## 258. **Regional Water Fund in Peru**

The Regional Water Fund as one of the main EEs has been accredited by the GCF since 2016. For its accreditation process, ProLitoralene received technical support from a GCF Readiness Project with Giz as delivery partner to build capacity and prepare the project proposal. The project is implemented in the Department of Madre de Dios (DMD) and will be carried out by the Ministry of Environment (MINAM), the National Service for Wetlands in the Province of Darién del Marañón (FPO01) with ProLitoralene as the EE managing a total amount of $8,972,400 of GCF funds. The project will be implemented through the Direct Access Entity (DAE) (EP35), with ProLitoralene as the Direct Access Entity managing a total amount of $8,972,400 of GCF funds. In addition to the DAE, the project will also have a Project Facilitator (PF) (EP36). The PF will be key, namely a Regional Water Fund in Peru, a competitive fund aimed at PES for high Andean ecosystems and water resources management. The PF will provide technical support to the DAE and Project Facility established for this project and in bringing its experience in managing PES and MERESI funds both nationally and internationally.

## 259. **SERNANP**

SERNANP is the Government entity, attached to the Ministry of Environment (MINAM) in charge of the management of protected areas in Peru. SERNANP has a long history of experience in PES projects carried out and has experience on project implementation in the NFPA. In the past, it has implemented projects with ProLitoralene as the EE in the buffer zone of the Manu National Park (MNP) and has supported project activities within the MNP and buffer zones, ensuring its efficient execution and the articulation of these activities with other actors.

## 260. **Institute of Montaña**

The Institute of Montaña is a non-profit organization that works for the conservation of the natural, cultural and social heritage of the Andean region in Peru. It has experience in PES projects implemented in Peru and they have and are currently implementing programs in the highlands of Ancash, Puno, Junín and Lima.

## 261. **Additional information on the AE and EE capacities to implement the project**

The additional information on the AE and EE capacities to implement the project is provided in Section E.4.

### D.5 Role of the Ministry of Economy and Finance (INDA)

#### 262. **Role of the Ministry of Economy and Finance, as Peru’s NDA**

The Ministry of Economy and Finance, as Peru's NDA, has been involved in the process of development and approval of the project proposal. The NDA has provided technical support to ProLitoralene as the project proponent, along with ProLitoralene, MINAM and Giz as the AE have introduced the project idea to the NDA during the Project Development Proposal stage. The NDA has also been involved in the process of development during the Funding Proposal development stage participating for example in a workshop held in June 2023 on the selection of the GCF project proposal. The NDA will continue to provide technical support and guidance for project selection by the NDA.

### D.5 Engagement with other organizations and relevant other stakeholders

#### 263. **Several consultations in the target areas have been carried out**

Several consultations in the target areas have been carried out by Giz in close coordination with project partners, including MINAM, SERNANP, and the National Water and Funding Proposal development. A list of the consultations held can be found in Annex 7.

#### 264. **National level meetings were held with different stakeholders**

At national level, meetings were held with national government programs that operate in the territory, such as MINAM, SERNANP, and other relevant organizations. These meetings were also attended by representatives of the SENANAP. The project development team engaged for example with cooperatives, with other non-governmental organizations (NGOs), with local communities, with the National Water Center (CENPO) (Small Grants FEG program) to integrate lessons learned and best practices into the project design. In addition, meetings were held with the National Water Center (CENPO), the National Forest Centre (CCF), DESCODUR and CONDESAN (National Infrastructure for Water Security Project) to participate in the development of the project proposal and to provide inputs on the integration of lessons learned and best practices into the project design.

#### 265. **More meetings during the Funding Proposal stage**

Meetings with national, regional and local stakeholders were carried out in October 2022, November 2022 and May 2023 by organizing different workshops to discuss the project proposal, its components, the expected results, potential impacts of the project and potential project risks and mitigation measures. As part of these consultations (May 2023), the project and development team also organized a workshop with local communities in the target areas that were further integrated into the project design. In general, the project was welcomed by the stakeholders, at submission stage, with 85% of the members of the main direct project beneficiaries (e.g., local communities) supporting the project proposal. The project received strong support from the communities (82% men) out of which 65% were members of the main potential direct project beneficiaries (e.g., local communities). These consultations were organized and coordinated by MIDGRAN and SERNANP with local stakeholders to ensure the participation of community-based associations, women’s groups and elderly people.

## D.6 Efficiency and effectiveness (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

### 265. Cost-effectiveness

The project has been widely seen as a cost-effective approach to address the challenges communities face when dealing with climate change. A recent study that evaluated the ecosystem services provided by the project found that the total value of ecosystem services generated was valued at USD 400 (Mangin, 2017). Rotondo et al. (2017) analyzed the Puna ecosystems' impact by land use and climate change scenarios and concluded that the most significant changes will be in the provision of these key ES: long fallowing, soil amendment, conservation tillage, rotational grazing, perennial grasses, and agroforestry. The project has also been recognized as a cost-effective approach to address traditional livelihoods through the promotion of sustainable land management practices such as tree planting, crop breeding, climate change mitigation schemes and payment for ecosystem services, and adapted traditional livestock systems (Rotondo et al., 2017).

In it: the cost-benefit analysis for the management of native grasslands in Chinchaypuco and Matueles, Peru, indicated that the project has a net present value of USD 3.5 million per year over a period of 10-15 years (Riedl et al., 2017). Gamble and De Biere (2015) show that a subset of proposed ESA alternatives would provide significant benefits in terms of ecosystem services, with the highest benefit being infrastructure for water provision, with prices below USD 0.25/m³.

### 268. Financial Viability

The project will implement an integrated landscape approach to comprehensive obtain adaptation benefits and mitigation co-benefits. The project will support 60,715 people directly (40% men) and approximately 3 million people indirectly (50% women) through the maintenance of ecosystem services that sustain agriculture, water resources, and biodiversity in the Puna region. This will ensure that these ecosystems will be sustainable management, equivalent to a co-benefit of 467,875 t CO2e of avoided deforestation.

### D.6 Financial Viability

For further justification of the GCF grant, please refer to section B.3. As noted above in Annex A: The financial IRR of Local initiatives supported by the Puna Facility is positive even without grants; GCF grant will be used for technical assistance and capacity building. The project will also support the implementation of the Local initiatives for reasons including: subsistence nature of the beneficiaries in the non-commercial sector, lack of access to credit, and limited financial resources. The project will not fund the entire ESRAC investment, nor is limited access to finance to fund ESA measures and CCF; continue to provide technical assistance and capacity building. The project will also support the use of resources to hire technical experts in lieu of the Puna Facility's technical assistance. Importantly, the operation of the Puna Facility will be more efficient with the implementation of the Local initiatives under Agrobased Matching Sub-Windows through the repayment feature. All Local initiatives funded by the Puna Facility will be subject to a 10% matching requirement. The project will also support the use of resources to hire technical experts in lieu of the Puna Facility's technical assistance.

The economic NPV of the project is 8.2%, which is more than 6% (the social discount rate estimated by the World Bank and the Ministry of Economy and Finance). The economic NPV is positive even with extremely low CO2 costs and with a 35% drop in emission savings volume, as long as the emission savings volume and economic benefits are higher than the discount rate of 8%, and the economic NPV is therefore positive if the emission savings volume and economic benefits are higher than the discount rate of 8%. The project is economically more attractive even when the base case assumption of EUR 601.

#### D.6.3 Application of best available technologies and practices

The project will apply sustainable landscape management practices, soil amendments, ancestral and modern technologies for water and soil conservation, exclusion zones and communal reserves, among other measures to improve land productivity and resilience. The project will also promote innovation through the investment in Climate Resilient Value Chains, which integrate resilient practices and technologies that are adapted to local conditions and climate change impacts. These include the use of season for distribution in times of drought by supplementing water from rainfall. This, together with efficient irrigation technologies, will improve and make sustainable use of water resources as an example for fodder production.

## Activity 1.1.1: Setting up a basis for financing and implementing EEA measures and Climate Resilient Value Chains

A communication strategy will be created to develop awareness about the project and create the basis for the implementation of EEA measures and CRVC through the Puna Facility. Technical assistance for financial planning and preparation of proposals will be provided in order to participate in the call for proposals.

### Sub-activity 1.1.1.1: Inform and identify communities, associations, cooperatives and Civil Society Organisations interested in participating

* **4** Community, Cooperatives and/or small and medium enterprises supported in 1.1.1.2 selection
* **1** Training for project staff on standardization implementation
* **1** Guideline (including participatory methodology) developed for site-specific climate diagnostics and preparation of intervention plans

### Sub-activity 1.1.1.2: Development of site-specific climate diagnostics and preparation of participatory intervention plans

* **4** Class 100 Local initiative project plans
* **3** Trainings for project staff on site-specific climate diagnostics implemented

## Activity 1.1.2: Financing and implementing of EEA measures and Climate Resilient Value Chains

This activity will focus on promoting climate-resilient Puna ecosystems and CRVC through the implementation of climate-focused Local initiatives at local landscape level. The activity will be carried out in collaboration with the Puna Facility (established under Activity 2.1.1) and through public sub-activity 1.1.2.1: Implementation of Local initiatives financed by the Puna Facility

* **127** Local initiative Grant Agreements signed

### Sub-activity 1.1.2.2: Financial support through MIDAGRI

**Page 101**
---

E

### Sub-activity 1.1.3 Technical assistance for implementing EBA measures and Climate Resilient Value Chains at landscape level

This activity will focus on providing technical assistance for implementing EBA measures and CRVC through MIDAGRI and the Pura Facility and enhancing market access and economic opportunities for the Climate Resilient and Sustainable Value Chains.

- At least 8 public investment projects implemented
- At least 15 tailored TA packages offered
- At least 647 producers trained
- Digital platform for sale of products operating

### Sub-activity 1.2.1 Recover, document and scale up ancestral knowledge and practices

This activity seeks to develop and strengthen the capacities of communities, local experts (called yachaychos or karmayos), and technical staff in order to recover, document, and disseminate ancestral knowledge and land management practices, increase the implementation of ancestral water and land management practices, and promote intergenerational transmission. The project will build on existing ancestral practices, knowledge and innovation with building intergenerational transmission of ancestral practices, knowledge and innovation in a context of change (climate change) while maintaining appreciation of people's own values, knowledge and practices.

- At least 912 local experts (273 yachaychos or karmayos) capacitated
- At least 4 communication materials in native languages on lessons learned from ancestral practices and innovation are produced
- At least 5 dissemination events about lessons learned from ancestors

### Sub-activity 1.2.1.1 Capacity building of local experts for the transfer of ancestral knowledge and land management practices, and promotion of Climate Resilient Value Chains (CRVC) measures

### Sub-activity 1.2.1.2 Production and dissemination of information materials on ancestral knowledge and land management practices and innovation in a context of change

## Private Sector Engagement Strategy, including criteria for selecting companies in which advocacy actions will be deployed (for example, companies committed to social and environmental standards), criteria for selection of sites related to CRVCs, criteria for selecting demonstration sites, and an effective communication strategy for engaging with the private sector. Finally, in order to increase the use of state funds for investments in climate change adaptation projects, it is necessary to mobilise resources from public programmes for the High Adaptation Fund (HAF) and the National Climate Fund. To this end, access to and alignment of investments from MIDAGRI and MINAM will be sought through the development of improved instructions for access to public financing.

### Activity 2.1.2
**Strengthen capacities to develop and implement mechanisms for EBA in the Andean region**

The activity will seek to overcome the barriers by public authorities to the implementation of EBA, including developing a mechanism for retribution for ecosystem services (MERES) and strengthening the capacity of stakeholders' capacities to identify and overcome bottlenecks and constraints. A technical assistance package will be developed in close coordination with the Ministry of Environment and the National Superintendence of Water Supply and Sanitation of Peru (SANSP).

### Activity 2.1.3
**Support the greening of microlines and business models, to promote EBA**

This activity will focus on two main topics: (1) Supporting the greening of microlines and business models and (2) supporting the dissemination of green financial products and services.

## Sub-activity 2.1.3.1
**Promote access and alignment of public investments and other government programmes for EBA and Climate Resilient Value Chains (CRVC) measures**

**Technical assistance to improve existing hydrological MERES processes in the project areas**

### Sub-activity 2.1.3.1.2

**Support to financial institutions in greening microcredits**

At least 2 business model EBA and CRVC by specific criteria according to the Project

### Activity 3.1.1 Strengthen the capacities of relevant national, regional and local planning and governance processes integrated EBA and climate resilience

The new approaches that will be promoted by Resilience Puna require progressive development of capacities of implementers, including government officials, to ensure replicability and sustainability of the changes introduced by the project. Therefore, in order to mainstream EBA and climate resilience into national, regional and local development plans, including budgets and investment projects at all levels (national, regional and local) will be strengthened and gender perspectives will be considered.

### Sub-activity 3.1.1.1 Strengthen the capacities of relevant national, regional and local planning and governance processes integrated EBA and Climate Resilient Value Chains

#### Sectional and sectorial platforms for the articulation and scaling up of EBA measures
* At least 605 (182 men/423 women) trained
* Online and/or presentational training materials developed to mainstream EBA and CVIC business models developed

## Activity 3.1.2 Strengthening regulatory frameworks and M&E systems at national level

The activity aims to provide technical support to MIDAGRI and MINAM to improve the regulatory framework for the promotion of EBA measures in the Andean areas of the country. In addition, it will provide technical assistance to these institutions as well as to other government entities to measure impact of EBA interventions.

### Sub-activity 3.1.2.1 Improving national regulatory frameworks linked to EBA measures and Climate Resilient Value Chains

The activity aims to strengthen participatory sectoral and territorial platforms so that the project EBA measures and CVCR developed by project beneficiaries can be replicated and scaled up. Lessons learned from the pilot phase will inform research work and CIVIC planning, and implementation will be integrated.

### Sub-activity 3.1.2.1 Integration of EBA measures and Climate Resilient Value Chains within the scope of the project

* **Sub-activity 3.1.2.1.1 Framework which includes the promotion of EBA and CVCR interventions in the Andean areas of**

### E.7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

#### 272. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements
- Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (in addition to the ESMP, PLECP and GAF) will comply with the relevant GCF policies as stipulated in the GCF Evaluation Policy, Principles, Guidelines and Requirements.
- The project will apply a customized results-based monitoring and evaluation (M&E) system. The M&E system will track project inputs, activities and sub-activities. Outputs and impacts, as well as associated financial flows across all outputs in all project interventions areas.

#### 273. These arrangements include:
- GIZ Standard Operating Procedures ("GIZ's evaluation policy - principles, guidelines and requirements")
- Monitoring and evaluation framework
- Integrated results management framework and logical framework
- Implementation of the M&E system
- Requirements of GCF's Annual Performance Report
- Local monitoring and reporting

#### 274. Procedures and requirements of project partners and stakeholders in Peru and at national level:
- The project will be implemented through a digital tool (e.g., dashboard) for the project M&E system that will follow-up on progress of all results, indicators Logframe activities and outputs.
- Access to EBI will be provided to collect data generated by the implementation of their activities and sub-activities. Additionally, Proyecto de EE will support monitoring and evaluation through the use of a digital tool (e.g., dashboard) for the project M&E system that will follow-up on progress of all results, indicators Logframe activities and outputs.
- As input to feed the project M&E System: Instituto de Mitigación will support this work through the implementation and strengthening of community-based monitoring systems. The project partners will be responsible for collecting data in the field using an app that can be connected to a data-driven information management system which will also be a module of the project M&E system.

## Project M&E System

| Figure 18: Project M&E system |
| --- |

### Recruitment of M&E staff

277. Immediately after the project commences, GIZ's GCF AE will make available one expert (with gender expertise) to oversee and coordinate the project M&E and reporting routines. As soon as GCF project procedure becomes available in Pura, the project will recruit a M&E specialist. The specialist will work closely together with the GCF AE and the Project Manager to ensure that all required data is collected and reported on time according to the requirements of the system when requested. In addition, the experts will maintain the Cloud Database, and additional special inputs may be required for certain technical areas.

### Independent monitoring and evaluation studies

278. Monitoring and evaluation are carried out by an independent unit with a mid-term evaluation and a final evaluation of the project. GIZ evaluations are carried out by GIZ's independent evaluation unit. This unit is steered by a corporate unit that is separate from operational business and reports directly to the Management Board.


---
**Page 109**
---

GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 105

E

Moreover, evaluations are conducted with the support of external evaluators. GCF applies the Evaluation criteria for German bilateral development cooperation (GIZ) on a standards basis in project evaluations. These are based on the six evaluation criteria agreed by the Development Assistance Committee (DAC) of the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development (OECD): relevance, effectiveness, efficiency, overarching development results (impact), sustainability, and coherence with respect to international commitments and national strategies. The GCF also applies the OECD DAC principles on good governance, ownership, innovativeness in results areas, replication and scalability as well as unexpected results.

278. GCF encourages active participation of stakeholders throughout project implementation. This includes stakeholder engagement and participation. All provisions for upholding the GCF evaluation principles described in Chapter V (a-d) of the Evaluation Policy are followed (see DAC standards and DeGIZ evaluation standards). In line with the OECD DAC principles on good governance, ownership, innovativeness in results areas, replication and scalability as well as unexpected results, the GCF will ensure that all stakeholders have a say. An interim Independent Evaluation Report and a Final Independent Evaluation Report will be submitted to the GCF as per time agreed in the funded activity programme.

280. GCF’s Evaluation Principle of Credibility and Robustness: As a matter of principle, GCF takes care to use an appropriate combination of quantitative and qualitative data for its evaluations.

To ensure robust evaluation, GCF uses a theory-based approach as a minimum standard for its central project evaluation. The contribution analysis has proven to be the most effective method in this regard. This is because it allows the evaluator to identify the expected results and the actual results that can be associated with a project. Furthermore, the analysis should increase the understanding and knowledge of what works, what does not work and why.

281. Promoting transparency: GCF will use a rating system for its evaluations. The ratings are based on a scale from 0 to 5 (see activity 1.2.3). A score of less than 100 points using a points system serve to increase the transparency of ratings, while enabling better comparability between individual projects.

282. Results of each program evaluation should be reflected within the project design, in influencing uptake, and improve overall project implementation (adaptive management).

E.7.4 Data collection and frequency

The PMU will ensure data collection for implemented activities through responsible executing parties GIZ, MIDAGRI, SERNAP Progrante and Instituto de Montaña in charge of leading the monitoring activities at local level. The PMU will supervise and guide the monitoring and evaluation. The PMU will assign a monitoring officer based on PMU inputs to lead every commune and make a summary report attached to GCF. The PMU must meet once again at least every six months.

E.7.5 Measurement and Evaluation (M&E) of implemented Local initiatives through Puna Facility to evaluate increase of resilience of local communities

284. The M&E of implemented Local initiatives is an essential activity for monitoring the project’s efficiency and effectiveness as well as for continued learning across the project. Instituto de Montaña will support in the implementation of the community level M&E (for more information please see activity 1.2.2).

F. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

F.1. Identification of mitigation measures (max. 3 pages)

A detailed analysis of the project risks is provided in Chapter 10 of the Feasibility Study (Annex 2a). Environmental and social risks are considered in the Environmental and Social Impact Assessment (ESIA, Annex B4). Indigenous Peoples and Local Communities (IPLCs) are considered in the Indigenous Peoples and Local Communities Consultation Plan (IPCLCP) (ESMP: Annex 6b). Gender-specific risks are considered in the Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan (Annexes 2c and 2d).

Selected Risk Factor 1: Insufficient financing may put at risk the long-term sustainability of the public or private investment

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Medium |

Description

Insufficient public or private financial flows may put at risk the long term sustainability of the project. Market failure for EIA measures and Climate Resilient Value Chains (CRVC) in South America may be insufficient. Fluctuations in the market value of cash products (e.g., Southern American corn/maize, rubber) may limit economic activity and entrepreneurship.

From project onset, activities will focus on increasing engagement of private entities (hydrological generation enterprises, agricultural cooperatives, etc.) with the project. A financial strategy will be developed to ensure that sufficient resources are available for the project. A communication strategy will be developed to make the case for potential external losses in these industries if Punta ecosystems are not conserved. The project will also work closely with local communities and other stakeholders to promote the economic benefits of maintaining the functionality of the hydrological MERSEH scheme. The promotion of microfinancing for EIA will be done both on the offer and the demand side. On the offer side, financial institutions will be encouraged to provide financing for projects that comply with the project’s measures and/or Climate Resilient Value Chains they will continue promoting through their own channels. Local communities, agricultural cooperatives, etc., will be encouraged to access microfinance from financial institutions in order to promote development and access to finance (including financial literacy) to ensure successful income-generating activities. Income distribution will also be promoted as a way of ensuring that the benefits of the project are shared.

Selected Risk Factor 2: Local stakeholders are not sufficiently organized, engaged and interested in participating in the project

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | High |

Description

Participation from local stakeholders in the project activities is expected to be low as it is expected due to the lack of interest due to the lack of interest. This could happen due to various factors, e.g., not seeing the value of EIA measures or the perception that the EIA process will not lead to any tangible benefits. The project will seek to mitigate this risk through stakeholder engagement. In addition, the project will emphasize documentation and dissemination of information to facilitate capacity building and awareness raising on the importance of conservation efforts in the Amazon region.

Mitigation Measure(s)

The project has been designed in close cooperation with key local stakeholders, and local consultations with potential project beneficiaries have been carried out to ensure that their interests and views are being addressed in the project. Continuous engagement will be maintained throughout the project implementation phase through regular meetings and workshops. The engagement will be proactive and participatory, ensuring that all stakeholders feel involved and committed to the project. The project will develop adequate communication and engagement strategies to ensure reaching out and continuously engaging with local communities and other stakeholders during the project’s implementation phases and beyond. This will include continuous monitoring of stakeholder engagement levels and regular reporting on progress.

Selected Risk Factor 3: Coordination among regional government stakeholders is insufficient to sustain project results

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Low |

Description

Coordination among government stakeholders is insufficient and the lack of interest and ownership of the project may limit the sustainability of project results.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

The project is aligned with the priorities of the Government of Peru and has been developed in close coordination with Government institutions that have a key role in its implementation, e.g., MEGADRI and Profrutano. Extensive consultations are being carried out to ensure that all relevant stakeholders will be involved in the project design process.

The main objective of the project is to focus on strengthening multi-level governance to ensure that the national institutions will have the technical capacity to implement the project and achieve its goals.

### Selected Risk Factor 4: Risk of continuity due to political instability

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Low | Medium |

**Description**

The project is carried out on the assumption of sustained commitment from the Government of Peru to follow up NDC goals and from MEGADRI to continue its open and proactive engagement. Political instability in Peru may affect project continuity.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

MEGADRI has carried out a consistent engagement with the Government of Peru to support the Government’s access to good governance financing for this project and will continue to do so throughout project inception and implementation. This approach provides a general framework that can be adapted as needed, but if political instability in Peru affects the country’s policies or procedures, it can be evidenced from the recent change in administration without affecting the project construction process.

### Selected Risk Factor 5: Maladaptation and degradation of ecosystems and social structures

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Low |

**Description**

Improved economic opportunities in the SHAP may lead to increased pressure on ecosystems (e.g., a growth in cattle head numbers as a result of business-oriented approaches). Moreover, the support to certain Value chains such as crops may result in negative impacts for other species or ecosystem services. Social structures may continue to deteriorate because of increased ecosystem degradation.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

All proposed agricultural interventions are intended to increase productivity while ensuring that the carrying capacity of Puna ecosystems is not exceeded. The project will promote the use of best available scientific knowledge and modern technologies for water and soil conservation, exclusion zones and common resources, among others, to ensure resource sustainability in the long term.

The planning for this project will start from local social characteristics and cultural values that could have an influence on risks or opportunities related to the project. The project will also be based on lessons learned from previous experiences into planning and decision-making at macro scale. Project beneficiaries will be trained on community-based monitoring practices, and the results of these activities will be used as a basis for future projects. Best practices will be reinforced, integrating the best available science to determine risks in carrying capacity or intensity of agricultural activities.

### Selected Risk Factor 6: Difficulties and issues in accessing technical and financial support

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Low |

**Description**

Access to MEGADRI programs as well as technical and financial support from the Pura Facility will be on a voluntary basis. This may result in limited access for some project beneficiaries, which could have negative impacts on the project results.

Unlocking of public financing via MERESHE systems may require lengthy negotiations beyond the life of this project.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

MEGADRI has committed about 35 percent of its annual budget to technical assistance and communication obtained from a more streamlined approach for execution of MEGADRI’s funds will be operational. The project will provide support for project beneficiaries and MEGADRI officers to facilitate the communication materials for the application process. Complementarily, it will be ensured with

The Pure Facility will adopt the project’s Governance Structure as the Project Steering Committee and Project Management Committees will have an active role within the governance structure of the Facility. Participation of all relevant stakeholders (GOZ, Provinces, Districts, NGOs, CSOs, etc.) in the planning, implementation, monitoring, evaluation, and execution of activities to meet targets. Lessons learned from project execution will be integrated into the procedures of the corresponding GOZ/Provinces/Districts. The project will also support the establishment of a network of qualified CSOs and financed by the Pure Facility will be planned as to maximize concentration of positive impacts on ecosystem services, biodiversity conservation, and poverty reduction. For those MERSE where agreements between parties have not been reached, support will be provided for the development of a joint agreement. G. OCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

### G.1 ENVIRONMENTAL AND SOCIAL IMPACT ASSESSMENT AND ENVIRONMENTAL AND SOCIAL MANAGEMENT PLAN

285. The Environmental and Social Impact Assessment and Environmental (ESIA) of the project is provided in Annex 6a. The ESIA has been prepared based on the information available at the time of preparation, including the results of available literature concerning its ecosystems and social aspects. In parallel to the ESIA, stakeholder consultations have been carried out with the indigenous peoples and local communities (IPLCs) living within the project area as well as with the Indigenous Peoples and Local Communities Engagement Plan (IPLECP). The ESIA primarily relies on primary data obtained through stakeholder consultations and secondary information from available literature. It is important to note that the ESIA does not cover all aspects of the project due to the nature of the interventions these impacts will not be irreversible or complete in nature and will be easily reversible with appropriate mitigation measures.

286. The ESS primarily relies on primary data obtained through stakeholder consultations and secondary information from available literature concerning its ecosystems and social aspects. In parallel to the ESIA, stakeholder consultations have been carried out with the indigenous peoples and local communities (IPLCs) living within the project area as well as with the Indigenous Peoples and Local Communities Engagement Plan (IPLECP). The ESS document provides next to an extensive mapping of stakeholders a summary of three missions which were conducted in 2018. These missions aimed at understanding the context, the actors involved and their roles to better understand the potential risk from social and environmental point of view and the sensibility of the population towards the project.

287. In general, stakeholders interviewed during fieldwork approved the project and its main components. Stakeholders expressed their willingness to support the project in order to improve their livelihoods. In addition, producers expressed their concern about a collective approach versus individual support as most of the production systems are still based on individual plots. The project will be implemented through two components such as building of ochras and/or irrigation investments being considered. In particular, women are interested in the implementation of the project.

288. Furthermore, based on the analysis in general, the impacts of the project can be assessed as low to medium, in the following areas: (1) Environmental and Social Impact Assessment (ESIA) and Environmental and Climate Resilient Value Chain) and component 2 (regarding activity 1.1 establish the Pura Facility for the long-term storage of ochra). The ESIA has been prepared based on the information available at the time of preparation, which is a conflict driver; therefore and based on the results an ESIP and IPLECP has been designed for the management of the project. The ESIP and IPLECP are provided in Annex 6b. More details about these documents can be found in ESS Reference source not found, below, for ESS 7 Indigenous Peoples Table 9 details the respective components and activities. The ESIP and IPLECP will ensure that the project is implemented in compliance with the Environmental and Social Standards (ESS) and assessing the achievement of ESSM implementation through a Monitoring and Evaluation System (MES). The MES will be integrated into the Environmental, Social Safeguards and Management Monitoring System (ESS MS system). The ESS MS system encompasses specific procedures for the identification, assessment, mitigation, monitoring and reporting of environmental and social risks. It will also include a mechanism to ensure that the project is implemented in compliance with the ESSM, including executing, attesting, reviewing, and sustaining the Environmental and Social Requirements. Its development will take into account the recommendations provided by the ESIP and IPLECP.

289. In addition, ESSI requires the establishment of an external communication and grievance redress mechanism related to the project. The independent Redress Mechanism handles complaints related to environmental and social issues arising from the project implementation in accordance with its rules and procedures, including ESS standards. If a complaint is filed with the Independent Redress Mechanism, GIS as the Accompanying Information System will be used for the management of the information system. It will be finalized during the project inception phase and will be aligned with existing systems of Protraphone and MINDEP. The GIS will also be integrated into the ESS MS system.

# G.1.3 Indigenous Peoples and Local Communities Engagement Plan (ILCEP)

The project is targeting mainly the Southern High Andes of Peru (SHAIP Region), concentrating on four regions in Southern Peru: Cusco, Apurímac, Puno and Arequipa. The population of the local communities in this area is estimated at 105,724 people. Most of these people are Quechua speakers. Indigenous peoples consider themselves as part of the Quechua people. Most of the Quechua population lives organized politically within the Federation of Quechua Communities of Peru (Federación de Comunidades Quechua del Perú - FUCP). The Quechua people have a strong sense of identity, and their culture is based on their social, economic, and cultural features. Almost the entirety of the population in the project area belong to the Quechua ethnic group.

The Quechua people are mainly agriculturalists who live in small communities scattered throughout the Andes mountain range. They cultivate crops such as potatoes, corn, quinoa, beans, and other vegetables. Children attend primary school only on a minority continue and complete their secondary education instead of following to work with their families or to migrate to urban areas. The Quechua people are organized politically in several groups: the Federation of Quechua Communities of Peru (FUCP), the Association of Indigenous Organizations of Peru (AIP), and the National Council of Indigenous Peoples (CNPI). However, in the local terms of the Restored Forest project area, no one organization is recognized as representative.

## Culturally inappropriate access and rights to agricultural, biodiversity, and grassland

Inappropriate customary use of lands, forests, water bodies, and other natural resources can lead to conflicts between indigenous peoples and local communities. This project will address such issues by offering alternative access and livelihood options.
- Develop a strategy for addressing the needs of vulnerable groups in areas where poor and landless households are concentrated.
- Identify and support community-based approaches, and project personnel, promote, and Civil Society Organizations (CSOs) to ensure that indigenous peoples' rights are respected.

## Inappropriate customary use of lands

Respect Indigenous Peoples and Local Communities’ spiritually held sites and territories.
- Respect traditional land tenure systems and practices, and livelihoods.

### Possible complaints from Indigenous People about the project

Possible complaints may include: lack of consultation with indigenous peoples; insufficient information provided to them; and a lack of involvement in decision-making processes. These issues are particularly relevant when the poor and elderly are not being considered.
- Responsible for communicating with all stakeholders, including the Indigenous Peoples and Local Communities involved in the project area.

### G2. Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The project's Gender Analysis (GA; see Annex Bii) and Gender Action Plan (GAP; see Annex Biii) align with international commitments to gender equality and women’s empowerment, including the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action, the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW), national policies like the Green Climate Change Action Plan (GCAP), National Policy on Gender Equality. These policies recognize that gender equality is a fundamental human right and essential to sustainable development in political, cultural, climate, and economic rights.
- Recognizing that the impacts of global warming affect men and women differently, the project will address these issues by ensuring that all stakeholders are involved in decision-making; making lack of voice, vulnerability of their housing and household environment (e.g., health, food, gender based violence), and limited access to productive resources (land, water, seeds, tools, etc.) a priority.
- As mentioned in Section D.2.3, women in the SHAAP face more barriers than men that hinder the achievement of their goals for sustainable livelihoods and climate resilience. These include lack of access to land (use, control) and receive lower income. They also have limited access to microfinance, less opportunities and time for education, and are often excluded from decision-making processes.
- The project will address these gender gaps by ensuring that women’s voices are heard in all aspects of the project, including decision-making; making lack of voice, vulnerability of their housing and household environment (e.g., health, food, gender based violence), and limited gender-sensitive information (data, systematization of experiences, lessons learnt, behavioural changes).
- For this reason, the project will focus on women’s empowerment by addressing these barriers in terms of building social and technical capacities, as well as opportunities for their economic autonomy.
- Based on the identified gender gaps and recommendations, a Gender Action Plan (GAP) has been developed for the project. The GAP includes specific actions and targets that are integrated into the project's GA for more detailed information, please see Annex Biii.

# Overview of gender risks and actions integrated into the design

## Description of the Risk Factors

### Risk of not collecting disaggregated data on gender equality

- **Description:** Lack of disaggregated data to track female and male beneficiaries and ensure gender-sensitive project management.
- **Actions:**
  - Ensure collection, compilation, and quality checking of women's data in workshops offered by the project.
  - Ensure that all project staff are trained in gender analysis and communication tools to ensure that all project communication materials and guidelines include a gender equality and social inclusion (GESI) lens.

### Beneficiaries not using the programme services for fear of harassment

- **Description:** Fear of harassment or discrimination among women and girls.
- **Actions:**
  - Gender-based violence (GBV) and Sexual Exploitation Abuse and Harassment (SEAH) aspects will be integrated into all project activities.
  - Ensure that identified beneficiaries (local councils, communities, associations, cooperatives, etc.) are informed about the services provided by GIZ and their rights to access them.

### Designing GESI measures without considering women's needs, responsibilities, and decision-making power

- **Description:** Insufficient participation of women in the design process.
- **Actions:**
  - Ensure a gender-sensitive community planning process for investing in GESI measures and ensuring that women are involved as stakeholders.
  - Value Chain: Design and implement action research modules for ecosystem-based adaptation with a gender-sensitive approach.

### Ensuring women access to technical assistance including financial education, access to markets

- **Description:** Women's lack of access to technical assistance.
- **Actions:**
  - Systematisation of best practices of women's knowledge for replication. The project will promote the exchange and sharing of experiences among women in the field.
  - Dissemination of best practices of women's knowledge for replication. The project will promote the exchange and sharing of experiences among women in the field.

### Women not declaring ancestral knowledge

- **Description:** Gender criteria considered as part of the local initiative evaluation.
- **Actions:**
  - Ensure that all gender-related indicators are included in the M&E processes (indicators, governance platform, project development, monitoring, reporting and evaluation).
  - Gender criteria will be considered as part of the Local Initiative Evaluation.

### Planning processes do not include gender perspective

- **Description:** Lack of inclusion of women's perspectives in planning.
- **Actions:**
  - The training design will include a discussion identifying gender gaps and needs.
  - Provide training on gender equality and social inclusion (GESI) for planning processes.
  - Strengthening the disaggregation of the M&E system with gender GESI approach at MDG/GR.

### GIZ Head Office in Germany

- **Description:** GIZ head office in Germany (GIZ HQ) serves as the Accreditation Entity (AE), administering project funds and acting on behalf of the German Federal Ministry for Economic Cooperation and Development (BMZ). The AE is responsible for the Accreditation Master Agreement signed between GIZ and QF. The financial management of the project will be carried out by the QF.

Recipients have to report on the proper use of funds and deliver progress as a prerequisite for any further disbursement. Subrecipients are required to comply with the GIZ Financial Management Rules and German Accounting Standards.
  - Funds will be hosted by the public private Public Facility Manager and manage the different sub windows and complementary TA under components I and II. Under HQ supervision, Proterpase will ensure that all fiduciary standards of the GIZ are met.

- **Description:** The project will host the public private Public Facility Manager and manage the different sub windows and complementary TA under component 1 and 2. Under HQ supervision, Proterpase will ensure that all fiduciary standards of the GIZ are met.

## G.3 Procurement Guidelines

### 300. In the case of procurement by GIZ,

GIZ will follow its own Procurement Guidelines. GIZ is required to comply with the applicable national law and regulations for public procurement in accordance with the German Regulation on the Award of Public Contracts (VgVG) and, if applicable, the Contracting Rules for the Award of Public Contracts (VgVZ).

### 301. When awarding contracts for supplies and services including consultancy services to be financed in full or in part by public funds, the Executing Entities should follow the GIZ Procurement Guidelines.

When awarding contracts for supplies and services including consultancy services to be financed in full or in part by public funds, the Executing Entities should follow the GIZ Procurement Guidelines. When awarding a project with public funds the Executing Entities should take reasonable account of economic efficiency as well as ecological sustainability.

### 302. The Procurement Guidelines shall not contradict the applicable national procurement law and/or regulations for public procurement in accordance with the German Regulation on the Award of Public Contracts (VgVG) and, if applicable, the Contracting Rules for the Award of Public Contracts (VgVZ). When awarding a project with public funds the Executing Entities country are to be observed; the procurement procedures mentioned in the Guidelines are obligatory provisions. The Executing Entities should ensure that the procurement activities carried out in accordance with the GIZ Procurement Guidelines comply with national law and regulations.

### 303. GIZ, in its function as AE will have monitoring and oversight responsibility on the procurement activities carried out by the Executing Entities. The Executing Entities are required to provide all necessary information to ensure compliance with GIZ procurement guidelines which are contractually agreed and reviewed externally via annual audit.

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP226

## Proposal name: Resilient Puno: Ecosystem based Adaptation for sustainable High Andean communities and ecosystems in Peru

Accredited entity: Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ)

Country: Peru

Project/programme size: Medium

### I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board with the following remarks:

#### Strengths
- The project has a positive impact and sustainable development potential thanks to its combination of ecosystem-based adaptation interventions along key agriculture and animal husbandry value chains vital to livelihoods in vulnerable communities in the Peruvian Southern High Andes.
- The project features a strong model to address the geographical, financial, knowledge and governance barriers that affect climate-affected value chains.

#### Points of caution
- The Puno-Facility will provide grants and reimbursable grants for investments with the objective of increasing resilience. However, there is no comprehensive justification on the need for grants (especially for information entities with no marketability) or the rationale behind providing grants (for entities with established capacities and mechanisms to capture and crowdfund private finance).
- The project captures and crowdfunds out of private investment in Puno and reflows to support additional investments.

2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions listed in the term sheet and addendum XVII, titled “List of proposed conditions and recommendations” (PropCo).

### II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment


---
**Page 128**
---

# Project background

## 2.1 Project background

- Climate change and the unsustainable management of productive practices are degrading the puna ecosystems (peatlands, wetlands and grasslands) and their services they provide (e.g., water regulation of water; provision of food, fodder and fiber; nutrient and carbon regulation) in the Southern High Andes of Peru (SHAIP). The Andean Indigenous Peoples and local communities have a long history of managing these ecosystems sustainably by their capacity to adapt to environmental changes. Even though this traditional knowledge has been a key asset for the resilience of these communities, it is currently under threat due to climate change, increasing severity and unpredictability of extreme events, as well as the unpredictability of hydro cycles, quick snowmelt and droughts.

- In this context, the project aims to enhance ownership and resources of Andean IPCFs to build their resilience to climate change by defining, implementing and monitoring their ecosystem services management plans (ESMPs) in order to maintain or improve these ecosystems and ensure the continuous provision of their services such as water supply that will be captured during the dry season.

- Overall, the project is expected to promote the sustainable management of 23,914 ha of puna ecosystems, directly benefiting 60,715 people and indirectly another 2,013,856 people. The mitigation objective is to reduce GHG emissions by 1,000 tCO2e/year through the implementation of ESMPs.

- The accredited entity (AE) is the Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ), with the Ministry of Agricultural Development and Irrigation of Peru (MINAGRI) as the implementing partner. The project will be implemented in the Provinces of Montaña (Puno) and GIZ Peru will act as an executing entity (EE) receiving GCF funds, while MINAGRI will act as a co-executing entity (CEE). The Andean Sustainable Management Alliance (ASMAANT) will act as EEIs that will contribute with financing to achieve the project's objective.

- The project is submitted as environmental and social safeguards (ESS) category B.

- It was developed with support from the Project Preparation Facility.


## 2.2 Component-by-component analysis

### Overall, the project will support promoting a paradigm shift in the management of puna ecosystems and productive practices to increase the climate resilience of some of the most vulnerable communities in Peru. The project will also contribute to the development of policies that depend on these ecosystems for their livelihoods.

- To achieve this, the project consists of three components (1) through which it will support the scaling up Esh measures aligned and leveraged; and (3) motivated territorial governance for the integration of ESMPs in local planning processes.

#### Component 1: Puna ecosystems are respected, conserved and better managed through the implementation of Esh measures complemented by climate-resilient value chains (CRVCs) (total cost USD 20.5 million).

- By implementing investments on the ground (through component 1), the aims are (1) to maintain or improve the provision of puna ecosystem services for the climate resilience of Andean IPCFs and their communities; (2) to promote the conservation of these ecosystems that are dependent on and which impact those ecosystems. This component will provide financial and technical support through a call for a proposal mechanism for Esh and CRVC implementation on the ground.

- Component 1 is structured in two outputs:


---
**Page 129**
---

## Output 1.1 Investments for EBA measures and CRVCs are implemented at the local landscape level.

### Output 1.2: The use of Eba knowledge is recovered and disseminated, and local monitoring committees and observation systems are implemented.
- Component 3: Enhancing participation in climate change adaptation and resilience building activities in the Puna ecosystem (total cost: EUR 7,192,452; GCF cost: EUR 6,084,234; co-financed: EUR 1,118,218)

### Component 2. Comprises three complementary actions focused on the establishment and strengthening of complementary financial mechanisms.
- **Output 2.1**: Financial mechanisms for the implementation of Eba measures and improvement of climate-resilient livelihoods in the Puna ecosystem are established:
    - Activity 2.1.1: Strengthening the Puna Facility for long-term financing of Eba measures and CRVCs
    - Activity 2.1.2: Strengthen capacities to develop and implement innovative mechanisms (e.g., microfinance, insurance)
    - Activity 2.1.3: Support the greening of microcredits to promote Eba and CRVCs

### Component 3: EBA and climate resilience are mainstreamed into multilevel landscape governance mechanisms (total cost: EUR 7,192,452; GCF cost: EUR 6,084,234; co-financed: EUR 1,118,218)
- Through component 3, the project will promote integrated gender-sensitive landscape planning, governance platforms and policy improvement and coordination, fostering dialogue among stakeholders at all levels (local communities, indigenous peoples, local and national governments, rural communities, producer organizations, watershed committees, and multilevel extension services).

### Component 1: Comprises three of the following outputs:
- **Output 3.1**: Multilevel landscape governance is improved through strengthening of national capacity, regulatory frameworks and monitoring and evaluation systems.
    - Project (total cost: EUR 8,192,452; GCF cost: EUR 6,084,234)

### This cost will be met by the following grants and disbursement of the project activities, in compliance with the contractual obligations that are included or referred to in the legal agreements concluded with each for the implementation of the project. The total grant is less than 5 per cent of the gross grant of requested funding from GCF and is compliant with the GCF policy fees.

### III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: Medium to high

- The proposal aims to increase the resilience to climate change in six cities including major cities such as Cusco, Arequipa, Huancayo, and Lima.
- This will lead to significant improvements in ecosystem services, most notably water quality and availability, generating benefits for 2.075 million people living in the upper and lower parts of watersheds, protecting against increased risks of floods and droughts as a result of climate change.

#### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: Medium to high

- The project is expected to have 60,725 direct beneficiaries, particularly among the Quechua-speaking Indigenous Peoples living in the highly vulnerable Southern High Andes region of Peru.
- The project will support the GCF investment (financial and technical support) (1) through the conservation of key value chains such as cocoa, quinoa, and coca, and (2) by implementation of EDA mechanisms based on best practices and ancestral knowledge on an estimated 2.915 ha of puna ecosystems that are highly valuable to local communities, enhancing the provision of ecosystem services.

#### 3.2 Paradigm shift Scale: Medium to high

- The project presents a strong theory of change regarding the three key Puna ecosystems and the communities that depend on them.
- The theory of change focuses on transforming agricultural and animal husbandry value chains by addressing barriers related to access to credit, markets, and technology.

#### 3.2 Paradigm shift Scale: Medium to high

- The project will expand EDA approaches, so far piloted but not demonstrated at scale, by establishing financial mechanisms to channel public finance via the Puna Facility with funding windows for both public and private finance.
- This will enable the scaling up of ecosystem services mechanisms.
- Further, the project will give the capacity to community-level institutions to develop and market their own products and services, including the development of financial products for EDA approaches. Parallel development of both public and private finance sources is critical in this stage and scaling up the project's results.

#### 3.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

- The project features a strong theory of change and investment based on capturing ancestral knowledge, community monitoring, and learning lessons to identify and promote best practices.
- Community-level institutions will be trained on sustainable management of natural resources and rural extension services, will be trained-on sustainable management and traditional practices and tasked with knowledge dissemination to final beneficiaries.


---
**Page 131**
---

3.4 Needs of the recipient

Scale: High

Despite Peru’s status as a middle-income country, the project targets predominantly Indigenous Peoples’ communities in isolated areas of the Southern High Andes (69 per cent of beneficiaries are indigenous). These communities face significant challenges related to climate change, particularly droughts and the availability of natural resources in fragile high-altitude ecosystems. The project is expected to contribute to poverty reduction through increased income generation for over 42,000 beneficiaries.

As mentioned as part of the discussion on paradigm shift, the proposal takes on a comprehensive approach to tackling these needs, strengthening value chains, connectedness, governance and management systems, and ultimately the Puna ecosystems which form the basis of the livelihoods in the region.

3.5 Country ownership

Scale: High

The programme is part of Peru’s country programme and is well-aligned with both the Peruvian nationally determined contributions on agriculture and water, national adaptation plan, as well as sectoral policies on agriculture and green financing. The programme was developed through a participatory process involving all relevant stakeholders in Peru, with a full range of stakeholders at national, regional and local levels.

The project will be implemented by GCF’s implementing partner, Proyecto Verde, which is an RE to GCF and has ample experience in the operation of similar projects combining funding facilities and the provision of technical assistance, with community-based action in the areas of forestry and agriculture. The project will also benefit from the presence of a local non-profit organization (as charge of protected areas), SMM (non-profit focused on the conservation of the values of mountain ecosystems) which has strong experience in this area.

3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

Scale: Medium


---
**Page 132**
---

## The funding proposal package presents a well-developed economic and financial analysis with clear definition of scope and well-informed assumptions. It covers the economic and financial performance of the Puna Facility, including repayable, non-repayable and Agreements for Technical Assistance (ATA). The project is considered financially viable as 90 per cent of the funding is to be used for technical assistance and capacity-building, and therefore is not taken into account in the economic and financial analysis.

### The discount rate is set at a maximum level of 12.5 per cent based on World Bank's report (2012) on Peru’s climate investment. The results show that the net present value of the project capital is negative, which means that the project does not generate sufficient cash flow over reasonable 17-year time horizon, confirming that the proposed project is economically viable. However, if the discount rate is increased, for example to 8 per cent, which is used in other public sector projects in Peru, the net present value of the project capital becomes negative and the internal rate of return is reduced significantly to EUR 0.8 million.

### The financial analysis demonstrates the process of conversionality as demonstrated in the financial analysis. The AIE has conducted a scenario analysis testing financial results with different financial instruments (loan). The financial model shows mixed results: it is still financially viable if the project is financed by a loan, but the internal rate of return and estimated internal rate of return is above the weighted average cost of capital. The AE complements the results with a range of scenarios that show that even in case of a 10 per cent discount rate, loans would not make the project viable as cash shortfall is quite significant during the project implementation period. However, if the project is financed by grants or other instruments for the project period, Grants would be the most appropriate instrument.

### In terms of the financial sustainability beyond the project period, the key to the sustainability of the project lies in the ability to attract and mobilize financing resources from the private sector, which is the purpose of subactivity 2.1.1. To demonstrate such feasibility, the AIE was requested to conduct a scenario analysis testing financial results with different discount rates and estimated profits to be achieved by the end of the project, so that the private sector can see the potential profitability of the project. The scenario analysis shows that the Puna Facility value chain under a business-as-usual scenario and climate-resilient (with-the-project) scenarios where all climate-resilient scenarios show revenue growth and net cash flows.

### The financial model estimates the internal rate of return as 12 per cent, which is not sustainable for the project. The financial model estimates repayment rate as 50 per cent, assuming that different local interest rates are applied to each loan. At the end of the project implementation period and at project completion, the revolving funds may flow back into any of the windows under the Puna Facility, leaving uncertainty on how much funding would be reinvested, and hence sustainability is not guaranteed.

### The co-financing ratio for the project is 1:2.5, with a maximum level of co-financing of EUR 632/direct beneficiary and USD 290/non-beneficiary. Cost per direct beneficiary is at the higher range of adaptation portfolio in the Latin America and Caribbean region, as the average cost per direct beneficiary for projects in this region is EUR 488. The co-financing ratio is 1:6/2, with the sources entirely from the public sector.

### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### Environmental and social safeguards

##### Project background
The project aims to align public and private investments and increase access to State programmes with a view to improving the climate resilience of high


---
**Page 133**
---

Andean ecosystems and community livelihoods. Activities are intended to reduce the degree of losses from climate impacts, improve communities' relationship with ecosystems, increase their participation in the value chain and product transformation, improve their associative processes, and promote gender equality.

The project will finance the implementation of EIA measures and build CIVCs to restore and conserve the pinea ecosystems. The project will also improve structural conditions that will make more sustainable and climate-smart ways of living possible. The project will contribute to more sustainable and climate-smart ways of living, offering alternatives to reduce the unsustainable practices that are currently being used in the Andean region.

The project is expected to have positive environmental (EAS) impacts by enhancing the environmental and social (EAS) or benefits are the maintenance of ecosystem services in the project area. The project will also contribute to gender equality through the implementation, mainstreaming gender-responsive approaches in EIA and CIVC measures.


### Environmental and social risk category

The AE assigned the project as moderate EAS risks. The project is expected to have no significant adverse environmental or social impacts. The Secretariat confirms the proposed E&S risk category, considering that the project will largely have positive E&S impacts. However, there are some potential adverse E&S impacts which may be site-specific and/or temporary in nature (e.g., loss of biodiversity). The Secretariat confirms that the assigned risk category is within the accreditation level of the AE.


### Safeguards instrument

The AIE provided an environmental and social impact assessment (ESIA) for the project. The ESIA was prepared by the project proponent, following the EIA guideline proposal as included in the safeguarding document. The document provides an overview of the national context of the Andean region and describes the potential impacts that may arise from this project activities and a range of EIA measures and CIVC interventions targeted by the project.

As the project will mobilize public and private financing through the establishment of the Puna Facility, the ESIA will be used as the basis for the preparation of the EIA and ESMPs until they are developed during the project's implementation.


### Compliance with ESS standards

The following paragraphs provide a brief description of the programme’s compliance with ESS standards.

ESS 1: Assessment and management of environmental and social risks and impacts. The project proponent has prepared an ESIA for the project, which includes a SEAP targeted by the project and identified the potential E&S risks and impacts against the proposed EIA measures and CIVC interventions. The ESIA also provides information on how these risks will be managed in the project's ESPM. Most of the risks and impacts identified by the AE are of moderate scale and can be readily managed and mitigated through the appropriate procedures and plans as described in the SEAP. However, some E&S risks and impacts may arise from the interaction with existing or project-induced conflict developing from the participatory process for engaging beneficiaries and stakeholders in the project activities. The project proponent has identified CIVCs prioritized for future local initiatives. Identified conflict risks are related to the exclusion of disadvantaged groups and gender inequality. These risks will be managed through specific procedures to be followed by activities supported under the Puna Facility. The facility-specific EIA due diligence process will also be used as a basis for assessing the potential E&S impacts on project grants, use of limited resources (including for downstream water users) and ownership and land tenure issues.

ESS 2: Gender equality and culturally sensitive engagement and communication strategy to ensure that the potential beneficiaries are properly identified. In the context of the Puna Facility, where financed projects will be implemented in a participatory manner with local communities, the potential E&S risks and impacts of such financing cannot be assessed in detail in advance. The ESIA provides an overview of the gender equality and culturally sensitive engagement procedures to be followed by activities supported under the Puna Facility. The facility-specific EIA due diligence process will also be used as a basis for assessing the potential E&S impacts on project grants, use of limited resources (including for downstream water users) and ownership and land tenure issues.


---
**Page 134**

### ES 2: Labour and working conditions

The EIA identifies that the project may involve people exposed to risks of accident or contagion during the construction and land work of some EbA measures and CRVC interventions, where extensive manual labour may be involved.

As a large number of local communities will be directly affected by the project activities, potential occupational and safety risks are likely to be low and limited to the phase of local initiative implementation. The AE will require site-specific labour management procedures, including training on safe work practices, as well as ensuring that all workers have access to the provision of protective equipment when necessary, following the code of ethics and implementing guidelines.

### ES 3: Resource efficiency and pollution control

The EIA identifies that the project may involve low risks related to greenhouse gas emissions if the proposed CRVC interventions involve the planting of trees in areas where there is no existing vegetation or where it has been cleared abroad. The AE also identifies a low risk of pollution concerning the mismanagement of water resources, which could lead to contamination of surface and groundwater.

The AE will mitigate such risks by following the eligibility criteria to select proper EbA and CRVC interventions that are in line with the project’s objectives and the EIA findings on due diligence, and providing technical assistance at the local landscape level on managing the project’s environmental impacts.

### ES 4: Community health, safety, and security

The AE identifies that the project may expose local communities to low risks and impacts on their health and safety, including the exposure of children and women to increased risk from traffic accidents. The EIA also notes the potential conflicts over ownership and inappropriate selection or implementation of local interventions, which could lead to social conflict.

The AE will mitigate such risks by ensuring that the project is implemented in a manner that allows for the development of community-based management plans and local initiatives to be developed and implemented to mitigate such risks. Since the project will be locally implemented at the community level and by the community members, with support from local rural communities and other stakeholders, it is expected that these risks can be mitigated through activities.

### ES 5: Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement

The project does not include any activities that will require land acquisition through expropriation or other compulsory procedures. The EIA monitors and CRVC activities that will be funded by the project will be designed to ensure that the benefits of such activities are distributed equitably among those involved, including the local communities.

### ES 6: Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of natural resources

The AE has identified low risks and impacts on biodiversity as the project aims to improve access roads in areas where there is no existing vegetation or where it has been cleared abroad. Improved road access may facilitate greater access for the public and thus accelerate the degradation of ecosystems, which could lead to a loss of biodiversity.

However, the AE notes that this assessment is based on assumptions about the situation; there could be overestimating due to an incorrect choice on the CRVC component: agroforestry and reforestation may attract new pests and diseases into

Implementation Unit: Proponent will, in its capacity of one of the project’s EUs, be responsible for the management of the Puna Facility under the supervision of the AE.
