### Stakeholder engagement plan

The Proponent has developed a stakeholder engagement plan with the proposal, which summarizes the consultations conducted during the inception phase. The plan identifies key stakeholders at different levels and their respective roles and interests in the project, including the relevant ministries, local government agencies, non-government institutions, civil society institutions, representative organizations of indigenous peoples, representatives of rural communities, trade unions, professional organizations, and women’s organizations. The stakeholder engagement plan outlines how the Proponent will engage with these stakeholders throughout the proposal phase and the EUs to ensure that stakeholders are continuously engaged throughout the project life cycle, including through regular meetings, workshops, and other forms of communication such as community monitoring and the scaling up of best practices, including ancestral knowledge and practices. During the implementation phase, in addition to the engagement plan for project implementation, the AE should disclose the ESS documentation of all Category B subjects/risks funded by the Puna Facility in both English and the local language (if not English).

### Grievance redress mechanism

The Proponent has developed a grievance redress mechanism (GRM) during the inception phase that will allow any individual to register a complaint and will be harmonized with the GCF’s GRM at the national level, subnational level, and project implementation level. The project will also establish a dedicated SEAR-SEANP related GRM system to align with the requirements of the GCF Environmental and Social Policy, including to ensure accountability to the grievance redress mechanism established by the GCF Board. The Proponent has committed to follow up during implementation to ensure the effectiveness of the GRM system to be applied by the project and its stakeholders.

## Gender policy

The AE has provided a gender assessment and Gender Action Plan therefore complies with the requirements of the GCF Gender Policy.

### Gender assessment conducted through desk review available data and fieldwork in which consultations were conducted with officials within project locations, while also engaging with women’s organizations and other civil society groups.

The gender analysis has identified key factors that are hindering women's progress and resilience in the face of climate change. Although there is a formal institutional framework for gender equality in the Puna Facility, there is a lack of implementation at all levels. This is characterized by a high retention of human capital and a type of management that usually lacks recognition of women’s contributions to household income generation.

The project has identified several barriers faced by women in the context of climate change. The MINAM and SEANP have shown a sustained interest in incorporating gender approaches in their activities, including the development of a Gender Action Plan. Women are responsible for collecting water for cooking and cleaning, using the land for forest grazing and fetching firewood, which is often done on foot or by bicycle several kilometers from home on a daily basis and as a result are also among the first to feel the adverse effects of climate change.

Rural women also face several barriers such as lower levels of education, inability to speak a language other than Quechua, lack of access to credit, limited access to land for domestic and productive work. Despite women’s important roles in small-scale agriculture and water harvesting, they are often excluded from decision-making processes due to significant barriers, limit their availability, access to resources and opportunities for education, training,


---
**Page 137**
---

paid employment and participation in decision-making spaces. These translate into under-representation in community organizations, in water user’s organisations, and in decision-making spaces. Women also have less access to and control of natural resources and decisions about land use.

The gender action plan provides actions that address the gender gaps identified through the gender assessment. Actions have corresponding targets, indicators, timeline, budget and the respective responsible parties. The gender action plan is based on the theory of change and the three major components of the project: implementing investments on the ground (component 1), strengthening the institutional framework for landscape change to deliver financing for IFLC-led initial initiatives oriented to increase climate resilience (component 2), and promote integrated gender-responsive landscape planning, governance platforms and participatory processes (component 3). The theory of change is based on a set of assumptions that are supported by evidence from the baseline and CRV measures. Specific activities include training water utility officers of the Mechanisms of Resource Efficiency in Ecosystem Services (MERES) project on gender equality and women’s rights, aspects into the MERES processes (agreements, governance platforms, project development, monitoring and evaluation). The gender action plan also includes a specific budget line to finance for women and other traditionally marginalized groups (with a 50 per cent target going to women, women-headed households, women-led enterprises and women-only initiatives) in the context of Eba and CRVCs. Strengthen gender mainstreaming through the planning processes and training of public officials with an institutionalized training programme led by the Peruvian Ministry of Environment. The gender action plan also includes a commitment for women in decision-making planning processes through training and requirements for women’s representation on project steering committees, technical working groups and other decision-making bodies. Training and communication will be designed to be accessible for women and address practical barriers that may prevent their participation. Specific activities under this component include financial education, access to markets, implementation of local initiatives and the collection and systematic documentation of practices for women’s engagement and women-led IFLC initiatives for sharing and replication.

Given the content and scope of documents submitted, the following recommendations are made:

The AE to include baseline data collection activity related to the number of women producers and for women-led initiatives at the inception phase. Such information on intent needs to be collected in order to assess the gender impact of the project’s activities and responsibilities. Changes to the Gender Action Plan as a result of this action should be communicated to all parties.

The Secretariat notes that each IFLC will voluntarily apply to the Puna Facility with tailored technical support to refine their original proposals. Addressing the challenges for women, women-led initiatives and other traditionally marginalized groups requires due attention and strategies clearly outlined to ensure sufficient applications will be received from women (including women’s organizations) and that they are able to demonstrate their interest and ability to engage (persuasive society or the enabling environment within the home) in such voluntary action.).

The indicators for pre- and post-surveys will be given to recipients to measure increased capacity. There is a need to include indicators to measure this aspect.

## Project viability and concessionality

The AE carried out the economic and financial analysis with the EUR 182 million GCF grant portion to fund the establishment and operation of the PacifiC Fund. The analysis shows that the project is viable, as it generates a positive net present value (NPV) and internal rate of return (IRR). Without the GCF grant, the analysis results in an internal rate of return (IRR) of 68 per cent to 72 per cent per year for the loan option. However, due to the high BRRs, the AE argues that the loan option is not feasible, and grant is still required due to (1) larger NPV and (2) lower risk profile. The project also generates a positive net present value (NPV) of 30 per cent for the equity option. The IRR from GCF gives the credit risk of the target beneficiaries. The economic internal rate of return was positive with the sensitive analysis considering changes in shallow carbon price, groundwater and electricity prices.

### GCF portfolio concentration risk (low)

In the case of approval, the impact of this proposal on the GCF portfolio concentration in terms of results area and single proposal is not material.

## Compliance risk (medium/low)

This project is largely focused on addressing the resilience and sustainability of communities and ecosystems. The AE considers that there are no significant risks to local communities and institutions. Potential risks of money laundering or counterfeiting the financing of terrorism do not seem unusually great or significant. As an AG, GIZ has considerable experience in managing and supervising projects with a high risk profile and is well equipped for identifying and managing financial risks and compliance issues. Nevertheless, as capabilities of implementing partners and their local counterparts are limited, there may be some risks that they will not fully implement their internal controls and policies to address any risks and vulnerabilities as they may arise.

Accordingly, the AE considers that based on its experience and policies of GIZ, the compliance risk here is rated as medium.

# 4.4 Fiduciary

76. GIZ serves a dual role, acting as both the AE and one of the EEs through its management structure in Peru. In its capacity as the AE, GIZ oversees the project in accordance with the accredited fiduciary framework. The Secretariat members GIZ holds on behalf of the Secretariat, providing guidance to EEs (Professionals and IDM).

77. GIZ, as an EE, leads the technical assistance efforts at national and subnational levels, ensuring implementation, compliance with safeguards, and mainstreaming climate resilience and gender aspects.

78. In order to implement the project, GIZ will establish legal arrangements with MIDAGRI, SERAMIN, and other relevant actors in Peru.

79. Profosuape, an EE, manages the Pusa Facility with a budget of USD 19,175,000, implementing EBA measures and CRVs. MIDAGRI will act as an EE by contributing and scaling up its experience gained from the project to other regions in Peru.

80. SERAMIN will be responsible for the financial management of the project, ensuring the sustainability and scalability of the local initiatives supported by the project. Lastly, MIDAGRI and SERAMIN will work with their own funds, coordinating activities and contributing to the project's objectives.

81. Funds from GCF will be transferred directly to GIZ as the AE, who will then transfer funds to Professionals and IDM according to their respective budgets.

82. The financial management of the project will follow GIZ internal rules and regulations. GIZ will not open a specific bank account for the GCF project but will ensure that all funds provided by GCF are used exclusively for the project's objectives, with GIZ setting up separate cost units exclusively for the funds disbursed by GCF for each funded activity (ledger accounts).

83. As a general principle, GIZ disburses funds to recipients in accordance with the progress of the project. EEs receiving GCF proceeds from GIZ (Professionals and IDM) must report on the project's progress according to their respective budgets.

84. Independent external auditors will perform annual financial audits of the project in line with international auditing standards.

### Legal assessment

The AMA was signed with the AG on 15 November 2017 and became effective on 18 January 2018. The first amendment and restatement agreement amending and restating the AMA was signed on 3 December 2021 and became effective on 18 January 2024.

The AG has re-accredited the Board pursuant to decision R37/18. The first amendment and restatement agreement amending and restating the AMA was signed on 3 December 2021 and became effective on 18 January 2024.

The proposed project will be implemented in the Republic of Peru. The Secretariat provided a draft agreement on privileges and immunities, which means that, amongst other things, GCF has the right to sue or be sued by any person or entity in any court or tribunal to which it needs to be further assessed. The GCF Secretariat provided a draft agreement on privileges and immunities and a background note to the Government of the Republic of Peru on 9 September 2015.

The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism and Independent Integrity Unit have both expressed their willingness to provide support in the form of advice, assistance or investigations, as appropriate, in countries where GCF is not provided with relevant privileges and immunities. The Secretariat will only be able to make payments on behalf of GCF are made only after GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expropriation in the country, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.

### List of proposed conditions (including legal)

To address the matters raised in this section, it is recommended that any approval by the Board be made subject to the following conditions:

1. Submission of a completed due diligence form and substance satisfactory to the Secretariat within 180 days of the Date of Board approval; and
2. Completion of the legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the Secretariat.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP226

## Proposal name:
Resilient Puna: Ecosystem based Adaptation for sustainable High Andean communities and ecosystems in Peru

## Accredited entity:
Deutsche Gesellschaft für internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ)

## Country:
Peru

## Project/programme size:
Medium

### I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

1. This funding proposal for a medium-sized adaptation project in environmental and social safeguards category B is submitted by the Government of Peru through the Deutsche Gesellschaft für internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ).

2. The five executing entities (EE) are GIZ, the Ministry of Agricultural Development and Irrigation (MINAGRI), the National Service of Natural Areas Protected by the State (SERANP), the Peruvian Association for the Conservation of Nature (APN), and the Andean Community Institute Andrés de Montejo (IAM). MINAGRI and SERANP are public entities, Professional Corporation UTM is a profit-making entity with public and social interest, and IAM is a non-governmental organization.

3. The total cost of the funding proposal is US$ 78.12 million, of which EUR 69.79 million in GIZ funds and EUR 8.33 million in co-funding from German Federal Ministry for Economic Cooperation and Development and from the Government of Peru through MINAGRI and SERANP.

4. The aim of the Resilient Puna project is to increase the resilience of communities, ecosystems and livelihoods in the southern High Andes, which are negatively affected by climate change. Climate change impacts include increased temperatures, reduced precipitation, complemented by climate-resilient value chains, the establishment of public and private financing mechanisms for scaling up ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA) measures, and governance instruments. The project supports Quechua-speaking and other farming communities to revive mountain ecosystems and livelihoods in the face of climate change, while simultaneously maintaining crucial hydrological services for downstream water users and important stocks for global carbon regulation.

5. The project is structured into three main components, with the outcomes as shown in the table below:

| Component | Outcome | GIZ Funding (EUR) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1. Restoring ecosystems and communities | Puna ecosystems are restored, conserved and better managed through the implementation of ecosystem-based adaptation measures complemented by climate-resilient value chains | EUR 23.42 million |
| 2. Public and private investments for scaling up EbA measures | Public and private financing for EbA measures developed in a participatory manner and being actively used by vulnerable communities | EUR 6.05 million |

<sup>1</sup> Funding proposal package for "Resilient Puna: Ecosystem based Adaptation for sustainable High Andean communities and ecosystems in Peru", received from GIZ on 15 December 2023.

3. Multilevel territorial governance is strengthened through the incorporation of EbA measures

EbA and climate resilience are mainstreamed into multilevel landscape management instruments

EUR 3.93 million


6. The project includes the establishment and operation of the Puna Facility, implemented by ProFonseca, to deliver financing – through a call for proposals for local initiatives, led by indigenous communities, cooperatives, or other relevant stakeholders in the context of this project. The Puna Facility will fund EbA measures and climate resilient value chains, and will have three sub-windows: (i) Agroecology Matching Sub-Wind: for agroecological farming systems (local communities, producers' associations and cooperatives in the process of formalization), applying a participatory approach to the design and implementation of projects; (ii) Grant Sub-Wind: for micro and small enterprises, community enterprises and established cooperatives; and (iii) Agroecology Matching Sub-Wind: for formalized producer organizations with clear business models and clear target markets that are eligible for the MILAGRO support programme Agrisudá*.

### Impact potential

#### Scale: High

a. The project targets three adaptation results areas, enhancing the resilience of (i) most vulnerable people and communities; (ii) health and well-being, and food and water security; and (iii) ecosystems and ecosystem services.

9. Climate change mitigation through avoided emissions of greenhouse gases (GHGs) as a result of project interventions, estimated at 407,657 tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (tCO2 eq) over 15 years, is considered as a co-benefit of the project, and is discussed under section 1.3 below.

10. The funding proposal outlines the major climate change-related hazards characterizing the southern High Andes of Peru. Increased temperatures have contributed to glacier melting, which changes water availability in the region. Reduced snow cover has also reduced water availability in an area where 80 per cent of the population is heavily reliant on rain-fed agriculture. Other climate change-related hazards include increased frequency and intensity of events that are degrading the Puna ecosystems (peatlands, wetlands or bogs, and grasslands) and the services they provide such as regulation of water; provision of fodder, food and fibre; nutrient and carbon regulation.

11. The project will work in 19 districts in the regions of Puno, Cusco, Arequipa, Apurímac and Lima (see Figure 2). These are areas where communities live at altitudes above 3,500 m a.s.l., or are in a zone above 2,800 m, in vulnerable communities practices that are not sustainable. The project will also address the impacts of climate change and other climate hazards, including degraded lands and areas that have suffered melting of glaciers. Of the 91 districts, 36 will be eligible for the Puna Facility (covering 4,114,475 ha) and capacity building will be provided to 20 districts in the Apurímac region.

12. Four natural protected areas managed by SERENAP fall inside ecological districts: Noraycocha National Sanctuary, Chacraraju National Park, Salkantay National Sanctuary, and Salinas y Aguada Blanca National Reserve in the Arequipa region; and Ampay National Sanctuary in the Cusco region.

13. The project aims to directly benefit 60,715 people (of whom 30,888 will be female). This number is made up of:

- Individuals who implement local initiatives financed and supported by the Puna Facility, multiplied by the average number of members of the groups/legally entities supported and the average number of members per household in the region, representing 31,750 people.
- Individuals who will be supported by MGGF co-finance programmes, multiplied by the average number of members per household in the region, representing 26,315 people.
- Individuals who will receive technical assistance from the GCF-funded intervention (e.g. training and capacity building for local authorities and communities to prepare participatory intervention plans), representing 1,740 people; and
- Public officials who will receive capacity-building for adapting regulations and norms, representing 625 people.

13. The project will also indirectly benefit 2,811,056 people (of whom 1,095,928 are female) who will benefit from water generated by the improvement of ecosystems that contribute to water regulation in the Puna region and downstream areas, including residents of major downstream cities such as Cusco, Arequipa, Abancay and Calca.

14. Site-level activities will engage local farming communities, including indigenous Quechua farmers, smallholder farmers, pastoralists, agroforestry producers, micro, small and medium-sized enterprises, and local government. Adaptation benefits will accrue to vulnerable groups such as women, youth, the elderly, people with disabilities, and value chain interventions in component 1 of the project, to be sustainably financed and embedded in governance regimes through links residents in components 2 and 3 respectively.


---
**Page 145**

ground interventions will take place across 23,014 ha Puna ecosystems, contributing to the results targeted in the updated Strategic Plan for the GCF 2024-2027.

### Ecosystem-based adaptation

The funding proposal highlights seven key EbA measures and shows how each reduces vulnerability to specific climate hazards. The measures are co-designed with local communities and include: (1) restoration of family gachos (natural reserves), agroforestry, forest restoration with native species, restoration of andalusian oaks, reforestation with native species, and the establishment of agroforestry systems; contour farming, conservation agriculture and sustainable grassland management. Enhancing the study area’s resilience to climate change is a key objective of this project. The feasibility study annexed to the funding proposal outlines restoration techniques, costs and goals in great detail. A manual for project implementers and producers was developed and will be finalized by mid-2019. This manual will be translated into Spanish and Quechua.

### Climate-resilient value chains

Support to climate-resilient value chains includes a comprehensive approach to enhance the productivity and climate resilience of production systems through the integration of agroforestry, intercropping, crop diversification and soil addition. Target value chains are alpaca and vicuña fibres, potato and Andean grains, with complementary chains including traditional Andean crops’ breeding of small animals (guinea pigs), companion plants and medicinal plants. The Puna Facility is a key component provided to access and implement Puna Facility funded local initiatives (both EbA and climate-resilient value chain projects). It provides technical assistance for project implementation; facilitating access to MIDMIGRI technical assistance and the promotion of market access for new or expanded products.

17. The IFAP examines the potential impact of the project as high.

### 1.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: Medium to high

The proposed project is designed to bring about a paradigm shift away from the current situation of continuing degradation of Puna ecosystems of the southern High Andes region, with declining water availability and increasing vulnerability to climate change (high GHG emissions), leaving communities increasingly vulnerable to climate change. Under the project, a new approach will be developed for managing the Puna ecosystem in a way that allows it to manage the ecosystems sustainably, adapt to a changing climate and break the cycle of poverty and environmental degradation. This will include: (i) a new approach by farmers and ranchers; and a new approach by government in supporting them to become productive and ecologically sustainable, even in the face of a changing climate.

The project is designed to bring about a sustained effect beyond the project period through interventions on the ground in component 1. The establishment of mechanisms for long-term financing of ecosystem services (EBA) will be achieved through: (i) establishing the Puna Facility and working to ensure its long-term viability; (ii) leveraging public funds from existing payments for watershed services (PESHERSE) schemes; and (iii) negotiating with private sector actors, including water utilities, to establish mechanisms that promote investment in nature-based solutions, business development, product transformation and value addition.

### 2. Upscaling and replication

The potential for replicating the project approach in other parts of the High Andes is strong, and it is set up by component 3, enhancing the enabling environment for EBA through policy and regulatory framework. The creation of an enabling environment relies on transforming existing policies and regulations to support ecosystem-based adaptation (EBA) responsive technologies, ancestral practices and landscape interventions into local, regional and national governance structures. This will be achieved through: (i) strengthening and building institutional capacity and coordination to mainstream EBA through national and regional governments; (ii) establishing a national monitoring system for the implementation of EBA and gender considerations within the daily operations of MIDAGRI, MINAM and SEERNAP; improving norms, laws, budget and investment guidelines to enhance EBA and climate-resilient approaches in the High Andes region. This will be achieved through: (iii) strengthening the national monitoring and evaluation systems to allow the incorporation of EBA.

### Knowledge and learning

Knowledge has a very central role in the project strategy, which is based on the following principles: (i) knowledge as an asset; (ii) knowledge as a process; (iii) knowledge as a product. A key part of the EBA approach is consciously reviving and disseminating ancient practices and technologies that have been lost through time. These include: (i) creating micro water reservoirs (puquios in Quechua) for recharging aquifers; (ii) constructing stonework irrigation systems to increase water availability, reduce soil erosion and sedimentation or create water troughs; and (iii) holding annual festivals for communal cleaning of irrigation channels. The project will also seek to revive ancestral knowledge that has been lost through time: low valuation of native cultures, cultural erosion, high rates of rural-urban migration. Knowledge is still present, especially in some Quechua communities of Cusco, Apurímac and Puno, who retain forms of organization

---


* Established through Law 30153, PESHERSE provides that water utility companies must make regular payments that must be used for the protection and conservation of ecosystems that provide them with hydrological services.
* Funding proposal, paragraph 19.

**Page 147**

## The iTAP welcomes the inclusion of targeted activities in output 1.2 to make this knowledge revival possible.

Sub-activity 1.2.1 builds the capacity of local experts for the transfer and dissemination of ancestral knowledge on value chains measures, involving yachaytus, kamaypuyos, *talento rurales* and rural extensions, in a capacity-building process that involves the participation of national and subnational institutions. This includes knowledge of ancestral and innovative practices related to the conservation and sustainable use of natural resources, including managing cultivated pastures and growing crops. Other sub-activities facilitate knowledge-sharing among beneficiaries from different gender perspectives and disseminate information materials in accessible formats and languages.

### Sub-activity 1.2.4: Facilitating interdisciplinary dialogue on ancestral practices and innovation

This activity, led by the project team with the support of community organizations, is an important intervention. It is surprising, however, that no other project activities explicitly address the role of youth in the revival of traditional practices or counter the trends of out-migration and erosion of knowledge. A full revival of traditional practices will not be possible without more systematic engagement of the next generation, and the iTAP recognizes this as a priority for its future work.

### The project will collect and analyze information on the impact of Ekh measures

The project will collect and analyze information on the impact of Ekh measures to improve evidence of Ekh effectiveness and inform future decision-making processes. This includes the collection and analysis of baseline data generated by the community monitoring committees, for example on impact measurement of Ekh (off-season planting), as well as other indicators such as cost-effectiveness, carbon cost reduction in business operations, cost-effectiveness, and the type of ecosystems to be inventoried. The project will also collect and analyze information important as part of building the evidence base for the GEF global investment in Ekh approaches.

### Overall, based on the above, the paradigm shift potential of the project is assessed as medium to high

#### Sustainable development potential: Scale: High

The proposed project is aligned with the United Nations 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development (SDG) and its 17 goals. It contributes directly to the following Sustainable Development Goal (SDG): SDG 1 (No Poverty), SDG 5 (Zero Hunger), SDG 5 (Gender Equality), SDG 6 (Clean Water and Sanitation), SDG 10 (Reduced Inequalities), SDG 13 (Climate Action) and SDG 15 (Life on Land). Specific goals anticipated from project interventions are discussed below.

* The funding proposal (para. 19) states that "In the pre-colonial times, Andean worldview was represented by the quipu cords, which were used to record information about the environment, social organization and history of communities. These same reciprocity norms and rituals with respect to humans, animals, rocks, spirits, mountains, lakes, rivers, forests, plants, trees, and other natural resources are still in use today. The Andean worldview is based on their social and natural environment."
* There are many indigenous peoples who have acquired ancestral knowledge on agricultural and livestock practices by ancestral ways and transfer this knowledge to others.
* These are the custodians of traditional ecological knowledge and are known for their capacities and abilities to experiment, adapt, develop and teach good intercultural practices and rural innovations.


---
**Page 148**
---

27\. Environmental co-benefits. As a climate change mitigation co-benefit, GHG emissions will be avoided through the conservation, restoration and sustainable management of Puna ecosystems across 23,914 ha. The proposed intervention measures aim to prevent emissions from existing land uses (e.g., livestock grazing, agriculture, and crop farming), and contribute to carbon sequestration in soil and vegetation, through grassland management, reforestation, afforestation, agroforestry systems development, and wetlands restoration and conservation. Anticipated GHG reductions are calculated using the methodology described in the Green Climate Fund (GCF) Technical Document: Guidance for Land Use, Land Use Change and Forestry, applied though the tool Ex-ACT (version 9.4) developed by the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations. The overall expected reduction is estimated at 102,573 tCO2e/year. This corresponds to years analyzed (5-year implementation phase and 10-year capitalization phase), measured against a scenario baseline.

28\. In addition to carbon sequestration, the project will enhance biodiversity and ecosystem functioning – protecting and restoring peatlands, wetlands and grasslands, which provide a habitat for threatened species. The project will also contribute to water management, nutrient recycling, water regulation, water purification, local climate regulation, water retention and storage, soil conservation, improved air quality by reducing dust emissions of pollutants, and a post blanket protecting the underlying soils from erosion.

29\. Socioeconomic co-benefits. Awareness-raising and capacity-building of local stakeholders will be achieved through training workshops, which will also improve social capital and cohesion. Ancient knowledge and local expertise will be integrated through a participatory approach to ensure that traditional practices are maintained or improved. This will improve household income and nutritional status, and the ITAP welcomes the inclusion of an indicator for this co-benefit in the project’s results framework. Communities with high climate-resilient cash crops are expected to bring about local economic development, with new job opportunities being created through the Puna Facility. The project will provide access to finance for expanding production and agri-processing through the Puna Facility with a 10-year grace period. The extent to which grants and repayable grants (flexible, zero-interest loans) will promote financial inclusion in the region is not yet clear; however, there are no indications that microfinance remains to be seen (see section 1.6 below).

30\. Gender co-benefits. Engagement of women on project livelihood opportunities and decision-making processes will ensure their empowerment. The mainstreaming of a gender approach within all the project activities will promote gender equality and enhance the living conditions for women in the region. This will be achieved through promoting existing proven local initiatives (including EIA measures and climate-resilient value chains) with a gender-sensitive approach, increasing women’s access to credit and other financial services, adopting traditional practices and increasing women’s participation in watershed dialogue decision-making platforms. The project will also provide training for women on agri-processing activities and an indicator for co-benefit 5: “Gender responsive approaches mainstreamed in EIA and CRVC measures.” 

31\. The ITAP assesses the sustainable development potential of the project as high.

### 1.4 Needs of the recipient Scale: High

32\. Peru’s southern High Andes region is highly vulnerable to climate change, and is expected to experience a continuing trend of increased maximum temperatures, decreased


---
**Page 149**
---

minimum temperatures (with frost affecting crops), increased heavy rainfall events, longer dry periods between rains, and ongoing grassy retentive and wetting. High-mountain peatlands and wetlands are particularly vulnerable to the increasing length of dry spells and drought conditions, as well as being more susceptible to fire.

35. Climate change impacts are compounding already unsustainable farming practices by aggravating land-use management vulnerability. A decline in the once-widely used traditional knowledge used to sustainably manage the natural resources that support Andean livelihoods is also contributing to this problem. The lack of access to credit, markets, and cash, are causing declining agricultural productivity and incomes, with young people leaving the rural areas for urban centers. This has led many communities in the region to shift from crop farming to less labour-intensive cattle farming, accelerating the cycle of degradation of the natural grasslands.

36. The target agropastoralist communities in the southern High Andes of Peru region are highly vulnerable to climate change because of their reliance on rain-fed agriculture and extensive livestock production systems. Over 80% of the population living at or near the center of the southern High Andes of Peru districts have a low or very low Human Development Index. Local community members lack access to basic services such as potable water, electricity, health care, sanitation education and farming, and rely on wood, dung and peat as energy sources. Poverty is compounded by high levels of indebtedness, with many communities unable to access credit or access to finance often only an exorbitant costs via informal lenders known as "drop by drop" or "express loans", with low penetration by rural microfinance institutions.

37. Due to the lack of infrastructure and limited access to water regulatory services to maintain flow for hydropower, domestic and industrial use are also vulnerable to climate change impacts. The high levels of poverty in these communities have already contributed to the melting of 51 per cent of the region's glaciers, while changing rainfall patterns and longer drought periods have affected water availability, with decreases in overall rainfall potentially leading to a reduction in the flow for hydropower. In addition, peatland ecosystems could be released into the atmosphere if they continue degrading, with global consequences.

38. Overall, based on the above, the needs of the recipients are rated as high.


---
**Page 150**
---

## Policy and strategy alignment

The project is closely aligned with the targets set out in Peru’s nationally determined contribution (**) supporting the fulfilment of four adaptation measures related to agriculture and water: (**i) AG87 “Natural grassland management to secure food for livestock, including Andean camelids (vicuñas)” considering effects of climate change; (**ii) AG816 “Implementation of agroforestry systems in the Amazon region” considering effects of climate change”; and (**iv) AG213 “Interventions on planting and water harvesting for agricultural water security in the Andes” considering effects of climate change. The project will also contribute to the AAU to quantify the project’s potential contribution to these four targets (see table 21 in annex 2a to the funding proposal), and hopes the actual results will be tracked and fed into the national monitoring system for adaptation, as well as into Peru’s second nationally determined contribution to the Green Climate Fund on climate secretariat on adaptation.

The project will also contribute to implementation of Peru’s National adaptation plan through focusing on land use, hydrographic watersheds, restoration and conservation of ecosystems, recovering traditional knowledge and territorial planning, as well as microfinancing for the promotion of agroforestry systems in the Amazon region. The project is aligned with the 2021-2030 and the Plan for Risk Management and Adaptation to Climate Change in the Agricultural Sector (2018-2025). The project’s activities are also aligned with the National climate-resilient value chains are well aligned with the Road Map for Green Financing, the National Strategy for Economic Growth and the objectives of the National Competitiveness and Productivity Plan 2019–2030.

### Roles of key entities

The roles of the key entities in overseeing or implementing the project are described below. It is noted that two separate entities of GIZ are involved in the project, playing distinct roles, which are explained further below:

- **GIZ** as a German-based private, limited liability, non-profit company owned by the Federal Republic of Germany. It is serving as AE, providing oversight of the project. GIZ, as an implementing entity (IE), will be responsible for preparing and submitting proposals to GCF, with six approved proposals under implementation (FP17, FP12, FP103, FP98, FP65, and FP4). GIZ is also a member of the board of directors of the largest international groupers of capacity development and technical assistance on climate change worldwide.
- **GIZ Peru** as an independent legal structure based in Peru, is the main IE, and is responsible, either alone or with other IEs, for the execution of all of the project activities except those that are carried out by GIZ. It will be responsible for implementing the project under the supervision of GIZ (AE). MIDAGRI has extensive experience of channeling land and grant resources for national and subnational rural development projects from international financial institutions. It is currently implementing several projects with the World Bank, Inter-American Development, the World Bank and the Inter-American Development Bank. The project will also be implemented in close cooperation with other GIZ/MIDAGRI programmes, which form co-finance to the project (see section 1.6 below).
- **Profisangue**, a private non-profit organisation, is the EE responsible for the implementation of the project’s activities under sections 2.0 and 2.1.1 (with GIZ Peru). Profisangue's capacity is indicated by its accreditation as a national direct access entity by GCF since 2013, with two projects under implementation at AE and EE (FP98) and (FP75). Profisangue’s experience in designing and managing several


---
**Page 151**
---

environmental and watershed funds in Peru will be key for its role in managing the Puna Facility and leveraging the MERGE funds for the outer strategy:

(n) SERNANP is the Government entity attached to MINAM, in charge of ensuring the conservation of national protected areas (NPAs). As the governing entity of the national protected area system, SERNANP will be responsible for the management of NPAs, and it will be in charge of the execution of the project activities within the NPAs and buffer zones.

(n) IDM is a non-profit organization that works for the conservation of the natural, cultural and spiritual values of mountain peoples and ecosystems. It has been working in the Andean region since 1985, with projects in Bolivia, Ecuador, Peru, Colombia, Venezuela, and parts of Brazil, Chile, Argentina, Paraguay, Uruguay, and Mexico. IDM works in the departments of Ancash, Piura, Junín and Lima.

### Engagement with local communities, including civil society

Several consultations in the target area have been carried out by GIZ in close coordination with project partners. At all the national level, initial meetings were held with national government programmes operating in the project area to ensure their involvement in the project design process. In addition to this and with SERNANP, the National Institute for Research on Glaciers and Mountain Ecosystems and the Andean Development Institute (IDEA) have been involved as key partners. The GIZ project team also engaged with related donor-funded initiatives, to integrate lessons learned and best practices into the project design process.

The project has also worked closely with a number of local organizations that work in the Puna region, such as the Water Competence Centre, the Centre for Studies on Andean Ecosystems (CEDEA), and the Andean Development Institute (IDEA) to better develop the Andean Ecosystem to understand their perceptions of communities' needs in the context of climate change.

In addition to these meetings, a number of funding proposals consultations were held with national regional and local stakeholders in October and November 2022 and May 2023, with presentations by GIZ and project partners. These meetings were followed by workshops, after which feedback was integrated into the project design. In total, the project has consulted 783 people (31 per cent women and 62 per cent men) out of which 65 per cent were members of the potential beneficiary communities (including indigenous peoples and smallholder farming cooperatives), most of them of the Quechua population (see Annex 7 to the funding proposal). Consultations have been carried out in a participatory manner, with the active participation of communities, producer associations, women, youth and elderly people. During project implementation, GIZ will continue to engage with local stakeholders through its communication plan including "a culturally adequate consultation and communication strategy"10 and a grievance mechanism.

In response to a question from the ITAP about how consultations with Quechua-speaking Indigenous Peoples had been limited to individual communities rather than national bodies: "The project has not yet been able to engage in a dialogue with the political crisis in Peru, making such consultations impossible, but that the first consultations with representatives of the National Federation of Quechua Women, the Andean Women Confederation and with the National Organization of Andean and Amazonian Indigenous Women of Peru", and indicated that annex 7 could be updated to reflect ongoing consultations. The ITAP also noted that the project had already had an initial engagement with the GCF civil society organization network.

### Country ownership of the project is thus seen to be high.

1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

**(a)** MADAGRI will cover EUR 28.67 million through grants (EUR 2.41 million), in-kind contributions (EUR 0.35 million) and co-financing (EUR 25.91 million). This is a 50:50 grant, and 48 per cent (EUR 12.51 million) is made up of co-financing to the project, closely woven into the project logic, and broken down as follows:

- **(i)** public investment projects (implemented by the Agrarian Productive Development Program), including: (a) investments in infrastructure for agricultural innovation and the National Forestry and Wildlife Service; (ii) operational activities (implemented through programs such as Agrovalas, providing matching funding for agriculture); and (iii) capacity building and technical assistance to support target communities to meet the established technical, financial or institutional requirements of the project. This is in line with the project’s exit strategy.
- **(b)** SERNAP will contribute in-kind contributions worth EUR 0.95 million to the implementation of the project in the Arequipa, Apurímac and Lima regions, including staff time commitments in order to coordinate parallel interventions in NAPACs.

**(c)** Co-financing from the Government of Peru and the European Union for Forest Conservation and Development for the project will be fully integrated into the project activities, with a total grant amount of EUR 7.91 million. EUR 4.26 million will be implemented through GEF and EUR 3.65 million will be implemented through EU funding. The co-financing for the Puna Facility is EUR 150,000 to KM.

**In addition**, in order to complement the funding proposal, the project aims to mobilize additional resources from international donors and the private sector to support Pronafac in ensuring the sustainability of the Puna Facility (through sub-activity 2.1.1.2). The Government of Peru has committed EUR 30 million for this purpose, with EUR 5 million specifically allocated towards financing gender and monitoring related activities, and replenishing the Puna Facility’s operational budget.

**Level of concessionality**: For the elements of the project unrelated to the Puna Facility, that is, investment in public goods for EBA and strengthening of the enabling environment, the 100 per cent co-financing from GEF and EU will be provided. The combination of scaling up and scaling up EBA represents a cost-effective and efficient integrated landscape approach to obtaining environmental benefits at scale. This includes the use of innovative financing mechanisms including from High Andes interventions, on the cost-effectiveness of EBA approaches to addressing poverty and climate change in the region. In addition, the economic returns of the project involve private goods, for example Puna Facility investments in family crop farming or agroforestry systems, which are expected to be profitable at scale with relatively high yields and probably with relatively small margins (see discussion below on financial rate of return).

**Economic rate of return**: The economic internal rate of return (ERR) of the project as a whole is 8.2 per cent, which is more than two percentage points above the 6.0 per cent PPA.

social discount rate estimated by the World Bank and MEF. The economic net present value,
based on the 6 per cent social discount rate, is a positive EUR 11.9 million.
A sensitivity analysis shows that the EIRR is even positive with very low carbon prices and with a 30 per cent drop in
emissions from the baseline scenario. However, if the carbon price rises to EUR 25/tonne or more,
the economic net present value remains above the social discount rate of 6 per cent and the economic net present value is therefore positive. The EIRR is also positive with a carbon price as low as EUR 10/tonne.

The financial rates of return on climate-resilient production of the key products of native potatoes and alpacas are estimated by the AE as shown in the table below.

### These potential rates of return, calculated for the financial analysis, appear to the ITAP
to have been overestimated† creating an apparent mismatch between the potential profitability of the initiative and the level of support provided by the facility through pure grants from the Puna Facility. The ITAP acknowledges that the principle underlying the three sub-window design is to provide a mechanism to unlock existing financial and commercial capacities and the level of barriers they face‡ However, it seems short-sighted not to also consider the potential for collective EIA investments in the landscape. While this reality may indeed make sense for collective EIA investments in the landscape. But offering a pure grant to an individual farmer or small group of farmers to finance an EIA is currently
represents a missed opportunity – both to maximize the development of the association’s financial track record and to generate a return for replying the facility.


† The ITAP notes that the total volume of non-repayable grants (EUR 12.5 million) represents only 32 per cent of the total funds in the Puna Facility‡ The ITAP believes that the total volume of pure (non-repayable)
grants is unlikely to generate significant profits. The ITAP notes that GCF is not accredited with GF for on-lending, but believes that the design of the facility to cover only pure grants and repayable grants (the AE’s preferred option) may be a barrier to accessing GCF funds. It also notes that the effect on promoting recipients’ financial literacy and financial inclusion will be limited.
Ideally, the ITAP would like to see a more balanced approach towards financing EIA investments, favourable-term (long term, long period) loans, in order to maximize eligible participants’ capacity to finance their own EIA. This would also help to ensure that they are better equipped to enhance their prospects of accessing future commercial microlending and help to break the cycle of poverty.
The ITAP recommends that the AE and Profondage should think strategically about the
balance between pure grants and repayable grants offered through the Puna Facility. Ideally, the project should apply the principle of minimum concessionality, and avoid giving pure grants to


---
**Page 154**
---

product investments identified as having a high financial internal rate of return when supported without grants - instead providing repayable grants in these cases. This would help to maximize the borrowing organizations' repayment track record and replenish the fund to reach more people.

It is noted that the project explicitly addresses barriers to beneficiaries’ future access to concessional financing for microenterprises, by including a specific component of the interventions in greening microcredits – working with microfinance institutions with a presence in the project area. This will provide loans at concessional interest rates and/or grants for adaptation measures (e.g., Cajamar Los Andes, Caja Guaico, and Financiera Confrancia – an entity of BVA). The project also includes a component to support the development of new microfinance products (including for women) or develop new ones, incorporating the EBA approach in the financial design. This will include capacity building for financial institutions on greening awareness of greener microcredits to promote EBA and Climate Resilient Value Chains through an awareness campaign for potential clients, training for field staff, and technical assistance and cooperation with other actors.

This needs to be complemented by in-depth entrepreneurial and financial training sub-activity 1.1.3 – currently described only in the annexes.


### Replenishing the facility

Although the project proponents anticipate some returns on the repayable grants, this amount is expected only to be 50 per cent of the total disbursed, and applies to only a small proportion (around 20 per cent) of the total disbursement. As such, much of the Puna Facility operates like a small grants facility, and seems unlikely to attract private sector co-financing. The project proponents have recognized this limitation and designed special strategy for replenishment, and has designed sub-activity 2.1.1.2 to address this issue. This activity focuses on accessing funding from the private sector, both those interested in corporate social responsibility investment in the Andes and those companies that depend on the ecosystem services provided by the Puna ecosystems, such as hydrological regulation, carbon sequestration, and other products, who might wish to invest in the facility.


### Greening water use

One option is to develop a new loans or companies, however, would not constitute a long-term replenishment strategy for the Puna Facility. Two more sustainable options exist and could be considered as part of the strategy to develop the greening microcredit option, which may be implied in the funding proposal. The first option is to reach long-term agreements with companies dependent on upper catchment management for water provision such as hydroelectric power generation or other water-dependent industries under a payment for ecosystem services scheme. A second option could be a long-term source of State revenue channelled into the facility by law, such as through a tax on water use in the Andes region. This would require a specific Regulation contribution by mining, hydrocarbon, electricity and other companies to the Environmental Fund. The project proponents have indicated that this is an important part of their plan should be developed in sub-activity 2.1.1.2, explicitly targeting long-term agreements and State revenue contributions.


### Payment for Watershed Services

The efficiency and effectiveness of the project interventions is further enhanced by sub-activity 2.1.2.1 which will help to activate Peru’s existing environmental fund (Fondo Ambiental) as a source of funding for projects targeting the target regions? The project will provide technical assistance to water utilities for five existing microcredit institutions in Arequipa, Lima, Cusco and Apurimac. More information can be found in annex 2.1 to the funding proposal. These activities may support improvements in planning, evaluation and selection of EBA public investment projects; strengthening governance platforms; and improving the capacity of financial intermediaries to provide microfinance services.


### Not directly linked to the Puna Facility

Given that MERISE schemes are clearly circumscribed in how they operate


---
**Page 155**
---

systems, developing methodological tools for implementation, and reaching contractual agreements with communities. A methodology will also be developed, in coordination with MIDAGRI and MINAM, to calculate carbon sequestration in the Puna ecosystems and provide capacity building (technical assistance, training, workshops, seminars, academic) aligned with national legal and institutional arrangements for carbon markets.

The project's overall efficiency and effectiveness is rated as medium.


## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The iTAP believes that the project "Resilient Puna: Ecosystem based Adaptation for sustainable High Andean communities and ecosystems in Peru" has been well planned, considering the complexity of the region. The project is considered one of the most important ones for reducing the vulnerability of High Andean agropastoral communities and downstream water users, and preventing further degradation of ecosystem services that would otherwise lead to ecosystem collapse and significant GHG emissions.

The iTAP recommends that the AE undertake the following actions to strengthen the sustainability of the project:

(a) Include youth who are present in the target regions, while not necessarily being active in farming, in the entrepreneurial training (including financial literacy) through the technical assistance provided by the Puna Facility (sub-activity 2.1.3).

(b) Consider carefully the balance between pure grants and repayable grants offered through the Puna Facility, and apply the principle of minimum concessionality when supporting projects that are not directly related to climate change.

(c) Develop a full business plan for the Puna Facility, explicitly targeting long-term agreements with private sector partners, and long-term sources of revenues, including from natural environmental taxes and payments (sub-activity 2.1.1).

# Chapter 1: Introduction

The "Resilient Puna" Ecosystem-based approaches for sustainable High Andean communities and ecosystems in Peru" Project (hereafter 'Resilient Puna') aims to increase the resilience of Andean communities and ecosystems in Peru through three components: (i) Ecosystem management, conservation and restoration of high Andean ecosystems; an increased access to public goods and services provided by these ecosystems; (ii) Enhanced Ecosystem-based Adaptation (EBA). It is organised into three components: (i) Puna ecosystems are restored, conserved and managed through the implementation of ecosystem-based adaptation measures. Through implementa- tion of ESA measures; (ii) Public and private financing for EBA measures and climate resil- ient livelihoods are in place and accessible for vulnerable communities in the Puna Ecosystem; and (iii) The project will contribute to the development of a gender-sensitive policy and investment instruments. The project will invest in EBA and climate resilient value chains, providing tech- nical assistance and capacity building to local communities and private sector actors directly benefiting the conservation and restoration of 23,914 hectares of High Andean ecosys- tems.

Various international and national instruments have defined guidelines for gender equality in key climate change mitigation and adaptation actions*. Not only from a social but also from an economic perspective. Gender equality is not only about women's rights; it encompasses a number of issues based on the socially constructed relationships between men and women, which vary across cultures, geographies and time. Gender gaps are still wide and highly unequal in many countries. These inequalities are reflected throughout the life cycle, which are reflected in their health, education, employment, participation, decision- making power, access to resources, and exposure to violence, including sexual and gender-based violence, among others. Climate change represents a major driver of biodiversity loss, while eco- logical degradation is one of the main causes of climate change. Gender inequalities and stressors. Persistent gender inequalities underpin both of these crises, which increase vulnerability and reduce resilience in communities, particularly women's ability to main- taining their livelihoods and good living, and their access to rights, goods, and services.

The gender analysis of the Resilient Puna (RP) Project is in harmony with these international principles and guidelines. The RP project will be guided by the following national ones (Gender and Climate Change Action Plan PACC, National Policy on Gender Equality - National Strategic Development Plan to 2030, National Plan against Gender Violence, among other policies). These guidelines are also reflected in the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC), as well as in the access to the social, political, cultural, climatic and economic rights and resources. According to the report of the Commission for the Promotion and Advancement of Women (CELADE) (2017), the impacts of global warming that affect men and women differently, require adaptation strategies that are also gender-sensitive. The RP project will contribute to pro- moting the mainstreaming of a gender perspective in public policies to address climate change, with the aim of reducing vulnerability and increasing resilience at all levels: individual condi- tion, construction of roles and position of men and women in society (UN Women, 2017).

The project has adopted as its main approach "Ecosystem-based Adaptation" (EbA), an ap- proach that promotes working with nature to cope with gradual and extreme climate impacts, which affect the Andean ecosystems. The characteristics of EbA are: (i) It is based on the characteristics identified in order to better understand the gender approach; (ii.) It is a multi-sectoral approach that seeks to reduce vulnerability while at the same time generating social and economic benefits; and,

The Puna Ecosystems Project is a gender-sensitive project that seeks to promote the participation of women in decision-making processes at all levels, from local communities to national institutions, as well as the empowerment of women stems both from traditional norms and the Peruvian State's commitment to human rights treaties... i) It is a collaborative process in which communities, public and private organisations, work together to have an impact at the landscape level and generate sustainable opportunities over time for all parties.

Linking climate change and ESD (Education for Sustainable Development) is both challenging and enriching. It allows to move from analysing the resources, decisions, behaviours, roles and interrelations of men and women with their environment, towards integrating ways and mechanisms to break down the barriers that hinder the full participation of women in decision-making processes and in building climate resilience.

Specifically, the gender analysis has identified the main factors that are hindering women's progress in adapting to a face of climate change: Overburdened care and unpaid domestic work; low participation in decision-making; lack of income; vulnerability of their housing and household assets; and limited access to information. These factors have been identified as the gendered barriers to more equitable climate adaptation. Although the response to this situation requires a multi-sectoral approach, the project has focused on one dimension: women's empowerment. This dimension addressed in the project defines actions that may strengthen the role of women, seeking to "leveling the playing field" for their real and effective involvement in the implementation of the project.

The project recognizes that an adequate vision and inclusion of gender aspects in different stages is essential for ensuring the sustainability of the intervention. For this reason, both the Gender Analysis (GA) and the Gender Action Plan (GAP) of the Resilient Puna project have been developed as a tool to guide the project's implementation, monitoring and evaluation. Therefore, the RFP project will also conduct a mon- itoring strategy that includes gender indicators in order to ensure the effectiveness (no harm or no benefit).

The project will encourage women's participation and leadership to be pro- moted and recognized within the communities which the project will be developed, in many of which they are currently being traditionally excluded. The ultimate goal is to promote the recognition of the gender approach as a visible women’s barriers to contribute reducing asymmetries between men and women in decision-making processes and in building resilience for the rights of people, especially women, within a natural ecosystem. In this sense, having a reading of the gender perspective is essential for ensuring that the project contributes to empower people to build climate resilience and exercise their rights, while protecting the ecosystems on which they depend.

Finally, the methodology to collect information to prepare this gender action plan involved: a desk review of available data and fieldwork data collection techniques. Regarding desk review, it included an analysis of existing data from national and international databases or national mechanisms, as well as a comprehensive review of secondary literature that includes various research reports, articles and papers on the subject from both national and interna- tional organizations. The methodology to collect information in the field was based on: 1) two fields were organized to potential districts of intervention in the departments of Arequipa and Puno; 2) a gender analysis was conducted with the participation of the GAP conducted focus groups, in-depth interviews and surveys to analyze general perceptions about the Resilient Puna project. The survey included questions on women's empowerment, local livelihoods and gender gaps in rural communities. To address gender topics, 89 in- depth interviews were carried out (45 with men and 44 with women). A higher number of interviews with men was due to their greater willingness to be interviewed. Likewise, surveys were conducted among 102 households (63 with men and 39 with women) for the definition of indicators of the Gender Action Plan of the project. Likewise, the baseline reflects as close reality of the situation of women and men at the territorial level as well as the actors involved in the context in which they develop.

# Chapter 2: Information on gender dimensions in the country or the region

## 2.1 Meta level (norms)

Peru is the fourth country with the largest population in South America. In 2020, the population was 32.6 million inhabitants; 50.4% women and 49.6% men. This population lives mostly in urban centers, where 71% of Peruvians live (INEI, 2018). Peru is a country with high levels of inequality, in terms of income, property, access to basic social services, infrastructure and gender. In 2020, monetary poverty increased by 9.9 percentage points and reached 30% of the Peruvian population. This affected 46% of people living in rural areas (8 million people) and 50% of those living in urban areas (8.6 million people) (INEI, 2021b).

In terms of gender inequality, Peru has progressed differently than other countries for the main streams of gender equality: women's empowerment, but it is far from being equal. The most recent 2021 World Economic Forum (WEF) Global Gender Gap Index places Peru 37th out of 156 coun- tries in terms of economic participation and opportunity dimension (WEF, 2020–2021), health and survival, and political empowerment dimensions (WEF, 2020–2021) and 7th place in Latin America. Similarly, the Gender Inequality Index in 2019 placed Peru with 356 points, a position that places it as one of the countries with the highest gender inequality (UNDP, 2020).

Peru is a state that prohibits discrimination and recognises equality as a fundamental human right (Political Constitution of 1993, Art. 2 numeral 2). Article 191 of the Constitution establishes the responsibility of the State to ensure equal treatment between men and women in all areas or that constitute discriminatory acts. However, there is structural discrimination against women in Peru, which reproduces gender inequalities and perpetuates a cycle of violence and other forms of oppression for women, limiting their opportunities and systematically violating their rights. Women's structural discrimination is rooted in the patriarchal nature of society, which attributes to them a physical, economic and decision-making role, as well as the existence of gaps in different areas (reproductive health, employment and income, access to goods and services such as health, education, employment, etc.), and in other areas (such as violence), with the situation worsening in the rural settings. Structural discrimination based on gender does not affect all women equally; it is more severe for indigenous women, Afro-Peruvian, disabled, poor and non-poor women.

The supremacy of the masculine over the feminine is institutionalised in social relations, in public and private spheres, through various cultural and structural patterns (beliefs, prejudices, dogmas, and customs), which are reproduced, transmitted intergenerationally and throughout life. These structures reproduce gender inequalities and maintain men's power between men and men and male privilege. Discrimination against women is rooted in norms that perpetuate the subordination of women to men. The patriarchal nature of society is one of the main instruments used to maintain women in a situation of subordination and as a means of disciplining them to prevent their access to rights. This not only prevents their participation in public life, but also limits their ability to exercise their rights; it also makes it impossible for them to transform these spaces that reproduce patriarchal struc- tures of domination.

According to the National Social Relations Survey in 2019 (INEI/FRAS), for a acronym in Spanish), the index of social tolerance towards violence against women is 59% of the surveyed. When referring to attitudes that support such violence, 53% of male and female respondents


---
**Page 164**
---

state that women should first fulfill the role of mother and wife, and only then can achieve their personal projects (INEI, 2019).

Gender inequality has an impact on development and the economy, but above all on families' wellbeing, where women's unpaid care roles are often performed by polygynous men. In this context, which women tend to have a heavier burden of work and fewer opportunities to achieve their wellbeing. In the context of confinement due to the Covid 19 pandemic, there was a difference of 27 percent in the time spent on domestic chores between men and women (MIPRO, 2020). This burden for women made it more difficult for them to re-enter the job market after the crisis. In Peru, women's participation in the labor force is 45%, while their domestic care work as opposed to 16% of their male counterparts, showing that women spend 27 hours a week on unpaid care and domestic work.

By positioning the gender approach with key partners throughout the project implementation process, it will be ensured that the impact of the project will contribute to the promotion of the rights of women in Peru. To do this, it is necessary to strengthen the process of making visible the disadvantaged conditions in which rural women's participation in communities and decision-making spaces related to natural resources and land use takes place. The evidence shows that the empowerment of rural women has positive effects on household income, food security and nutrition, in general in families.

### 2.2 Macro level (policies and laws)

At the international level, Peru has ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW). It has also ratified the Inter-American Convention on the Prevention, Punishment and Eradication of Violence Against Women (ILO No. 190) and the Protocol to Prevent, Suppress and Punish Trafficking in Persons, Especially Women and Children (Protocol of Belo Pará), which establishes international standards to prevent, punish and eradicate violence against women. Peru has also signed other international commitments such as the Beijing Declaration and World Platform (1995) and the 2030 Agenda, whose Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) include gender equality in all its dimensions. SDG 5 seeks to ensure women's full and effective participation and equal opportunities for leadership at all decision-making levels.

At the national level, in addition to the constitutional norms, the Peruvian State has laws, regulations and guidelines that are mandatory for all levels of government. There are different policies that promote equal opportunities between men and women in public policies and plans, thus contributing to the achievement of gender equality as a strategic objective. These objectives: 1. To reduce violence against women; 2. To guarantee women's access to and participation in decision-making spaces at all levels of society, including political, economic and social rights; 5. To reduce institutional barriers that hinder equality in the public and private spheres.

These policies stipulate the obligation to promote the adoption of egalitarian values, practices, attitudes and behaviors between men and women, to guarantee the right to non-discrimination and equal treatment against violence. They seek to protect all members of society and all their rights (civil, political, economic, social, and cultural) and also establish obligations to promote the physical, economic, and decision-making autonomy of women in society and in public administration. These policies are based on the principle that women's empowerment is a priority for the State, with special attention to the most vulnerable groups. With respect to gender mainstreaming in the State, it basically consists of incorporating an approach that includes the promotion of equal information, the participation of women in spaces for dialogue and decision-making, as well as the incorporation of this approach in management documents, programs and projects.

# Main national instruments on gender

* Political Constitution of Peru (1993), Art. 2 inc. 2 (equality and non-discrimination based on sex or gender) (State responsibility for removing obstacles to equality or that constitute discrimination).

## National Policies

* **National Policy No. 11** (seeking to combat discrimination and promote equal opportunities between men and women).
* Supreme Decree No. 054-2011-PCM Bicentennial Plan: Peru 2011 (throughout the text, gender equality is promoted as a fundamental principle for achieving equal opportunities and universal access to basic services are proposed).
* National Policy No. 36 (adopted by the Government of Peru in 2005) proposes to eradicate discrimination and ensure equal access to opportunities and conditions, in a fair and inclusive society that leaves no one behind.

## Laws and regulations of legal force

* Law N° 27914 (2011), which amends Criminal Code (1991) by legislating Decree No. 1323 (2017) and Legislative Decree No. 1418 (2018): criminalises the dissemination of defamatory, pornographic or obscene images of a sexual nature in cases public servants and harassment, sexual harassment, sexual blackmail and dissemination of images, audiovisual material or text messages with sexual content; and modifies the procedure for sanctioning sexual harassment.
* Law N° 27943 (2011), which amends Decree No. 586-2000-PCM, to ensure equal opportunities or treatment in job offers and access to educational training.
* Law N° 27942 (2011), to prevent and punish sexual harassment.
* Law N° 30564 (2015), to prevent and punish violence against women and men.
* Legislative Decree No.1998 (2012): Law of Organization and Functions of the Ministry of Women and Gender Equality, which establishes the functions of the Ministry for women and gender mainstreaming.
* Law N° 30645 (2015), to prevent, punish and eradicate violence against women and men in public spaces.
* Law N° 30564 (2015), to prevent, punish and eradicate violence against women and men in private spaces.
* Law N° 30982 (2017): amends Law 24566, General Law of Peasant Communities, to strengthen the role of women in Peasant Communities.
* Legislative Decree No. 1008 (2017), which establishes measures to guarantee the care and protection of victims of violence against women and members of the family group during their stay at shelters or other places of refuge.
* Law N° 31000 (2020): modifies norms of the electoral legislation to guarantee parity and equal opportunities in elections for public office.
* Law N° 30982, (2017) that amends Law 24566, General Law of Peasant Communities, which establishes that at least one-third of the members of the Community Board, the body responsible for the administration and management of the community, must be women.
* Supreme Decree No. 03-2018-JUS-MMP: promotes mandatory training on gender perspective for the prevention of violence and discrimination in public administration.

## Supreme Decrees and Resolutions

* Supreme Decree No. 054-2011-PCM/MMP: Public entities of the National Government and Regional Governments create a commission, committee or working group, for Gender Equality.
* Supreme Decree No. 020-2018-JUS; National Human Rights Plan 2018 - 2021: establishes four strategic objectives for the protection of women: (1) Reduce violence against women and men in public spaces and private spaces; (2) Ensure that women have access to justice, guarantee the exercise of women's sexual and reproductive rights; (4) Guarantee the generation of women's own income.

- Supreme Resolution N° 129-2018-PCM, creates the multisectoral commission of a temporary nature against VAW, the fight against which constitutes a national policy.
- Supreme Decree No. 007-2018-MIMP, sets out the National Action Plan for Gender Equality to 2021 and sets as a priority guideline to promote equality and non-discrimination between men and women, as well as to guarantee the protection of women against all forms of violence.
- Supreme Decree No. 008-2019-MIMP, approves the National Policy on Gender Equality.
