Although these regulations are in place, the main challenge is their limited implementation. The persistent gap between what women and men are included in the insufficient political will for the effective mainstreaming of the gender approach, the absence of adequate diagno- ses, interventions, and monitoring mechanisms, and the lack of a clear communication strategy so that they understand that if they are not committed to closing gender gaps and their ade- quate management, all the actions carried out within the framework of their functions and contrib- uring to deepening them.

### 2.3 Mesa level (institutions)

Peru has a governing body for public policies for women since 1995 (Legislative Decree N°866 then MIMP) and now (MIMP) since 2017. Since 2012, it changed its name to the Ministry of Women and Vulnerable Populations (MIMP, for its acronym in Spanish), with Legislative Decree N° 1094. Today, this is the governing body for addressing national and sectoral policies on wom- en's rights, gender equality, women's empowerment, and the promotion of women's participation and strengthening of gender mainstreaming in policies, plans, programs and projects of the State. The Ministry has a strong role to play in promoting gender equality and needs to be strengthened, especially for the articulation with all sectors and levels of govern- ment. This is because it is not only a matter of policy but also of political will that allows it to fulfill its role. This weakness expands with the continuous political instability, absence of long-term state planning, and the lack of coordination between the executive branch and the current Parliament that avoids debates on key issues like the sexual rights for women and girls.

However, there is a strong social movement that defend women's and LGBTQ+ human rights, including the National Network for the Promotion of Women, the Stop Street Harassment col- lective, the Manuks Llamo Movement and the Floria Tristan Peruvian Women Centre (a member of the International Federation of Anti-violence against Women). There are also rural and indigenous women's movements with groups and organizations such as: the National Organization of Rural Women (ONAR), the National Association of Indigenous Women, the Federación Nacional de Peasant, Artisan, Indigenous, Nature and Sabad Women of Peru (FE- MABRIN), the Peruvian Federation of Women Workers (FEPERTRABAJO), women's associations and public policies favorable to women, carrying out awareness campaigns towards public opinion and gender equality cases before national and international judicial bodies.

In Peru, the Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ) GmbH participates in the Gender Roundtable for International Cooperation in Peru (MESAGEN). This is a coordinating body for international cooperation in Peru. Its objective is to contribute to gender equality and the empowerment of women, girls and adolescents, within the framework

# 2.4 Micro level (target groups)

In Peru, there are marked patterns of gender-based roles and/or behaviours, and historically established economic and social inequalities about women's and men's assets and opportunities persist, leading to greater discrimination in their access to options for full development. In both political and economic participation, men have a large advantage over women. Women also face more difficulties in accessing education, health services, and in control over time available for work and training. According to the World Economic Forum (WEF) 2021 Global Gender Gap Report, which shows only setbacks in the wage gap, ranking 128th out of 144 countries in the world.

## Health

Andean and indigenous women are the population group with least access to prenatal care. In 2019, 57% of Andean women received prenatal care during pregnancy as attended by a medical professional compared to 39.5% of women living in urban areas. Likewise, in the same year, infant care at institutional deliveries were reported, with a difference of 18.5 percentage points (60.4% rural versus 41.9% urban). In addition to the limited institutional care for deliveries, the deliveries that took place in a health facility were attended by nurses and not by medical professionals (17.4% rural versus 4.2% urban) (INEI, 2021).

Regarding the birth rate and teenage pregnancy, although the four departments prioritized by the project of the National Institute for Family Planning present the lowest rates in the country, the Department of Apurímac presents a ratio of 19.7% of teenage pregnancy, compared to 5% in the department of Cusco. The percentage of women who use modern contraceptive methods and family planning needs in the same departments, with Puno having the lowest percentage of modern contraception use of the four departments prioritized by the project (See Table S1-INEI, 2021).

In 2020, acute respiratory infections (ARI) in children under five years of age were higher in rural areas than urban areas. The percentage of ARI cases was 48.9%, compared to 37.6% in households where charcoal is used as fuel for cooking. 12.9% compared to 7.3% of cases when firewood, straw or agricultural waste is used. On the other hand, acute diarrhoea disease (ADD) was more frequent among children under five years old living in rural areas than urban areas, with a higher percentage of girls suffering from the disease, with a higher percentage of boys (9.9%) than girls (8.8%) (INEI, 2021).

Education: Literacy rates of women in the departments prioritised by the project exceed the national average (8% for women and 3% for men). In the case of Apurímac, 20% of women are literate compared to 5.9% at national level; in Cusco, 16% of women are literate and 3.8% at national level; in Puno, 14.2% of women are literate as opposed to 7.6% for men. Arequipa has a lower percentage of literacy, with 4.7% illiterate women (4.7%) and illiterate men (5.3%).

In 2021, the net school attendance rate was 91.4% for females and 91.1% for males (INEI 2022). Also, by 2021, Cusco had a higher net primary school attendance rate for girls than for boys; in Arequipa, Puno, and Apurímac, the opposite was true. In Arequipa, Puno, and Apurímac, the net secondary education attendance rate for adolescent girls aged 12 to 16 than for boys in the same age group, while Puno has the largest gap in favour of adolescent boys (MINEDU, 2020).

As at primary level, there is no significant difference between the sexes in terms of literacy or school level (See Table N.2). However, at the secondary level, inequality reappears, to the detriment of adolescent girls: In 2020, 71.9% of females compared to 76.7% of males attended a second-ary education course in Arequipa; 83.5% and 84.4%, respectively, in Cusco; 80.2% and 82.3%, respec-tively, in Puno (MINEDU, 2020). This is due to the fact that girls are more likely than boys to drop out of school, caused by economic or family reasons (45.8 rural, 43.9 urban); pregnancy; lack of interest in studies; and graduation or studies at an academy (11% rural and 27.5% urban) (MINEDU, 2020).

Income and employment: Between 2010 and 2019, the department with the highest income gap was Apurímac, with 28.2% more women than men with no income of their own. It is followed by Arequipa (27.4%), Cusco (26.5%) and Puno (23.8%). During the pandemic, the income gap narrowed in all department, but with two different dy-namics: In Apurímac, Arequipa, and Cusco, women with no income of their own had seen it rise; in Puno, the percentage of women without any income of their own had risen, the percentage of women fell significantly. One possible explanation is that, faced with the economic crisis, women have been forced to leave their homes and work for enter-prises to generate income. In Arequipa and at the national level, the reverse is true: confine-ment has made it more difficult for women to find employment. As a consequence, both percentages of people with no income of their own have risen sharply, more than women, narrowing the gap between the two (INEI, 2020).

Regarding employment, in rural areas, 60% of women are engaged in agricultural work. Sales and services represent only 41% of job positions for women in urban areas and 17.5% in rural areas (INEI/ENDES, 2021). At the national level, 38% of women aged 15-64 who are self-employed or work as unpaid family workers were engaged in agricultural activities. In contrast, only 9% of women in agricultural occupations receive a salary. 37.6% are unpaid family workers and only 46.3% of women in the informal sector received income in cash or in kind (INEI/ENDES, 2021). Among those who did not receive income in cash or in kind for their work, 32% were in agricultural occupations (INEI/ENDES, 2021).

Paid and unpaid domestic work: In general, in Peru, there is a higher percentage of women in informal employment due to the greater burden of unpaid domestic work, which reduces or prevents them from having a paid job. Domestic chores are often done by family members, like children, who are not able to attend school or work. Women with young children and working women assume 23 hours more unpaid work per week than men (39 hours and 28 minutes vs. 15 hours). The lack of infrastructure for childcare services is a major challenge in Peru. It is challenging as some of the occupations they carry out are not perceived as work and are not reported to the National Institute of Statistics and Informatics (INEI) or the Ministry of Labor of the informal sector (INEI/ENDES, 2021). While the burden of unpaid and care work at home has historically fallen on women, during the pandemic, the closure of schools and the saturation of the health system have increased this burden. Women assume a greater burden of work on their families for the duration of their confinement at home.

Participation in decision-making: Since the enactment of Law No. 29735 (Law of Political Party Representation), which established national-authority candidates, there has been an increase in women's participation at these levels! In 2021, 49 women and 81 men were elected as national authorities. Women represented only 36% of the total number of candidates in the previous period (2016 - 2021) to 57.30%. However, women's subnational representation is still very low: In 2021, there was no representation at all for women as national authorities representatives from the departments of Apurímac, Arequipa and Cusco. For Puno, there is no representation of women in Parliament, compared to five male congressmen representing this department (CNPE, 2022).

In general terms, the situation of women's political participation reflects the situation of all forms of violence against women in Peru at a national level and at the departmental level.

Gender Violence: In 2023, 55% of Peruvian women were victims of violence perpetrated by their husband or partner. Both physical violence and sexual violence were reported in higher proportions in rural areas, 28.6% and 7.2%, respectively; if ethnic self-identification is taken into account, the percentage of women reporting physical violence was highest among indigenous women (30%), followed by black women (28.6%) and white women (15%). Women from urban areas reported higher percentages of sexual violence than those in rural areas (7% vs 4.9%, respectively). The situation is more complex when considering the departmental level: while the percentage of physical violence is higher in Cusco and sexual violence is higher in Puno, both show a high prevalence of both types of violence.

In terms of seeking help for gender-based violence, 16% of women reported having sought help from a health service, 20.5% from a police station, and 34.8% from a non-governmental organization (NGO). The most common reasons given by victims not seeking help was because it was not necessary (42.9%), followed by a feeling of shame (16.3%) or fear of retaliation (10%). Other reasons included fear of being judged negatively, fear of losing custody of children, fear of causing problems to the family, and fear of being stigmatized (all 5% each). The percentage of women who reported having sought help was higher in Cusco than in other regions (38.7% vs 20%, respectively).

Victims of trafficking registered with the National Police of Peru were also more likely to be victims of violence perpetrated by their husband or partner, and this was especially true for those living in the departments prioritised by the project. In 2020, Cusco reported 19 trafficked women and Arequipa reported 3 (INEI, 2020). The same trend is observed when considering victims of violence perpetrated by a relative or other person known to them (INEI, 2020). Regarding femicides, in 2022, 7 femicides were reported in Cusco and 4 in Arequipa (MINVOP, 2022).

# Conclusion 1 | project format

The formal right to equality faces multiple barriers that are imprisoned in the persistence of gender inequalities and discrimination in all social spheres, constituting one of the main obstacles to sustainable development that leaves no one behind. In Peru, there is still no adequate guarantee for the full enjoyment of this right due to the presence of a 19% placed woman in a situation of vulnerability. In spite of this, there are some advances regulatory and institutional measures that have been adopted at different levels of government to mainstream gender perspective in public policies. In this line, working with political partners and agriculture and water stakeholders and users provides an opportunity for the State to address these issues. The main challenge is to ensure that the State has the need to carry out actions that, in the long term, collaborate in achieving a social and institutional transformation towards greater equality in highly masculinized sectors and on a post-pandemic context.

## Chapter 3: Gender dimensions in the sector

### 3.1 Meta level

On July 28, 2022, the UN General Assembly adopted resolution A/RES/76/369 that recognizes access to clean and healthy environment as a human right, as well as the need for full implementation of environmental agreements. It also recognizes that climate change, overuse of natural resources, deforestation, desertification, soil degradation, pollution, and waste prevent the full enjoyment of this right. According to the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC), climate change is a gender issue because women are more vulnerable to its effects; climate change can aggravate gender inequalities and increase women's vulnerability.

One of the most important international instruments in the field of women's human rights is the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW, 1979). This instrument establishes an obligation for the gender equality and equal opportunity to take measures against discrimination against her and to guarantee their access to the same rights as men. The CEDAW also includes a series of recommendations that include the importance of reinforcing the collection and analysis of disaggregated data, ensuring policy coherence, improving institutional capacity in the areas of gender equality and women's rights, and capacity building and access to technology. Similarly, with regard to rural women's rights, it recommends strengthening rural economic systems, promoting local employment and livelihood opportunities for women.

The Declaration and Platform for Action of the Fourth World Conference on Women in Beijing, 1995, is a comprehensive document that sets out the goals and strategies for achieving the full enjoyment of gender mainstreaming in all areas, including the environment. This platform includes 12 prioritized axes, one of which is "Women and the environment," highlighting the need for women's participation in decision-making spaces, the incorporation of their interests and


---
**Page 172**
---

concerns in programmes and policies, and the need to establish mechanisms to measure the impact of environment and development policies on women’s lives. In this framework, the Com- mission on the Status of Women (CSW) and the Economic Commission for Latin America and the Caribbean (ECLAC), focused exclusively to gender equality and the empowerment of women and to the follow-up of the Millennium Development Goals (MDGs) related to gender equality and the empower- ment of women and girls is a key aspect of climate change and sustainable development (CW66). It also highlights and reaffirms the leadership of women and girls as key agents of change in addressing climate change and sustainable development, including in relation to climate change. (C6-N 2012/7, United Nations, Economic and Social Council: Commission on the Status of Women)

The United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) seeks to stabilise greenhouse gas concentrations at levels that prevent human intervention that puts the envi- ronment at risk. The UNFCCC is also a framework for action in relation to gender equality, with the Lima Work Programme on Gender to promote gender balance and gender-responsive climate policy. In this context, the 2015 Paris Agreement (COP21) includes provisions on gender mainstreaming in all climate action. In this framework, the Paris Agreement, a commitment by UN- FCCC member states to limit global warming to less than 2 degrees Celsius, includes three lines of action: reducing emissions; adapting to the impacts of climate change; and building resilience. The agreement also sets out a series of actions, including mentions linked to gender equality and women’s empowerment, as measures that must be respected, promoted and taken into account in responses to climate change.

The 2030 Agenda and the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) include SDGs linked to gender equality and women’s empowerment. The SDG 5 is about achieving gender equality and the em- powerment of women and girls, as well as other SDGs that include gender-specific targets. For example, SDG 12 is about responsible consumption and production; SDG 13 is about climate action and the need to pay special attention to women, girls and people in vulnerable situations; SDG 14 is about reducing inequalities and including all people in social, political and economic life; SDG 15 is about protecting terrestrial ecosystems. In sum, the agenda highlights that gender equality is necessary to achieve the SDGs.

### Macro level

At the national level, Peru was one of the first countries to have a Gender and Climate Change Action Plan (PAGCC, for its acronym in Spanish), aimed at state action to reduce gender gaps in the face of climate change. The PAGCC includes actions related to access to natural resources, energy, food security, health, education, risk management. The PAGCC empha- sizes that the main actors are women and girls, who are more vulnerable than men to climate change. Activities are mainly focused on technical capacity building, generation and management of dis- aggregating data, gender-sensitive planning and budgeting, the promotion of gender equality in de- velopment of policies and instruments, and the development of mitigation and adaptation measures from a gender perspective, which are also included in the Regulations of the Frame- work Law on Climate Change.

Peru’s National Policy on Gender Equality (PNGE) is in line with the main international decla- rations and agreements on the protection of women's rights and gender equality**, identifies


---
**Page 173**
---

structural discrimination against women as a public problem. This policy points out that discrimination against women is caused by socio-cultural patterns that privilege the masculine over the feminine and the dominant over the subordinate, leading to a system of practices producing inferior and subordinate status that restricts their opportunities, and economic, social, political and cultural benefits (MIMP, 2019).

In 2019, the National Policy on Gender Equality (PNG, 2019) was approved, whose objective is to “address gender equality issues and consequences of the persistence of structural discrimination against women”, in accordance with the CEDAW and the Beijing Platform for Action. The PNG sets out a framework for action that includes: promoting economic and social rights” and considers the guidelines 4.2: Increase women’s access to and control over land, water resources, natural resources, technology and financial services; and 6.3: Grant rights linked to water and its associated goods in rural areas, for domestic and productive use and for environmental protection. The PNG also includes gender equality in the management and natural resources, with the participation of women, in a context of climate change, where it is important to consider the impacts on women’s access to land, water, forests, fisheries and associated goods, management of the conservation, recovery and sustainable use of eco-systems and natural resources, training, technical assistance and financing for women’s empowerment. The PNG also includes gender equality in the design and implementation of development with a gender perspective and the elimination of institutional and socio-cultural barriers that hinder women from participating fully in decision-making processes related to development management, such as the generation of disaggregated statistical information and follow-up information on the results.

Regarding spaces and mechanisms for the advancement of women and gender equality associated with adaptation to the impacts of climate change, the government of Peru has the Ministry of Environment (MINAM) and the Ministry of Women’s Rights and Gender Equality (MIMP). Both ministries are governing bodies for national gender equality and environmental policies, respectively. They are responsible for designing guidelines, policies, norms, programs, projects and budgets related to gender equality.

At the subnational level, through the Supreme Decree 005-2017-MIMP, the government of Peru established a mechanism for Gender Equality in each of the entities of the national and subnational levels. This mechanism is responsible for promoting the mainstreaming of the gender approach within state institutions and the reduction of gender inequalities at the local level. The mechanisms are also responsible for coordinating actions at the national, subnational and local levels of government, including the agencies involved in the implementation of the PNIG.

There is also a National Committee on Women and Climate Change - CONAMUCCO. This organization was set up by MINAM and made up of representatives of more than 50 organizations to strengthen women’s participation in climate action. The CONAMUCCO was created in response to the need for greater representation of women from vulnerable indigenous peoples in environmental decision-making. This space was created in the framework of the development of the PNIG, which includes gender equality as a cross-cutting theme and a prior consultation with indigenous organizations in Peru (including women’s organizations), highlighting the importance of including women in the design and implementation of sectors and levels of government, taking the PAACC and the PNIG as a reference and considering the need to promote the participation of women from vulnerable groups. The promotion of education for women and girls. The Multisectoral Strategic Plan (PEMIG) operationalizes the PNIG at the national level and includes gender equality as one of its public institutions responsible. As the MIMP is the lead agency for gender issues, the sectors must report to it on their progress in implementing the PNIG. It is important to note, as an opportunity, that the PEMIG also includes a cross-cutting theme: interculturality and intergenerational


---
**Page 174**
---

and includes a participatory process that includes indigenous and women’s organisations, among other key actors at the national level.

### 3.2 Mesa level

Women in the Andes, due to their socially constructed roles and responsibilities, and the still existing conceptions of male superiority struggle with a position of inferiority regarding access and control over resources, especially land and water. This is particularly true for women who produce agricultural products. Moreover, their responsibilities lie in the reproductive sphere, like domestic labor and childcare, which are not recognized as productive work and do not lead to remunerated paid employment or participation in decision-making spaces. This translates into under-representation in different types of organisations. Despite this, women actively participate in the management of household resources and production activities. While men are responsible for their participation and decision making in agriculture and husbandry, women are responsible for water supply, food preparation, childcare, cleaning, cooking, sewing, weaving and hygiene in family members, including the ill and disable (Carillo & Ríos, 2002).

The livelihoods of rural communities in the Andes are scarce and quite vulnerable to climatic factors. The main sources of income for these families are livestock products, sale of natural salt and iodine. The quantities available for the sale of these products are highly variable from year to year due to the high vulnerability of pastoral systems (highly fluctuating prices of pastures for livestock feed, access to water sources) and environmental factors (droughts and floods). These families also depend on the production of crops such as potatoes (main staple), maize (strong winds, low temperatures) as well as the scarcity of water resources do not allow them to grow agricultural crops or high nutritional quality pastures for livestock. Due to this precarious livelihood situation, women are forced to engage in informal activities and sell their products at local centres to earn supplementary income. In the absence of husbands and young people, house- hold chores fall on the shoulders of women who have to balance these responsibilities with other tasks such as looking after children or elderly family members. Women also wish to migrate permanently to the cities if their husbands can find income opportunities to make ends meet.

Family economy in rural households: During fieldwork, on the question of who contributes the most money to household expenses, women’s answers reveal a new situation: 40% of women consider that both husband and wife contribute equally. The remaining distribution is equal from both sides: husband and wife, while men’s answers are different: 46% think that husband and wife contribute in a similar way, but 42% state that it is men who con- tribute more than the woman (12% say that women contribute more). Women consider that the most important being textile handicrafts (35%), apone on foot (20%) and guinea pigs (17%). Raising livestock is considered by both men and women as a way of generating income, enabling them to obtain a greater income for their households. On the other hand, men control the income from the sale of agricultural products such as potatoes (43%), maize (35%), and wheat (19%). They show little or no participation in the commercialisation of textile products (2%).

### Domestic labour: Interviews conducted in the field with rural women from Cusco and Puno (November 2022) indicate that 58% of rural women dedicate 6 hours of domestic work per day, and 20% dedicate more than 10 hours. In contrast, only 34% of men dedicate less than 6 hours a day; meanwhile, only 21% of rural men spend 4 hours on domestic chores, while only 9% spend more than 4 hours. In this respect, 18% of women mentioned the acquisition of improved cookers or lique- fied gas as an important factor in reducing their workload. On average, women dedicate 30 minutes a day to cooking and cleaning. If we add 6 hours of domestic work to 9 hours of productive work, the total workload is at least 13 hours per day for rural women. In contrast, men dedicate only 45 minutes on average when they start their work at 8 am and finish at 8 pm, which means a total of 18 hours. It is known that in many cases, this time does not include the time spent on domestic chores such as washing water or firewood. On the other hand, 70% of rural men reported a domestic workload of less than 4 hours, and 52% a productive workload of more than 9 hours. This makes a total of less


---
**Page 175**
---

than 13 hours of work per day. This imbalance is greatest in households where men are absent to work outside the community, and where women head the household (40%). In terms of cooking fuel, most rural women cook over open fires using wood or dung as fuel, while only a few use modern stoves. Most rural women have traditional cookers and use firewood and dung as fuel. Even in the case of rural households that have electricity, it is used only for internal lighting, and gas-powered electric cookers or cooking stoves are not available to most women.

Participation and leadership: The interviews conducted in the field indicate that rural women do not participate equally with men in decision-making processes at all levels. In interviews, 55% of women report having held community government positions, 20% lower than that reported by men (74%). Women are also underrepresented as leaders or decision-makers, such as lower levels of formal education, speaking a language other than Spanish, low empowerment and lack of access to information. To date, there is still a wide gap in the presence of women as decision-makers. As noted above, 50% of rural women report having held community government positions while the project represents 25% compared to 75% for men. This same gap is reflected in the decision-making spaces in the territories and in the community, participation spaces in the four priority development areas.

Access to water: According to interviewees working during fieldwork, the availability of safe water is less than or equal to 50%. The other half of households obtain water from various sources. In the Apurímac region, 48% of rural households receive water supply from a public network inside the dwell- ing, 13% from a well, and 29% from a spring (INEI, 2016). In the rural Andes, the main carriers of water for human and animal consumption are springs. However, in the Apurímac region, the task of carrying water from a spring to the household is not a burden assigned only by women in rural households. It is likely that families can easily access 20% springs that arise from the groundwater table. Recently, 31% of interviewees reported having had access to wells or other well- available reservoirs to capture rainwater. Irrigation tasks in plots and pastures in highland areas are considered a women's task.

Health: In addition to the scarcity and distance of the health posts, a significant proportion of rural women and men distrust the medical personnel who provide healthcare. Only 43% of women and only 28% of men trust the medical personnel at the health post. It is worth mentioning that this degree of acceptance is higher in the districts of Puno: Nuñoa (57%) and Daniel Alcides Carrión (60%). The distrust of rural women is greater than that of men, and even more so in the districts of Puno. The reasons for rural women's distrust are mistrust and communication difficulties due to the use of a language other than Spanish. The main reason given by interviewees was the lack of trust in the medical personnel at the health post. Interviewed women stated that this is a reality that they face frequently: 85% of men had the same opinion, while only 60% of rural women agreed with it. In addition to distrust, it's mentioned that they appeal to peasant round (rondo campesino), mostly made up of men.

Education: Education among former population of the SHAP area shows very low rates. (See Table IV-1). The majority of the rural population in the Apurímac region did not pass the primary level of education, with 50% or more of their population, about one third (31%) of them having no formal education at all and another third (34%) having only a primary education. The level of access to higher education in the Apurímac area stands out as 27%, while in the rest of the departments this level is around 3%. The gender gap across the four priority development areas shows that secondary and secondary education is between 9 and 17 points with a more dramatic situation in Puno.

**Page 181**

Membership in a producer’s organisation, be it an association or a cooperative, is key to enable both women and men to adapt to climate change. This membership provides manageable transitional costs for women and men, as well as access to information and services that increase their resilience, based on the ecosystems in which they live. For this reason, although there is no specific gender equality indicator in the SHAP project, it is important to ensure that membership in a productive organisation is central to a project baseline, as well as to the Gender Action Plan (GAP) and the Gender Climate Change Action Plan (GCCAP), for policy implementation. Finally, in addition to their participation as members, the occupation of leadership positions by women within the organisations will contribute the closing of gaps, particularly those related to access to information, technical advice and training to improve their living conditions, both at the level of their families and their communities.

**Conclusion 2 for the project**

In general terms, women in the SHAP area face more barriers than men that hinder the achievement of gender equity in climate change adaptation measures. Women face unequal access to resources (use, control) and receive lower income. They also have limited access to education and training opportunities. In addition, they are less likely to have technical skills/capacity to develop and implement nature-based solutions and climate-resilient agri-cultural practices. The project proposes the strengthening of technical capacities, capacity building and awareness raising among women in order to address these barriers and weaknesses in institutions and regulation policies, to implement nature-based solutions and climate-resilient agricultural practices. This will be done through gender-sensitive and gender-regulated information data, systematization of experiences, lessons learnt, behavioral change communication strategies, and the development of mitigation and adaptation measures.

Chapter 4: Gender responsiveness and expertise, as well as gender equality in the partner organisation(s)

Public Partner Organisations

Pero is one of the first countries to have a Gender and Climate Change Action Plan (PAGCC, 2015), aimed at state action to reduce gender gaps in the fight against climate change. This Plan focuses on four main areas: access to information, education, training, health, education, risk management. Among the main scopes designed, this project proposes the strengthening of technical capacities, generation and management of disaggregated information (gender-sensitive data), capacity building and awareness raising among women and men policies and instruments and the development of mitigation and adaptation measures with a gender focus, which is also included in the Regulation of the Framework Law on Climate Change.

67


---
**Page 182**
---

In 2019, the National Policy on Gender Equality was approved (PNIG, 2019)[^1] whose objective is to "address, from various angles, the causes and consequences of the persistence of structural discrimination against women in Peru". The PNIG sets out a series of actions for implementation. Priority objective 4 of the PNIG states: "Guarantee the exercise of women's economic rights and their control over productive resources", which corresponds to guideline 6 of the PNIEC. Related services are: 4.2.3 Granting of rights linked to water and its associated assets in rural areas, for domestic and productive purposes; 4.2.5 Strengthening the capacity of women's organizations to manage ecosystems and natural resources, with the participation of women, in a context of climate change.

In both cases, it is expected to work on the formalization of agrarian property, women's rights linked to water and associated goods, conservation management, recovery and sustainable use of common property resources, as well as the strengthening of women's organizations and their enterprises. Likewise, priority objective 5 of the PNIG is linked to the development of capacities in women's organizations for the generation and management of information on gender equality and socio-cultural barriers that hinder equality; and objective 6 of the same policy, associated with the generation and management of statistical information disaggregated by sex, and to be developed through the PNIEC. The PNIG is a guideline that complements the PNIEC in the sense of the previous paragraph, through Supreme Decree No.005-2017-MIMP. The Peruvian government has established a National Intersectoral Committee (PNIEC) for the implementation of the PNIEC at the national and Regional Governments, including the attached bodies. The main purpose of these policies is to address gender inequalities in the institutional level, as well as at the level of goods and services of State bodies and public entities, with a view to promoting the actions and strategies involved in the implementation of the Related Pura Project.

At national level, public agencies in Peru are required by regulation (Supreme Decree No° 005-2017-MIMP) to include gender equality as an objective in their policies and programs. The Ministry of Women and Vulnerable Populations (MINVAM) is the institution specifically responsible for this issue. MINVAM, together with other institutions such as the Ministry of Environment (MINAM), are governing bodies for national gender equality and provide support to the PNIEC. They have established a National Intersectoral Committee on policies, norms, programmes and budgets related to women, environment and climate change response.

MINAM is the ministry that has shown greater interest in incorporating the gender approach both at the institutional level and in its public policies. In 2017, MINAM created the Working Group on Climate Change and Gender Equality (GACC) as a mechanism for coordination and installed the first National Committee on Women and Climate Change - CONAMUCC, made up of representatives from various institutions that are part of the GACC. The objective is to strengthen women's participation in climate action. This committee responds to the need to strengthen the presence and participation of indigenous peoples and women in environmental decision-making processes at all levels. MINAM has also been a pioneer in the implementation of the Framework Law on Climate Change, which included a prior consultation with indigenous peoples and women as part of its gender policy. The PNIEC is a guideline that complements it to work with a gender perspective and cultural and linguistic relevance between sectors and lev- els of government. MINAM has also been at the forefront in promoting the creation of a space with emphasis on indigenous women, valuing their knowledge and promoting the edu- cation of women and girls.

SERNAIP is part of MINAM.


[^1]: Supreme Decree 005-MIMP, April 2019. (PNIG: Perú NIEC Multisectorial Plan). Implementation of the PNIEC in the 62 services provided for by the 21 public institutions responsible. At the same time it also sets out the corresponding indicators and evaluation criteria to be used as a basis for monitoring progress. MINVAM is the lead agency for gender issues. The sectors must report to it on their progress in implementing the PNIEC.


---
**Page 183**
---

SERNANP, in 2018, launched the Working Group for Gender Equality through Presidential Resolution No. 204-2018-SERNANP. SERNANP has recently modified its regulations to pro- mote and ensure gender equality within the organization.

The Working Group is composed of representatives from the National Committees of each of the 70 Natural Protected Areas (RNPs), which are located in 22 depart- ments of the country. It has also designed the tool "¿How are we doing?" to analyze the pro- cesses, policies and practices of the RNPs with regard to gender equality.

The Working Group is part of the National Commission for Gender Equality (CNEG). These institutions have organised two contests: “Entrepreneurs by nature and Entrepreneurs by Call” in 2018 and 2019. The first contest seeks to promote women’s entrepreneurship, partic- ularly with regard to the sustainable use of natural resources associated with RNPs. The contests also seek to empower the role of women, promoting that 20% of the winning proposals must be led by women.

Moreover, SERNANP's Annual Welfare and Social Development Plan 2022 includes the need to achieve gender equality in the organization as well as in the management of RNPs. This aims at increas- ing women’s participation in national protected areas. The organisation has a general special- ist responsible for reporting on the SERNANP indicators of this PNIP but has not been involved in the elaboration or implementation of the gender equality plan.

The training on gender, audio-visual pieces on violence, reporting and harassment and knowledge on reproductive health are part of the training activities. The inclusion of women in traditionally "male" activities are recognised, e.g., trail building, and work as park rangers. How- ever, some aspects remain to be addressed: there is a lack of specific gender training for staff; spe- cific knowledge of gender equality and that there is a need to strengthen institutional knowledge on gender equality in the organisation.

MIDAGRI has a Commission for Gender Equality (Ministerial Resolution No. 0347-2017- MINAGRIC). In 2021, it launched the Directorate for the Promotion of Farming Women to artic- ulate, propose and promote technical and regulatory instruments and mechanisms to increase the participation of women in agricultural production.

In 2022: MIDAGRI approved the Strategy "Entrepreneurship of Rural and Indigenous Women" to finance projects that seek to improve the economic conditions of rural and indigenous women with technical advice in agriculture, forestry, husbandry, and handcraft. MIDAGRI intervenes in the study and implementation of gender equality policies and strategies for the inclusion of women and men, considering gender roles, the use of time, the reconciliation of family, personal, and work life, and aspects of the organisational climate that allow for egalitarian relationships.

MIDAGRI has shown through different projects its willingness to mainstream gender and/or equal opportunities between women and men. However, there is still a need to strengthen awareness-raising on gender equality in MIDAGRI. The gender approach is not yet institutionalised; an approach can be applied in programmes and projects throughout the entire cycle. During field- work, it is necessary to consider the participation of women and men in all activities. In the agro- genicuous within MIDAGRI and the Agropur and Agrobas, for example. Some representa- tives indicated that they do not have a gender perspective on their work; others even considered no need for a gender perspective in the work, as there is "no discrimination against women". Not all Agropur or Agrobasis have gender specialists.

For Agropur, it is still necessary to consider the gender approach as an integral part of the work (not a separation between 'technical' issues and the invisibilization of the 'social') during

# Other Partner Organizations

## PROFONANPE

PROFONANPE is responsible for mobilizing, managing and channeling resources for the implementation of programmes and projects linked to the country's environmental sustainability. It coordinates with other public institutions as well as private entities, such as universities and private institutions, in case of its participation in the elaboration of the Concept Note and the formulation of proposals. PROFONANPE ensures compliance with the criteria established by the National Plan for Gender Equality and the inclusion of gender considerations (including policies to prevent harassment, violence and exploitation of the social environment) in a series of proposed strategies and tools completed in an Environmental Social Management System (ESMS). The ESMS complies with international standards to which the entity is accredited, such as the Green Climate Fund (GCF) and the Adaptation Fund (AF). The ESMS comprises two main elements: the Social and Environmental

# Conclusion 3 for the project

## Public institutions

Although there is a formal institutional framework for gender mainstreaming in public institutions, the mechanisms to operationalise them are fragile. This is characterised by a high risk of failure and the existence of a typical "gender gap" between the formulation of policies and their fulfilment of their functions. Despite these challenges, MIDAGRI and SERINAP has shown that it is possible to overcome this gender gap in public institutions if the institutional framework is strengthened and viable. In the case of MIDAGRI, some efforts have been made to pro- mote gender mainstreaming as a priority opportunity to better promote the incorporation of the gender action into MIDAGRI actions.

## Other partner organizations

PRODOR has the instruments and capacities to mainstream gender in its activities. In general, the Resilient Puna project has the potential to contribute to gender mainstreaming both in management and planning instruments and through greater participation of women in training, dialogue, and decision-making processes.

# Chapter 5: Gender responsiveness and expertise in the project/ among project staff in the country

## 5.1 Equal opportunities in the team

The team members have not yet been appointed, but all of them will be trained in gender equity and intercultural competence. In addition to this, it is important to mention that the project will also count with a focal point for safeguards and gender. It is recommended that this person should possess knowledge on natural resources management (NRM) and gender, as well as gender expertise, with clear knowledge of how to integrate gender in projects implementation from a cross-cutting perspective.

In addition, the project will be led by GIZ South Cone (Cone Sur), to which the offices of GIZ in Peru are linked. The Cone Sur has an Intercultural and Gender Group (GIG). This group follows the guidelines established by the Strategy as well as the BMZ Country Action Plan (2019-2023). In addition, the GIG South Cone Sur has a Policy and Protocol against sexual harassment and gender discrimination in the workplace in accordance with the National Law of Gender Equality and Non-Discrimination and Equal Treatment (Supreme Decree No. 014/2019-MIMP). In 2020, staff received a specialised training about institutional policies on gender equality and non-discrimination, such as the Sexual Harassment Intervention Committee (HSI). Twenty-five people participated, 18 women and seven from various GIZ programs and projects in Peru. Also, in 2020, a specialised training of six sessions was held for staff at the office in Peru. The participation of 25 employees in average, in each one of these sessions. An analysis of the staff shows that there is no significant difference between the number of women and men in remuneration between male and female staff (GIZ Peru 2019). Currently, there are five colleagues who have been appointed to the management team, three of whom are project managers.

## 5.2 Gender expertise of the team members

All GIZ South Cone staff in Peru receive a training in gender topics during their induction process. Therefore, new staff will be aware of the existence of an internal policy of zero tolerance to discrimination and sexual harassment. The GIG South Cone Sur is also supported by the Gender and Interculturality working group (GIG), which allows for the exchange of experiences among colleagues from different projects and sectors, as well as collegial advice related to gender-responsive project design.

### Conclusion 4 for the project

It is recommended that the team, once constituted, should have the necessary support to strengthen its knowledge and skills in gender mainstreaming, in order to ensure that project implementation is carried out with a gender perspective. It will be important to consider gender mainstreaming with intersectionality must be considered in all the activities to be carried out within the framework of the project.

# 1. Introduction

The "Resilient Puna" ecosystem-based approaches for sustainable High Andean communities and ecosystems project (hereafter “Resilient Puna” project) aims to increase the resilience of Andean communities in the departments of Arequipa, Cusco, Puno, Lima (Yauyos) and Apurímac through management, conservation and restoration of high Andean ecosystems; to also promote gender equality and women’s empowerment as a key element for climate change adaptation towards Ecosystem-based Adaptation (EbA). It is organised into three components: (i) Puna ecosystems are restored, conserved and better managed to support climate resilient livelihoods, through the implementation of EbA; (ii) Climate-resilient livelihoods in place and accessible for vulnerable communities in the Puna Ecosystems are strengthened by providing access to markets and services; and (iii) Gender equality governance instruments. The project will invest in EDA measures and Climate Resilient Value Chains, providing technical support to communities, producer associations and cooperatives to achieve sustainable livelihoods directly benefiting the conservation and restoration of 23,914 hectares of high Andean ecosystems.

According to the GCF gender action plan (GAP), one of the aims of combining climate change to promote mechanisms for gender equality is capacity for efficient climate change adaptation measures in developing countries. This also includes a focus on women living in vulnerable communities. Specifically, the GCF proposes the need to develop a Gender Action Plan (GAP) that details the actions needed to mainstream gender equality issues into all aspects of project design and implementation and evaluation of the project, respecting and valuing the contribution of women and men. Climate change and adaptation requires the intersection of approaches that allow for the sustainable development of communities in the face of climate change. Therefore, it is important that women and girls who are often “left behind” in the attention of the main economic, climate and development agendas, key elements across this GAP are:

- (a) Supporting innovative solutions to combat climate change through a comprehensive gender perspective
- (b) Doing targeted investments that:
  - (i) Promote gender equality through climate change mitigation and adaptation measures
    ✓ Minimise social, gender and climate-related risks in all climate action change.
  - (ii) Contribute to reducing the gender gap in situations of social, economic and environmental vulnerability and poverty, by ensuring that women’s voices are heard and that mainstream gender equality issues.

The central objective of the GAP is to propose measures to break down barriers and reduce asymmetrical situations in the care, exploitation and use of natural resources, as well as the rights of people, especially women. The following table shows the main components of the Gender Action Plan conducted for the project. The design of gender measures and indicators are oriented towards developing and implementing a gender-responsive* approach to increase women's well-being

| Component | Description |
| --- | --- |
| 1. | Strengthening the capacity of women in the Puna Ecosystems |
| 2. | Promoting access to markets for women in the Puna Ecosystems |
| 3. | Improving the quality and quantity of information on gender equality issues |

*Please note that the GIZ working definition for gender response follows the OECD Gender Equality Continuum (2020). A gender-responsive approach only considers gender differences, inequalities, values and rights, e.g., by targeting and including a certain gender group in a project or programme. A gender-sensitive approach takes into account all aspects of gender equality, e.g., by considering the needs of women and men when designing strategies, policies, programmes and projects. A gender transformative approach goes beyond these considerations to challenge and change power relations from certain resources regardless of their gender (e.g., trainings or strategies are developed that consider gendered needs, preferences, and constraints).


---
**Page 193**
---

by taking into account gender-specific needs, preferences and overcome gaps to ensure that women’s rights are advanced and that they will benefit from project activities.

In Peru, although progress has been made, especially in the formal regulatory sphere, gender inequality persists, which can be clearly seen in the gender gaps and barriers to the full enjoyment of the human rights of women. These gaps are aggravated in the case of Peruvian women who face multiple factors of discrimination such as ethnicity, age, disability, religion, sexual orientation, gender identity, place of residence, etc.). The persistence of gender gaps at all levels and in all sectors is due to the fact that women’s voices have not been heard or taken into account on one behind. In the context post Covid pandemic, it is urgent to work on gender gaps that have been reinforced, partly because of women's increasing burden of domestic and unpaid care work.

Women, due to their socially constructed roles and responsibilities, and the persistence of conceptualizations of gender equality in Peru, are in an inferior position compared to men in access to and control of natural resources and decisions about land, water, production and sale of agricultural products, livestock, fishing, etc.). In addition, women’s contributions to the productive sphere (agriculture) and their contributions are not taken into account. Equally, the fulfillment of the roles in the reproductive sphere (domestic chores and care family) as almost exclusive responsibilities, overloads their daily workload, limiting their participation in decision-making spaces, access to resources, education, training, employment, and participation in decision-making spaces, which translates into under-representation in community organizations. In a wider organizational context, this discrimination is rooted in norms and an institutional culture that reinforces women’s inferior status, as well as in the unequal assignment of hierarchical roles. In this context, violence against women is also present, which is used by men to maintain their power over women and as a means of disciplining them to prevent their access to rights, as occurs in cases where they try to access decision-making positions. This not only prevents them from participating in social, political, economic and productive life but also reinforces the gender gap that makes it difficult and hard to transform these spaces that reproduce patriarchal structures of domination.

This project “Resilient Puma” will address existing gender gaps, especially those related to vulnerability and access to resources. Women are often in charge of collecting water for cooking and cleaning, using the land for livestock grazing, fetching firewood, among others. Women use and interact with natural resources and ecosystems on a daily basis. They are also responsible for managing family income and providing food security by accessing resources to look after their families. Gender inequality and social exclusion only increase the negative effects of unsustainable and destructive environmental management practices.

Considering these gaps, this project will work towards gender equality and against women’s and girls’ invisibility in decision-making spaces and access to resources and land. This Gender Action Plan (GAP) proposes interventions in all phases of the Resilient Puma Project cycle. The project will also conduct a monitoring strategy to evaluate and verify that no negative results are produced by the project activities (do no harm).

Gender impact and related risks

Considering that in the Andean region women play an important role in small-scale agriculture and water harvest but are still underrepresented in local level decision-making, Women are represented to a very high extent in the gender gap at depth. This is due to their limited access to productive resources and participation in the development of value chains and income-generating measures and their involvement in planning and decision-making processes and therefore enhance living conditions and economic opportunities of Andean women.


5


---
**Page 194**
---

Project - Gender Action Plan → p.04

Unintentional negative impacts could be that active participation of women might not be welcome by their partners/husbands/sons and which lead to conflicts within families and/or communities and in some cases lead to domestic violence. Another risk might be that decision making bodies in traditional communities and/or governmental entities allow women's participation but don't respect women's opinion on the matter, which could lead to frustration and early resignation of women. Similarly, women lead businesses could not thrive due to resistance by men lead business partners.

Methodology

The methodology of this Gender Action Plan involved a desk review and analysis of available secondary literature. The desk review was based on a comprehensive review of secondary literature that includes various research reports, articles and papers on the subject from both national and international organizations. It is also based on fieldwork in Afghanistan, Pakistan, Nepal, India, Bangladesh, Sri Lanka, Myanmar, Cambodia, Laos, Vietnam, and Indonesia between 2019-2022. Other gender analyses carried out for projects implemented in Peru and Ecuador were also utilised in carrying out this study.

6

2. Priority areas for Gender Mainstreaming

### 2.1 Gender dimensions in country/ region/ sector

#### Gender institutional framework

Peru is a state that prohibits discrimination and has committed to close gender gaps by signing international conventions and treaties and launching different mechanisms nationally for mainstreaming the gender approach in public policy. At the international level, Peru is part of the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW), 2010) and the Inter-American Convention on the Prevention, Punishment and Eradication of Violence Against Women (Convention of Belém do Pará); the Beijing Declaration and World Platform (1995); and the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development. Peru is also member of the Commission on the Status of Women (CSW) in terms of climate change. Peru is also a member of the Commission on the Status of Women (CSW) in terms of climate change. Peru is also a member of the Commission on the Status of Women (CSW) in terms of climate change. Peru is also a member of the Commission on the Status of Women (CSW) in terms of climate change.

At the national level, in 2015, Peru launched a Gender and Climate Change Action Plan (PAGCC) to reduce gender gaps in the fight against climate change. Moreover, the National Gender Equality Policy (PNGE) launched in 2019, identifies structural discrimination against women as a public problem. The PNGE aims at promoting equality between men and women by ensuring access to resources, capacity building and the elimination of institutional and socio-cultural barriers, information management and monitoring of information on investment and public spending with a gender perspective. In addition, in 2019, the National Institute for Statistics and Informatics (INEI) launched in 2019, the MINAM installed the National Committee of Women and Climate Change (CONAMUCC) as an open space for the participation of women in Peru’s climate change policy management. CONAMUCC hosts more than 35 national and international NGOs at the national level to work on gender and climate change issues.

Despite these institutional efforts, there is structural discrimination against women which limits the state capacity to implement and enforce these policies. As a consequence, women lack opportunities to fulfill their autonomy (physical, economic and decision-making), including gender gaps in access to resources such as education, reproductive health, empowerment, labor market participation, political participation, use of time, etc. This situation occurs especially in rural settings including the south high Andes of Peru (SHAP area).

#### Gender barriers in the rural Andes and opportunities for gender mainstreaming

According to the conclusions drawn from the Gender Analysis conducted for the project (see Annex 8a) following are some of the main findings:

**Barrier 1: Economic and financial barriers based on conclusion 2**

Women and men in the Andes have unequal access to loans. In 2012 only between 5% and 20% of men farmers took out a loan; and for women farmers it is even lower, between 4% and 12%; (INEI, 2013). According to the National Agricultural Census (Censo Nacional Agropecuario) and the Survey related to the National Agricultural Census (CENSAG), there are more women with no income of their own. For example in Arequipa only 45% of women have an income of their own, while for men this figure is 70%. In the case of Arequipa women without income of their own (38.1%) while men (22.3%). In Cusco


---
**Page 196**
---

Project - Gender Action Plan – p.04

women (30.9%) and men (18.7%). Finally in Peru, women 25.1% while men 17.4%. In the case of women in agricultural occupations, 37.6% of unpaid family workers and only 46.3% of women in agricultural occupations received cash income.

Although women’s participation in farming production has increased in the Andes, this growth has not necessarily translated into better economic conditions for women’s long-term well-being. Women are looking for better jobs, men have to take care of the household and the farm, with limited access to resources and prospects for better income due to lack of access, time constraints, low education levels and capital limitations. Rural women in the Andes region often do not have access primary food or sell their small surpluses in local markets, while men’s participation in productive activities is more closely linked to the family farm. Women are also less likely than men to be involved in decision-making about the project's region is related to the limited access and control over natural resources due to socially constructed gender roles and responsibilities. On average, women manage between 30%-54% less farming land than men in the SHAP area.

Barrier 2: Technical barriers based on conclusion 2

Women have limited opportunities for education, training, paid employment, and participation in decision-making processes at all levels of government. Despite this, women actively participate in managing key natural resources such as water and land. The availability of safe water in rural households is a major concern for women who are responsible for collecting it to meet household needs and animal consumption, and while irrigation tasks are considered masculine, families can easily access the necessary tools and equipment. Women also have limited access to credit and loans. In Peru, guinea pigs is the most important economic activity for women, while men tend to control the income from livestock and by products and show little or no participation in the commercialization of textile products.

Rural women also face other barriers (lower levels of education (indicated by a 23% literacy rate compared with 40.5% among men), limited access to language training, lack of knowledge about Quechua as their mother tongue), low wages and remuneration and leadership and the burden of domestic and productive work, among others) that prevent them from having equal access to communal government resources and services. Women are also less likely than men to be involved in decision-making inside the communities, but as well as in decision-making spaces local, regional, and national levels.

Finally, the incorporation of the gender approach in the territorial planning processes and investment projects (including measures acknowledging women’s knowledge, needs, responsibilities and domestic burden) demonstrates a positive trend. However, it is necessary to include competent women at all levels and formal fora of the Peruvian State that allow for the inclusion of women's visions from the formulation, implementation and evaluation of projects.

Barrier 3: Knowledge and information barriers based on conclusion 2

Women are less likely than men to have access to credit or loans. Women also receive less training, technical assistance, and entrepreneurship support, which limits them on the one the hand to have an informed decision in case they participate in decision-making plans and on the other their projects are not as well structured as those of men. In addition, women do not always have appropriate training to improve access to markets and financial services. There are also low loan rates among women, which is a barrier for women's participation in agricultural cooperatives or associations, committees, or cooperatives in the same rate in Arequipa, Apurímac, Cusco but show poor results. In addition, lack of information about credit and loans, as well as other services often prevent women from achieving the same level of visibility as men, or benefit from the same opportunities and recognition, despite their deep knowledge of agriculture, water and ecosystems.

* *


---
**Page 197**
---

Barrier 4: Institutional barriers based on conclusion 1 and 3

In general terms, in Peru there is a fairly advanced regulatory framework at different levels of government requiring the incorporation of a gender perspective in public policies; however, there is still no adequate guarantee for the full realization of the right to equality. There is a scarce institutional framework for gender mainstreaming in public institutions, but the mechanisms to operationalize them are fragile. This is characterized by an insufficient budgetary and a type of management that usually lacks resources and capacities for the fulfilment of its functions.

Opportunities for gender mainstreaming

Based on the identified barriers in the Gender Analysis (as described above) the Resilient Puna with the proposed financial mechanisms (please see chapter 3) proposed has the potential to contribute to women empowerment and gender mainstreaming both in financial instruments and in territorial planning. This will allow the communities to have a greater capacity for adaptation, resilience and adaptive capacity to climate change. This only could be achieved through greater access to financial resources and participation of women in training, dialogue, and decision-making processes. For this project, the inclusion of women as beneficiaries and as providers of services and goods, and as service providers is an opportunity to contribute to closing the gaps and tackle the barriers through gender mainstreaming while the need to carry out actions that, in the long term, collaborate in achieving a social and economic transformation towards greater equality in highly masculinized sectors and in a post-pandemic context.


---
**Page 198**
---

Project –Gender Action Plan → v.04

## 2.2. Gender expertise, gender responsiveness, and equal opportunities in the partner organizations

Although there is a formal institutional framework for gender mainstreaming in public institutions, the mechanisms to operationalise them are fragile. This is characterised by a high rotation of human capital and a type of management that usually lacks resources and capacities for the fulfilment of its functions. In this context, it is important to highlight that the Resilient Puna project has not adopted a gender approach in their actions that should be strengthened and visualized. In the case of MADGRI, some efforts have been made to promote gender equity. This is indeed an opportunity to better promote gender equality within the project team.

## 2.3. Gender expertise and responsiveness in the project team

All team members will bring a good level of gender equality and interculturality approaches. It is important to mention that some project members already have intermediate knowledge on gender and interculturality approaches based on previous academic and professional experience. The project will also count with a focal point for safeguards and gender. GIZ South Cone staff in Peru has been trained in the Gender Equality Approach (GEA) and Gender Mainstreaming. Members of FONAG are gender experts, which allows for the exchange of experiences among colleagues from different projects and sectors, as well as collegial advice related to gender-responsive project design.

Guiding principle of this Gender Action Plan is as follows: Considering that in the Andean region women play an important role in small-scale agriculture and water harvest but are still underrepresented in local level decision making. Women can be exposed to a very high vulnerability and a high degree of domestic violence.

The gender action plan aims at increasing women’s participation in value chains and income-generating measures and their involvement in decision-making and territorial planning processes and therefore enhance living conditions and economic opportunities of Andean women.
